I agree with Aali 


[237 


£o.4"9 


2 


put a stop to that hostility which had previously existed between various of our 
Agents. It 1s exceedingly desirable, however, that any policy thus entered upon 
should be strictly and impartially onforeed, and, if 1 am to believe the reports | 
veceive, 1 do not believe that this at the present time is the eas? in Syria, where 
\Ir. Moore has the most positive orders from me not to interfere in affairs of the 
internal administration of the country without reference to Constantinople, and 
special instructions to that effect; whereas the Count of Bentivoglio takes up 
the person who was lately Christian Kaimakam of Mount Lebanon, but whose 
authority has been lately suspended, and insists on his reinstallation in office 
as a matter in which the dignity and honour of his. country are concerned. If 
any general rule is to be established between the two Governments, a question of 
this kind should not be taken up by the Consul of either in this manner. It 
should either be left calmly to the independent decision of the Porte itself, or a 
common opinion should be arrived at by the respective Consuls-General at 
Beyrout, or the respective Ambassadors here. 1 mention this case especially, 
because it explains the whole system which T think has hitherto been so fatally 
pursued, A particular Consul takes up a particular local authority ; he identifies 
his Government with this ‘ndividual, whose cause he invariably advocates with 
more zeal than if deserves. Either other Governments must submit to this, in 
which ease they establish an influence paramount over the Porte, as well as over 
themselves; or they must resist it, in which case there arises an infinitude of 
never-ending dispules on matters which, in nine cases out of ten, might be traced 
to causes wholly insignificant. 
What I find at present is this: ‘fa Pasha isa weak man, he either crosses 
his arms on his breast and does nothing, or he surrenders himself to the 


patronage of some Consul with a strong will and authoritative manners, In the 
of him; in the second, one 


first case his own Government naturally complains 
the others run him down as 


Government usually cries him up as a pheenix, and 
a monster. On the other hand, if a Pasha of strong mind and independent 


character is sent anywhere—a man who goes his own way without attending to 
this Consul or that—all the Consuls usually combine agamst him, and hold. his 
assumption of independence as an act against their Consular authority. 3 
I find also that each Consul is too apt to wish and expect that the Pasha 
should obey him and him alone ; and, moreover, that each not only wishes to 
govern, but to appear to govern, and to have it said that everything is done by 
his influence, which, if he is an Englishman, he calls English influence, if a 
Frenchman, French ; but which, whether French or English, is an influence that 
exeites the jealousy of his colleagues, and damages the position of the Pasha who 
is charged with the government of the district in which he resides. 
| do not know whether your Lordship will approve of this, but I tell our 
Consuls whenever they can exercise a beneficial influence, to do so, but not to 
he ostentatious of it, and rather to give the credit of whatever good they are 
able to perform to the Turkish authorities than boast of it themselves. 
| have, &e. 


(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

ment could cope with ; but, on the other hand, in those cases of a mixed or 
general nature, where more than one sect is concerned, and where the interests 
of the general community are at stake, there mixed tribunals must have juris- 
diction and the general authority prevail. It will be jepoee le, moreover, to 
attempt to carry any plan into effect with the assent of the ruling race, which 
should have for its evident object the placing of any other race over it. It is 


not a policy of pumice alone which will prevent this. The Turk thinks he is 
superior to the Arab, as he thinks he is superior to the Armenian or Greek ; 
and the habit of command has made him in many respects superior to both. 

Between the Greek and himself, moreover, he feels that when the question 

is one of power, the struggle is one of life and death. The Hellene, in fact, 
would no more accept equality than the Turk. He has the same pride of 
lineage and the same ambition for supreme place. The best Government will 
not content him, if he is not the master in it ; the Ionians reject the British rule, 
and it is therefore easy to understand that the same people will not be satisfied 
with the Turkish rule, even after it has undergone a vast amelioration. 

I say all this, not to check our efforts for improving and advancing the 
Christian populations in this Empire, far from it, but in order to steady and 
metbodize our views as to the degree in which, with the assent of the Turks, 
reforms having for their object the amelioration of the position of the Rayahs 
may be promptly carried out. 

Independently, however, of this peculiar subject, the whole conduct of the 
Government here, and of its administration, has, as 1 have said, to be improved ; 
and although in many instances it seems to me in the way of being so, its 
progress, I fear, will be slower than the times in which we live, and the dangers 
which surround it will allow, unless some extraordinary exertions are made, 
some extraordinary measures are adopted. . 

Turkey has, in fact, to be Europeanized to sone with its enemies or rivals 
in Europe; and I own I do not see any way in which it can be so with sufficient 
rapidity to be in time to meet coming events, unless by the agency of Furopeans. 
If the Turks would freely employ Europeans in the different departments where 
their knowledge, energy, and habits of business are required, they might within 
a few years make a great advance, and educate a race that would hereafter 
dispense with European assistance. ‘There seems, however, to this plan an 
insuperable repugnance, which is no doubt fostered by the peculiar position 
which foreigners hold in this country. The Turkish Government cannot appro- 
priate such foreigners to itself, because they cannot be under its laws and 

jurisdiction. Here, indeed, is one of the great difficulties whieh, in consi- 
gens the prospects and maintenance of the Turkish Empire, one has to deal 
with, 

Clear evidence on the part of Turkey of a firm desire to establish a Govern- 
ment that Europe would approve of, and a firm determination on the part of 
Europe to maintain this empire on such conditions, might suggest a reconsi- 
deration of our Capitulations, with a view to their gradual modification; but | 

fear, in the present state of things, it is useless to do more than glance at this 
idea; and I thus conclude the sketch with which I have troubled your Lordship, 
without however, | fear, giving any very clear picture of what is in itself so 
confused, and without arriving at any very definite result, 

What | have said, however, may be thus summed up:— 


Great changes for the better have of late years taken place in this 
country. 


‘These changes are as rapid and general as could, under the circumstances, 
and within the time which has elapsed since their commencement, have taken 
place. But they leave an immense space of reform yet to be traversed. 


The position of the Christian population is considerably improved, and may 
be yet, by a few simple and not very difficult measures, much advanced, But 
[ fear any extensive change must be gradual in its progress, and as it bas always 
to be remembered that the Mu.sulman class is, generally speaking, the upper 
and proprietor ¢lass, so we are not to consider all the oppression which takes 
place in the provinces as oppression against the Christians, but often as oppres- 
ion against the lower cliss of the population, both Mussulman and Christian, 
Good Local Governors, even with the existing laws, would go far to remove this ; 
but the requisites for such Governors are not easily found amongst the individuals 
from whom a Grand Vizier has to make his selection. The way, however, to

On the other hand, as to many branches of administration, namely, those 
which concern Finance, the Army, Navy, ‘Telegraphs, Public Works, &., these 
can only, in my opinion, be placed on an European basis with sufficient rapidity 
to meet the erisis with which Turkey *s threatened, by the employment of 
competent Europeans; and the great thing is to persuade the ‘Turks of this 
necessity, and to facilitate, as far as we can, the removal of obstacles that exist, 
or any prejudices they entertain against it.

No. 3. 
Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russeil.—(Received August 4.) 


(Private and Confidential.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, July 26, 1859. 

MR. HORNBY has shown me the sketch ofan able Report which he has 
drawn up for the ‘nformation of Her Majesty's Government, on the late judicial 
reforms in Turkey. 

I agree with all Mr. Hornby’s views as to the principles on which such 
reforms should be carried out; but he rather underrates, In my opinion, the 
difficulties which this Government has to contend with, and does not take 
sufficiently into consideration the causes, not of a judictal character, which stand 
in the way of immediate and adequate judicial improvements. 

The objections found by Mr. Hornby against the new reforms are :—- 

|. ‘That they were got up hastily to meet the urgent pressure Her Majesty's 
Government. made on the Ottoman Government, and do not represent, and are 
not adapted to, these countries, but are simply taken from the institutions of a 
country foreign to and dissimilar from this. 

9 That a new Court of Second Instance, lately erected, is merely a Court 
of Inquiry without the power of decision. 

8, That all Courts which do not consist of one, but of many Judges, are 
relieved from all responsibility ; and that, notwithstanding the principle of many 
Judges, and not of one, is that which the Porte now constantly adopts in the 
formation of her Courts, and especially in that of the Court. just alluded to. 

With respect to the first objection, the Turkish Government says in its 
defence, that the reforms in question were not got up hastily to meet present 
difficulties, but were duly and carefully weighed before being adopted, and that 
the new scheme is imitated from the French Code; because had the Turkish 
Government set about framing a new Code of its own, it must have employed 
in its construction its own men of Taw, bound to consult the precepts of the 
Koran; and that thus things would have been left in much the same state as 
they were. By adopting a new and foreign system, it hints that it separated 
the divine from the human law, and rendered useless the authority and 
knowledge of the exponents of the former. 

Next and secondly, as to the limited powers of the new Court of Second 
Instance or Inquiry, ‘The Turks say they were afraid to give at once to a Mixed 
Court with Christian Judges the power of actually pronouncing sentence on the 
lives and property of Mussulmans, and that they, consequently, deemed it 
prudent to habituate the Turkish people to see Christians in the first instance 
on the judicial seat, investigating such cases and passing an opinion on them. 
This opinion, however, the Supreme Court, which, though Mussulman, is 
generally composed of inen of great ability, 1s bound to respect. 

With regard to Mr. Hornby’s observations, that where there are many 
Judges instead of one, the aforesaid Judges are relieved of all responsibility, the 
statement is assuredly correct; but the object the Government has here in view 
is rather a political than a judicial one. 

In fact, the introduction of the Christian clement into the exercise of 
judicial pewers can hardly be executed except by some method of the sort; for 


7 


if you were in this country to have only one Judge, that Judge could not, in 
the present state of opinion, be a Christian. But by introducing Christian 
Judges into the Tribunals, you accustom the public mind to the idea of 
Christians sitting in judgment on Mussulmans. t is true that this may be all 
hi Bain, but the gain, though small at first, is calculated to become eventually 
great. ; 

In fact, the whole of the proposed changes are worth little in themselves, 
and to the eye of a mere judicial reformer; but looked at by the statesman 
they may be regarded as a means for taking the popular mind, as it were, off its 
old hinges, and preparing the way for a better state of things. 

fam, however, of opinion that the formation of a new Tribunal attached to 
the Municipality of Pera might, if properly organized on the principles laid 
down by Mr. Hornby, be productive of great advantage. 

My endeavours, therefore, are for the moment, concentrated im the attempt 
to create some good Tribunal of this kind, which, if it could bring the argument 
of practicable and visible advantage before the public eye, would do more, I 
think, to advance the question of judicial reform than can ever be done by any 
arguments merely on paper, however conclusive. hac 


I have, &c. 


(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

No, 4, 

(No 68.) Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell —(Received August 4.) 
My Lord, 


= Constantinople, July 26, 1859. 
I HAVE troubled your Lordship upon this occasion with much that 
relates to reforms in Turkey. 

; There is one reform, however, to which I wish to give a separate place, 
which has long dwelt in my mind, and which, if practicable, will be of immense 
benefit not only to the native population of Turkey, but to the foreigners whio 


inhabit it: in fact I think it difficult to calculate its effects or to ascertain its 


benefits; neither do I conceive it altogether impracticable. 

I'he reform to which I allude, is the establishment of one general cole and 
mode of procedure for the subjects of all the foreign States who reside in this 
Empire. This will naturally lead to common tribunals, which might then be 
placed on a suitable footing adapted to the circumstances of the case. 

The Mixed Tribunals which now exist of necessity furnish some ground for 
bringing the subjects of different Sstates together in a court of justice, and 
though, according to the rule now adopted, the law varies in each particular case 
as the party may be prosecutor or defendant, it would surely be better for all 
parties to get rid of so absurd a rule, and to have a common law by which a 
man, whether as prosecutor or defendant, should have justice dealt out to him. 
Moreover, | have not the slightest doubt that if one code should be adopted for 
foreign States it would soon be a model to Turkey from which her new code 
would be formed: and that thus the way would be prepared better than it could 
be in any other for allowing to the Turkish Government those rights which 
every other Government exercises within its own dominions, the alienation of 
which renders its perfect independence impossible, There are difficulties 
doubtless, in the way of this plan, and it may perhaps appear theoretical that i 
should propose it; but its scope is so wide that I think it may not unwisely be 
considered before dismissing it 1s unattainable. | 


I have, &c. 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

No. 7. 


Lord J. Russell to Sir H. Bulwer, 

(No. 97.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, August 16, 1859. 

WITH reference to your despatch No. 81 of the Srd, reporting the conver- 
sation which took place between Fuad Pasha, M, Faleonnet, and your Excellency 
on the subject of the Turkish finances, I have to state to you that the promises 
made by Fuad Pasha on that occasion appear to be very satisfactory, but that 
unless the Sultan can be induced to limit his own expenses to a fixed sum, and 
to put a stop to the plan of incurring debts for his own personal disbursements, 
the progress of Turkey towards ruin is certain. 

I have further to instruet your Excellency to endeavour to induce your 
colleagues to join with you in making a joint representation to the Grand Vizier 
on this subject. 


Lam, &c. 
: (Signed) J. RUSSELL.

No. 8. 


Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell.—(Received August 28.) 
(No. 106.) 


My Lord, Therapia, August 16, 1859. 

I HAVE had ‘some conversation with M. Thouvenel since his return as 
to the policy of France, and 1 think 1 have also ascertained pretty aceurately the 
policy of Russia as to this country. ‘ 

The French, for various reasons, amongst which may be named a dislike to 
seem to adopt too suddenly an entire change of conduct in the East, are for 
civing the Turks a further trial, and awaiting the effects of such counsels with 
respect to reform and good government as we may be disposed to urge in 
conjunction with them, 

I think their mind, however, is pretty well made up as to the unavailing 
effect of these counsels, and, having no faith in the government of any other 
wortion of the population, they look forward in this case to the partition of the 
Empire amongst foreign Powers. 

The Russians, with the same views, are disposed to follow those views out 
somewhat diflerently. ‘They will be more abstinent in advice, more cautious 
and forbearing in manner, and re-assume, if possible, their old position of 
protectors to a falling Power, until such time as may seem most convenient to 
them for putting their foot on the neck of the people to whom they have been 
giving their hand. 

With respect to Austria, my opinion is that it will probably relapse into its 
former alliance with the Cabinet of St. Petersburgh, and follow out with it such 
changes in this part of the world as circumstances may produce. 

Prussia will likewise be found in the end by the side of Russia. 

__ The only real chance of upholding the Ottoman power for any length of 
time, therefore, depends on usand on the Turks themselves. As to reforms, if 
there is a question of great, general, and rapid reforms, such as would convert 
the whole of ‘Turkey in a very few years into a perfectly well-governed Empire 
on European principles, it is idle expecting this. ! see, for instance, it is asserted 
in some journals, that murders and robberies are still committed in ‘Turkey, 
notwithstanding its new laws and its reforms. Such criticism is absurd and 
unreasonable. There are not only robberies and murders, which, untortunately, 
are to be heard of in the most civilized communities, but disorder, corruption, 
weakness ; what was certain to exist, in short, when the old discipline and 
despotism were relaxed, until such period (and these changes will be the effect 
of years) as a new system of diseipline and government has been instituted and 
entered into the habits of the people. ‘To attempt to make this new discipline 
and government too complicated and too perfect will be an error; to expect it 
to produce more in a given time than it can do is a wilful delusion, 

The most we can hope from Turkey is that within the next few years she 


ry Laat hk = J qv | Pay iu Ga ALY i 9 (Seni ee 
itd ei Aste eee ate aE 


10 


will establish a regular system of finance, a tolerable army, and tribunals more 
adequate for the ends of justice—an operation, however, considering there are 
according to this new system, judges to form, lawyers to educate, perfectly new 
ideas as to equality before the law to create,which is not, and cannot be, either a 
very quick or a very easy process. 

)f one thing | am quite conyinced ; the more simple the reforms that are 
attempted, whether in law or in administration, the more chance there is of their 
success. Tor years to come, in fact, the Government will depend more on the 
men employed than on the laws they administer. Responsibility must be 
concentrated, punishment inflicted, and rewards given, peculation discoun- 
tenanced, adequate salaries afforded, a class of honest and capable officials formed ; 
and this, which seems easy on paper, is difficult in practice, especially in a 
country where a Minister knows that a concubine, # woman, a favourite, may at 
any moment disorganize his plans and effect his disgrace. 

1 cannot, then, with all the difficulties and contingencies which present 
themselves to my mind, and with the formidable and hostile combination likely 
at any moment to be formed, speak with such confidence as to the future 
prospects of this Government as would relieve that’of Her Majesty from anxiety 
on its account, I think, indeed, we must hold ourselves prepared for any 
catastrophe that may occur, any ‘course that events may sumeest, 

Still I do not, on the other hand, deem that the task of maintaining the 
Turkish Empire is to be despaired of, roviding too many things are not 
demanded from these people at once, and that their attention is not confused 
and distracted {rom the more immediate and essential subjects they have to deal 
with, of which their financial eondition is no doubt the first, 

With respect to the question of the Rayahs, 1 have already more than once 
treated it, 

The changes to be made here must be made gradually ; beeause, in the first 
place, the Rayahs themselves could not be made fit at once for vast or sudden 
ones; and secondly, because the Mussulman population, on whom, for some 
time at lenst, the Sultan can alone count 05 an army, would not, without disgust, 
bear violent and abrupt ones. But they must be made, visibly and really, by 
euch administrative measures from time to time as are best calculated to elevate 
the character of this class, and fit it for the employment of the State; and by 
such judicial measures as will procure for it, as rapidly as the state of society 
allows, a practical civil equality before the law. 

As to the course which the British Embassy should pursue on all these 
matters, it has been often thought over by me, ‘There are two sets of persons : 
the one say, “Bully the Turks ; you ean get them to do nothing but by 
bullying ;” the others suy, ‘ Leave the Turks alone, and they will find out what 
is best for them.” 

In regard to the first plan, if we wanted simply to obtain something for 
ourselves, and to crush this people in obtaining it, the advice would be sensible 
enovgh; but it is surely clear that if you make a people lose all self-respect, if 
you drive them as a set of slaves to any task, even if it be one calculated in 
itself to be useful to them, you destroy that which 18 the first essential in a State 
which you wish to make, or to keep, independent—its vitality, its patriotism, its 
individuality, ‘The first instant, moreover, at which the lash ceases to fall, the 
herd stops; nor can anything done wholly unwillingly be done well. 

On the other hand, it you leave the Turks entirely to themselves, the effect 
of climate, of character, of old customs, still, at least amongst the masses, would 
prevail over the notions of the few, who are more sensible of the necessity of 
changes, than disposed to constant and strenuous efforts to make them. 

My notion, then, is that the best language I can hold is one of friendly 
encouragement on the one side, painting, perhaps, insomewhat flattering colours 
what has been already done in ths way of reform, and friendly remonstrance on 
the other, demonstrative of the absolute and rigorous necessity of further 
changes. 

‘Above all, 1 think it most essential not to obtain (for the sake of a momen- 
tary triumph, simply in appearance) promises of greater things than can really 
be performed, a course which leads to endless dissents; faction making the one 
party, in the end, wholly incredulous as to the value of any promises, and the 
other equally careless as to the performance of them, 

| have only to add, that when M, Thouvenel has communicated to me more 


Il 


fully his ideas—and I faney his intention is to bring forward some general 
programme, which may be called the French one—I shall be most willing and 
anxious to co-operate with him in all that seems practicable or advisable, without 
disputing or caring about the title of authorship; while, on the other hand, | 
shall state, with equal frankness, my objections to such portions of his scheme as 
appear to me unsuited cither to the country or the occasion. 
I have, &e. 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER, 


No. 9. 


Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell. —(Received September 25.) 
(No. 153.) 


My Lord, Therapid, September 14, 1859. 

WITH reference to your Lordship’s despatch on the expenses of the Sultan, 
I had some conversation with M. Thouvenel as to such a joint representation 
as that which your Lordship suggested. | 

He said he was perfectly ready to make any isolated representation such as 
I might myself make; but he could not make a formal joint representation 
without authority from home. 

Tam convinced that any attempt tosound my colleagues in a similar manner 
would be equally unsuccessful; in fact, many of them would make use of my 
request in order to poison the Sultan’s mind against me, and to destroy any 
influence over His Majesty | may possess; whilst the failure which | should 
ultimately experience would produce a directly opposite effect from that which 
your Lordship desires, It is, moreover, exceedingly questionable whether the 
Grand Vizier himself could venture to present any ‘ha representation as that 
in question to his Master. | 

I would, therefore, venture to ask your Lordship’s permissiou to be allowed 
to pursue that course relative to this subject which | think most likely to be 
snecessful, i 

_ Inthe meantime, however, M. Thouvenel and myself spoke the other night 
to the Grand Vizier very seriously, at my house, on the matter; and | told his 
Highness frankly the despatch I had received. 

j What strikes me, at the moment, as the best plan, would be to induce the 
Ministry to resign on any occasion where the Sultan's exigencies might justify the 
measure; and to let it be generally understood that any Government which 
accepted office with different views and principles would meet with anything 
but countenance from the Great Foreign Powers. In this manner something 
like a constitutional spirit might he engendered, and the patriotism and inde- 
pengence of men in office encouraged. A fitting oceasion, however, must be 
found. 

_In the meantime I had a long conversation with the Sultan, who, owing 
to the sympathy T had manifested in his illness, was especially communicative 
and gracious; and | took the opportunity of alluding to the financial state of 
the country, and the ruin that would inevitably befall the Kimpire unless it was 
remedied, It is no doubt absolutely necessary that some limit should be 
established to the indulgence of His Majesty’s reckless generosity and prodigal 
habits; but in reality, although at the present moment every furthing he Terman 
has to be obtained on such onerous conditions as to be ruinous to the State, if 
anything like general order and economy were introduced into the financial 
administration, even the expensive tastes Abdul Medjid indulges in would be 
but of small comparative importance. 7 | 

The Commission proceeds with its work ; but far too slowly. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

I TRANSMIT, for your Excellency’s information, a copy of a letter from 
the Treasury containing observations on your views for the improvement of the 
finances of Turkey, as set forth in your despateh No. 105 of the 16th ultimo; 
and in recommending these observations to your Eycellency's particular atten-

Sir A. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell.—(Recewed October 14.) 
(No. 193.) 
My Lord, Therapia, October 4, L859. 

LOOKING for a suitable opportunity to carry out the instructions 
conveyed in your Lordship’s despatch No. 97 of the 16th of August, relative to 
the extravagant expenditure of the Sultan, I agreed with the French Ambassador 
that the Representatives of the six Powers might ask an audience of the Sultan, 
and whilst congratulating him on the discovery of the recent conspiracy call his 
serious attention to the state of the country and the absolute necessity of order, 
economy, and vigour in the Government ; and atour instigation Messrs. Prokesch, 
Labanoif, Joannini, and Rehfues, all agreed to adopt this course. But the 
Grand Vizier and Fuad Pasha, when our intention was communicated to them, 
declared, somewhat to our surprise, that they should be obliged to tender their 
resignations as soon as the audience which we solicited had taken place, as they 
felt sure that the Sultan would not fail to attribute to their instigation the 
course pursued hy the Representatives. : 

A question then arose as to what steps we should take. 

For my own part | deemed it advisable, on the one hand, not to add to the 

difficulties of the situation by provoking a Ministerial crisis; and, on the other 
hand, equally advisable not to forego the opportunity which the union of the six 
Representatives offered for endeavouring, by a joint representation, to produce 
some effect on the march of affairs. I therefore suggested that we should 
address ourselves to the Grand Vizier, who is the official organ of the Sultan, 
instead of to His Majesty himself, and that we should do this in such a manner 
as would give to our representation a common character, although each might 
communicate it separately (which his Highness Aali Pasha seemed to desire) to 
the Porte. 
-_M. Thouvenel, who acted in this matter, as 1 must say I always find him act, 
in the most friendly manner, considered my idea a good one: and consequently 
at a meeting between his Excellency and myself, with Baron Prokesch and 
Prince Labanoff, the annexed memorandum was agreed to, a8 expressing our 
joint opinions; each undertaking to transmit it to the Grand Vizier, with a 
separate instruction to his interpreter. Mine is herewith inclosed; and it 
appears to have given Aali Pasha much satisfaction. 

Count Joannini and M. de Rehfues took the same course as the four 
Representatives above-named ; and the Memorandum was presented yesterday, 
It is, | understand, to be laid, together with the several instructions which 
accompanied it, before the Cabinet, and then submitted by all the Ministers 
collectively to His Imperial Majesty.

LES Représentants des Puissances qui, en garantissant le maintien et 
Vintéerité de !Empire Ottoman, ont temoigne de lintérét particulier qu’elles 
attachent & sa prospérité, se croient en devoir, en face des circonstances graves 
du moment, d’appeler toute la sollicitude de la Sublime Porte sur la situation 
politique et financiére du pays. L’Europe a marqué dans son sein une place 
importante & la Turquie; mais elle a bien compris qu'une caution extérieure ne 
serait pas suffisante pour réaliser ses veeux si la Puissance ainsi fortifiée au 
dehors ne s'aidait pas elle-méme, et si son organisation ne se vivifiait par lappli- 
cation graduelle et soutenue des réformes dont Sa Majeste le Sultan a librement 
et solennecllement pose les principes. Une tache si vaste, sans doute, ne saurait 
étre Peeuvre d'un jour, mais sa poursuite ne comporte pas de réliche; et dans Ja 
période de transition que traverse l'Empire entre les décombres d'un régime 
détruit et les matérianx non encore faconnés dun régime nouveau, une impulsion 
constante est indispensable pour stimuler les retardataires, calmer les impatients, 
et contraindre les uns et les autres a travailler au bien commun. 

Or, les Représentants ci-dessus désignés ont le regret de constater, sans 
prétendre en préciser la cause, que eette impulsion ne se manifeste pas de fugon 
x-atteindre le but anguel doit tendre elle-méme la Sublime Porte. Ce malaise 
qui régne dans T'esprit des diverses populations de I'Empire ne disparaitra que 
lorsque la masse de la nation entreverra clairement et dans un avenir prochain 
V'époque oti elle jouira de la sécurite résultant du mouvement normal d'une 
société satisfaite d'elle-méme, paisiblement occupée du développement de ses 
ressources, et dirigée par une administration attentive a ses hesoins matériels, 
ennemie des abus, économe surtout et intelligente dans l'emploi des déniers 

ublies. L’accomplissement de ces conditions générales de la prosperité des 
‘tats est independante des différences de religion et de race; la question & 
résoudre est celle de l'établissement d'un Gouvernement sous lequel tous les 
sujets de Sa Majeste le Sultan, Musulmans et Chrétiens, au lieu de souffrir de 
maux analogues partageront les mémes bienfaits.

Sir H. Bulwer to Mr. Pisani. 


Monsieur, Therapia, ce 3 Octobre, 1859. 


LES circonstances graves qui viennent de se passer ici, et qui existent 
encore autour de nous, ont inspiré Vidée 4 moi ainsi qua plusieurs de mes 
colligues de solliciter une audience du Sultan, afin de lui faire parvenir nos 
opinions communes. 

Il parait toutefois que le Grand Véeir regarde une démarche dans laquelle 
nous avons espéré qu'il puiserait une nouvelle foree, comme inopportune dans ce 
moment. 

Il est naturel qu'aprés une telle déclaration venant de si haut et d'un 
personnage non moins respectable par ses qualites personnelles qu’éminent par 
sa position publique et officielle, nous ne prenions pas sur nous la responsabilité 
de l’'acte que nous avons prémédité. . 

Mais, d’un autre edte, il nous a paru impossible d'accepter la responsabilité 
du silence auprés des organes accrédités de Sa Majesté sur les maux que nous 
prévoyons, si une main puissante, et affermie par la Providence Divine, ne dinge 
pas dans ce moment le vaisseau de I'Etat loin des écueils qui le menacent. 

Vous prendrez cette occasion d’insister surtout aupres du Grand Vézir et de 
Fuad Pacha sur l'état financier du pays; vous parlerez de la nécessité absolue de 
économie de la purt de tout le monde, tant de celle du Souverain que de ses 
Ministres. C'est en s'abstenant des dépenses que ni le Gouvernement ni le pays 
exigent qu’on puisse' trouver, sans trop peser sur le peuple; les ressources 
nécessaires pour les besoins réels de l'Empire, Je dois ajouter que Véconomie 
scule ne suflira pas; il faut de Vordre, un systéme régulier, un budget connu, 
C'est inutile d’espérer le progrés de la Turquie aussi longtemps que cette

No. 12, 
Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell.—(Received October 22.) 
(No. 211.) 
My Lord, Therapia, October 11, 1859. 


I HAVE deemed it better instead of making a monthly report merely 
Consular, to make a monthly report comprehending}the general politics and 
condition of the Empire. ' 

I need hardly say that the chief point of present interest here 1s, as might 
be expected, still the recent conspiracy. Mole 

Little further light, however, is thrown upon it. It seems clear that 
Hussein Pasha, a man of energy and consideration, was its chiel, and would 
have presided over its execution: his courage was tested at Kars, and few 

eople could have been more fitted to take the lead ina coup demain. But in 
judgment and ability he is deficient, and could never have controlled the 
movement he was prepared to originate. Having been forced to leave the 
capital by his appointment to Larissa, the conspirators wanted ® man of 
execution in his place; and they selected a certain Pasha who commands the 
fortresses at the mouth of the Black Sea. It is doubtful in what spirit this 
Pasha ut first received their overtures; but at all events when they had confided 
to him fully their designs and resources, he communicated the same to the 
Seraskier, The Government then took the mensures necessary to arrest the 
persons implicated, and destroy the plot at its roots. Tussein Pasha has arrived 
at Constantinople and been examined. All he denies, and that the principal 
conspirators deny, is the idea of assassination. They say that their wish was 
reform, and a change of Ministers and system through the Sultan, whose power, 
however, was to have been abridged in the new order of things. Phey deny 
also any idea of reaction in a legislative or administrative sense, although they 
avow that many practices which have been lately introduced, and which shock 
the Mussulman notions of propriety, such as the greater freedom given to 
women, would have been put a stop to, and the Mussulman feelings generally, as 
far as they were not incompatible with the introduction of European improvements, 
carefully consulted. The programme they now put forward 18 in fact a plausible 
one, but there is littke doubt if their attempt had a first success, it would have 
soon assumed a fanatical character, and caused frightful disorders throughout 
the Empire. If, however, there were no other parties concerned than those 
who appear on the scene at present, the insurrection meditated could not have 
triumphed. ‘The conspirators, indeed, seem themselves to have been sensible of 
this, but many say they had been led to believe that persons of the highest rank 
in the Empire were favourable to their plans. The Government affect, I think 
wisely, to believe that this was impossible, and without going deeper into the 
matter will condemn the persons now in confinement, under different categories ; 
the most culpable receiving the sentence of death, which will not, I believe, be 
“arricd into exeeution, 
=n “The memoir transmitted to the Grand Vizier by the Representatives of the 
Great Powers has, | understand, been presented to the Sultan. It will be some 
davs before I shall be able to say what exact effect it has produced, — | 
’ The important consideration which these events bring forward is the great 
unpopularity of the Sultan, which has made people throughout the Empire, | 


15 


understand, sympathise with the revolution rather than with the constituted 
authorities, . 

The various Consular reports speak of this feeling. 

With respect to the Empire generally, some of the districts bordering on 
Montenegro have returned to their allegiance. Drobniak, Piva, and some are 
still in arms, agreeing solely to the payment of a tribute, but refusing to receive 
Tarkish Mudirs (sub-Governors) or Turkish guards. A difference, moreover, 
has just, [ understand, arisen between the Russian and French Commissioners 
and their colleagues, but no details have reached me, 

Disputes as to attacks and counter-attacks on the part of the border 
inhabitants still continue. 

As to the Uskoks they are a lawless race, whose chief avocation is to 
pillage and murder; their own name is another for brigand, and it is probable 
that if any attack has been made upon them, its object will prove to have been 
in relation to this their peculiar character, 

In Servia, the intrigues and efforts of Milosch to increase his power by 
means of the Skouptschina, or National Assembly, appear to have been checked 
by remonstrances addressed to him from St. Petersburgh, in consequence of 
which he sent a message to the Pasha at Belgrade, assuring his Excellency he, 
himself, had no disposition to alter his relations with the Porte, but that if the 
nation should insist upon changes, then he would submit its desires to the 
Sultan and the guaranteeing Powers. 

The bearers of the firman recognizing Prince Couza were daily expected 
at Jassy, where the firman for Moldavia will be received first, and at Bucharest, 
where the firman for Wallachia will be read with the same ceremony. 

The camp of Ploresti is broken up, and there is reason to hope that now 
that the Prince feels more secure in his position he will infuse more justice and 
moderation into his administration, 

L understand his Highness’s journey to Constantinople is deferred till the 
spring. 

At Salonica, the arrival of Ismail Pasha, the new Governor, has raised hopes 
of a better state of things in that Pashalic, where the want of energy on the 
part of his predecessor had defeated his good intentions. 

The prompt and energetic measures timely adopted by the Purte to put 
down the disturbances in Candia, aided by the conciliatory dispositions and 
ability of Cabouli Eifendi, the Porte’s Commissioner, have tended to keep the 
Grecks there quiet, aud the Ottoman Government intends, on Cabouli’s return, 
to consider what measures, if any, can be adopted for the general satisfaction 
and good government of the Island. 

Mehemed Pasha with his troops still remains at Prosnevo, endeavouring to 
eollect the taxes so long due, 

In Syria the measures adopted by the local authorities to prevent the 
quarrel which had arisen between the Drwuses and the Christians at Beit-Miri, 
and in which several lives were sacrificed, from assuming greater dimensions, 
appear to have succeeded, and tranquillity seems restored. 

The various statements of the proceedings of the Arab tribes in the neigh- 
bourhood of Mardin are very confused. But it is certain that they are plunder- 
ing wherever they can venture to do so. 

The reeall of Omer Pasha from the government of Bagdad is owing to his 
attempting too much and doing too little; but his successor is a person not go 
fitted for his post as he ought to be, 

He is, however, said to have been always very friendly in his disposition 
towards Great Britain, and will receive strict injunetions to continue so. 

The complaints brought repeatedly against the Pasha of Erzcroum both by 
Mr. Hoghes and Mr, Dalyell induced me to urge the Porte to replace him by 
a fitter person, and I have the satisfaction to state that Edhem Pasha, late 
Governor of Euippopoll has been selected by the Sultan for the government. of 
a province in which the Protestant community has had so much to complain of 


vexatious proceedings, and the good government of which is generally so 
important to the Empire, 

On the Zod instant Mouktar Bey, the official Agent of the Viceroy of Egypt 
at the Porte, lett Constantinople in charge of instructions to the Viceroy, to put 
an effectual stop to the works of the Suez Canal. A copy of those instructions 
is inclosed in my despatch No, 168 of the 20th ultimo.

untimely act of authority, I suggested to M. Thouvenel the idea of conveying to 
Kibrisli Pasha, confidentially, but in a manner he could make use of with the 
Sultan, our opinions as to the advisability of sparing the lives of the two 
criminals in question, who obtained, in consequence, from the Sultan the order 
that Hussein Pasha and Sheikh Ahmed Effendi should be confined in a fortress 
for life. 

Though no knowledge of the conspiracy appears to have previously existed 
in the northern provinces of Turkey, the general opinion among Mussulmans 
unconnected with the Government has been, since its discovery, in favour of it, 
on account of the objects proposed by the conspirators ; viz., the suppression of 
peculation and abuses in high quarters, and the diminution of the ruinous 
extravagance generally attributed to the Seraglio. | 

Indeed, the intelligence of the failure of the plot was not, I am credibly 
informed, satisfactory in Asia Minor, where a good deal of discantent prevails, 
and this especially at the head-quarters of the army of Anatolia; nor is such 
discontent surprising, considering the constant irregularity observed everywhere 
as to the payment of the military. ‘The troops in Bosnia and the Herzegovina 
are, for instance, eight months in arrear, a hardship more severely felt by the 
Rediffs, who, a year sinee, were taken from their homes and agricultural pursuits, 
leaving no provision for their families, Such insufficient funds as have trom 
time to time been remitted, either direct trom Constantinople or as have been 
afforded out of the halfcollected revenue of the province, are absorbed in 
purchasing provisions, which contractors will no longer provide on credit. 

The continued resistance of the revolted villages on the Montenegrin 
frontier had determined Dervish Pasha to strike a decisive blow, anda large 
body of Irregulars was collected for that purpose, but in consequence of the 
timely submission of the insurgent districts of Viva and Drobniak (in the Herze- 
ovina), | understand that the force in question had not left Mostar. 

‘As to the delimitation of the boundary, its progress has been attended with 
even greater difficulty than could have been expected, 

Vassit Pasha refuses to recognize that portion of the new Montenegrin 
frontier laid down by the French and Russian Commissioners m the neighbour- 
hood of Nisksich, which gives to Montenegro three or four villages not marked 
in the official map. Much excitement exists, on the other hand, among the 
Turks of Nisksich, regarding the cession made to Montenegro, and it will not be 
easy to satisfy the demands of those Mussulman proprictors whose lands, by the 
new arrangement, have fallen within the adjoining Principality. Nor ts this all: 
the Commission, threatened with violence by the people of Spuz, was actually 
compelled to retire to Seutart, where, though the Porte sent orders for employing 
force, if necessary, in their behalf, it remained some time, though Tam happy to 
say its labours have been recommenced, 

On the Servian frontier, motives of economy have dictated a large reduction 
of force, and the army of observation stationed at Novi Bazaar, Pristina, Nisch, 
and Sofia, has been reduced to 8,000 men instead of 20,000, notwithstanding 
the military schemes of Prince Milosch, which it is presumed he can hardly 
push forward in the winter season ; and, indeed, respecting which it appears he 
has been lately warned by Russia to be more cautions. 

Little ia known of the proceedings of the late Skouptchina, as they have 
not been published, But his Highness has at least gained two objects he had 
at heart, this body having first consented to the reduction of the number of 
Senators to about a filth of that fixed by the Hatti-Sheriff of 1830, Article 17 ; 
and, secondly, given the Prince the power to collect mone wherever it can be 
found, to meet the expenses of an increased military establishment. 

The law of succession has been also promulgated, and allows Prince Michael, 
now declared heir-apparent, to adopt a succession in the event of his demise 
without male heirs; and provides that on any interruption of the adoptive line, 
‘asue of Obrenowitch in the female line may be chosen; and that even in the 
cage of all the above conditions becoming inexceutable, whosoever may be declared 
Prince by popular election must assume the name of Obrenowitch, and be held 
to represent the Dynasty of Milosch. 

This sufficiently shows thut the recent Assembly was a mere tool in the 
hands of a party, and it is more than probable that it has overdone its work. 

In Wallachia a new Government has been formed, under the presidency of 
M. John Ghyka (the ex-Prince of Samos). In reporting this appointment, 


19 


Mr. Green remarks: “It is difficult to say which trusts the other the least 

Prince Couza or his Prime Minister; and I believe myself that the Prince has 
formed a pretty just estimate of the character of the Minister, and the Minister 
of that of the Prince. 

The first act of the new Administration was to slacken the restrictions 
imposed on the press by the late one; and as the caution-money required by the 
recent regulations will no longer be exacted, the newspapers have commenced 
reappearing. 

The Hospodar left Bucharest for Moldavia on the #8th of October, and his 
presence at Ismail, where the inhabitants of the newly-acquired territory loudly 
complain of the exactions of the authorities, may prove benelicial. 

It is to be observed thar he seems tired of the part of Constitutional 
Sovereign he has hitherto professed to play even to affectation ; and whilst 
taking upon himself all responsiblity for the acts of his Ministers, he has 
likewise assumed alone the supreme command of the army of the two 
Principalities. 

From the configuration of the frontier in the neighbourhood of Bayazid 
(near which town the Russian, Persian, and Turkish territories meet) ) any 
displacement of boundary is at that point of more than ordinary importance. I 
have therefore ealled the attention of the Porte to the information which has 
reached me, that some of the pyramids of stone erected by the Boundary 
Commission for Russia and ‘Turkey have been destroyed by the Kurds in. the 
neighbourhood of Bayazid, although T learn at the same time from Mr. Dalyell, 
that, in consequence of orders from the Pasha of Erzercum, measures are being 
taken for replacing the pyramids on their proper sites. 

In contrast with the conduct of some Provincial Governors, I have to notice 
the universal satisfaction which is given by Ahmed Pasha, of Rhodes. He 
lately undertook a tour in the islands of the Turkish Archipelago, which are 
under his jurisdiction, for the purpose of ascertaining their condition and wants. 
By his unassisted efforts he established Commercial Tribunals at Scio and 
Lemnos, so that they and the other larger Islands of Rhodes, Cyprus, and 
Mytilene, are af present in possession of an institution by which it is to be 
expected that the interests of merchants, whether native or foreign, will be 
protected, and trade in general receive a new impulse. He also reformed many 
abuses, and made several regulations for the amelioration of the condition of the 
people, instituting a searching inquiry into the conduct of officials, including the 
Archbishop of Searpanthos, who for many years has proved the scourge of that 
island, and who will be banished to Patmos. | 

[ have laid some stress on these details, because they prove, first, that there 
are some Turks in high stations who understand the necessity of, and take an 
interest in, the improvement of their country ; and secondly, that if (amongst the 
few Turks capable of undertaking such a task) a few Pashas were chosen who 
invested with great influence and authority, should visit, inspect, and reform the 
administration of the principal districts of the Empire, much more would be done 
for ameliorating its conditions in a short space of time than will ever be effected 
by the Pashas of a second-rate grade, with a small local authority, who are either 
completely in the hands of a clique of persons in their province, or never 
venture to do anything without reference to Constantinople. 

_ _Uhave long pressed upon the Porte a plan of this kind, and great benefits 
will, I am confident, be the result, if | ultimately succeed in getting it 
adopted, 5 

In Syria the unsettled state of Kesrouan continues. The peasantry still 
oppose their Chiefs, the Hazins, who appeal in vain to the authorities for aid to 
be enabled either to return to their homes, or to be put in possession of their 
roperty, confiscated by the leader of the revolt against their hereditary authority. 
Teanwhile some of the members of this ancient family are compelled to seek 
charity m the different places in which they are dispersed. ‘Themselves fugitives 
their families are at the mercy of the hostile population. 

A sum of 20,000 iastres has been assessed on the Druses who burnt and 
plundered property, subsequently to the encounter of Beit-Meri. This sum is 
not considered adequate, and but a small portion of the pillaged property has 
been restored, the authors escaping hitherto punished. 

___ Agents of the French Consul-General are traversing the disturbed districts, 
Twenty-seven persons compose the staff of that Agent, and large supplementary

be executed. 

In concluding this brief sketch of the last month's events, I ought to say 
that, in recording the changes in aflairs from time to time, I merely paint the 
variations of a kaleidescope the contents of which never really alter, although 
they are constantly taking a new position and appearance. The only thing that 
ean create a really new state of things in Turkey would be a thorough reform in 
its financial system, and till we see what the new Grand Vizier will do in this 
respect everything respecting the future must be considered as uncertain.

No. 15. 


Sir H, Bulwer to Lord J, Russell.—(Recetved December 10.) 
(No. 282.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, November 30, 1859. 

I READ with great attention the inclosure from the Treasury contained 
in your Lordship’s despatch No. 143, relative to the report which 1 forwarded to 
your Lordship respecting the state of the Turkish finances; and I ought to state 
that that report was simply intended to give your Lordship what I believe had 
never before been sent to the Foreign Office—a short and rapid sketch of the 
financial state of this country, and my opinions thereupon without overburdening 
the same by details, or laying down as absolute doctrines ideas which after all 
must depend on facts in no small degree uncertain, 

On some points, however, IT cannot help thinking that my observations 
have been misunderstood, since the counter-observations made to them hardly 
apply. 

Mr, Laing, for instance, seams to consider the suggestion which I made to 
the Turkish Government as to the advisability of employing a Turco-Kuropean 
Commission in order to establish the basis of a new fisanial system, and also 
my recommendation as to the employment, for some time to come, of Europeans 
in certain branches of any new financial administration that might thus be 
adopted, as counselling an abdication by the Ottoman Government of its own 
sovercign and national authority in financial matters. Now the question of 
Turkish ignorance on such matters being admitted, | know-nf no other way of 
informing the ignorant than by the employment of persons capable of giving the 
information required; and a Commission composed of Turks of eminence, and 
of Europeans specially versed in financial affairs, seemed, and still seems to me, 
the best mode of combining a system which has to be framed upon the two 
elements of a knowledge of Turkey and its resources, and a knowledge of the 
manner in which the resources of a country are generally made most available, 
and its receipts and expenditure best regulated. It seems to me also, I confess, 
that if a new financial system founded upon European practice be adopted, it 
would require, as atmatter of course, for some time to come people versed in 
that practice to set it in movement; but see nothing in all this which is either 
directly or indireetly a cession of the independent power of Turkey over her

without some proof that the language held on this subject is sincere. 

As to the mode of raising the funds necessary for the works on the Danube, 
the question is one on which Her Majesty's Government is far better able to 
form an opinion than I can be; but I cannot help fulfilling a duty by saying 
that, as the resources of the Turkish Empire are known to be, at the present 
time, in so embarragsed a condition, the expenses of the Danubian Commission 
should be very closely watched. Nor is it to be forgotten that the opening of 
the Kustendjt Railway tends not only to diminish the requirements of the future 
permanent works that are projected, but also that of the temporary works 
which are now being constructed, concerning which I think it might be desirable 
to fix, as early as possible, upon some definite point at which the continued 
outlay is to cease, 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER. 


No. 16. 


Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J, Rusgell,—(Recetved March 5.) 
(No. 104.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, February 21, 1860. 

THE Turkish Government has recently been acting up to its promises, and 
in some degree according to the system I some time since suggested, that is to 
say, a regular budzet is being drawn up through the aid of the Mixed Financial 
Commission appointed last year at my recommendation, and the depreciated 
paper-money called kaimés, the existence of which has tended so prejudicially to 
influence the exchange, is on the point of being withdrawn; an arrangement 
being also made for withdrawing gradually, and, as 1 think, too gradually, the 
beshliks, or present silver currency which is current in the provinees, and which 
ranks much higher than the kaimé, though it is consigoribly lower m real 
value than the sum for which it passes current. | 

In order, however, to complete the amount necessary for withdrawing the 
kaimés, it is the intention of the Porte to levy on Constantinople and its 
neighbourhood where this paper now circulates, a special tax, whether on the 
tenants of houses and lands, or on those lands and houses themselves, is not quite 
certain. 

If on the latter, as houses and lands can only be heid by law by Turkish 
subjects, and when possessed by Europeans are only possessed under some fiction 
or arrangement which represents the proprietor to be a Rayah, the Turkish 
Government will naturally be able to place the same tax on that property which 
is possessed irregularly by Muropeuns that it does on the same property held 
regularly by Rayahe. 

With regard to a tax on the tenants of lands or houses according to the 
value of the rent paid, I think also if that tax is levied fairly on Ottoman 
subjects and foreigners, there can be no reasonable ground of complaint. 

The letter and spirit of our Treaty of 1838 is to the effect that British 
subjects should be, with respect to internal impositions, ou the same footing as the 
most-favoured Turkish subjects ; nor can the exemption from haratch, a personal 
tax, imposed formerly as a mark of degradation on Christian Rayahs, from which 
the Capitulations declare that Englishmen shall be free, be held, I think, a8 
granting an exemption from that general taxation not then contemplated, and to 
which it did not refer. 

The obvious injustice of allowing foreign residents to be exempt from the 
ordinary taxation imposed on native residents, even of the most-favoured class, 
needs no comment; and unless | hear from your Lordship to the contrary I shall 
consider that matter settled by your Lordship’s despatch No. of 
relative to the Principalities : this view being also in conformity with the views 
of other foreign Powers—Russia alone, | believe, excepted. 

Another question has, however, arisen both here and in the Principalities on 
which I take a different view, I believe, from some of my colleagues. | allude to 


a tax which has been laid not only on all shopkeepers, but also on importing 
merchants, 


24 


The foreigners keeping a shop cannot, I think, object to pay, for the libert 
of doing so, what the native subject pays. This is a tax upon @ profession an 
upon a person; but with respect to merchants importing goods, and who sell 
such goods only in wholesale, the case is very different. I do not look upon this 
as a tax upon a profession or a person, but in reality a tax upon foreign goods, and 
though 1am disposed to consider that our Commercial Treaties with Turkey would 
fairly admit of alteration, still as long 4s they exist their principle should be 
strictly maintained, and 1 think we ought to resist any tax of the above- 
mentioned description, not merely upon our own subjects importing goods, but 
also upon Turkish subjects or Moldo-Wallachians. The pretension in question 
‘s in fact reinstituting the very abuse which the Treaty of 1838 tended to 
destroy; and if I find that the attempt I thus denounce is insisted upon, I shall, 
f not advised otherwise, strenuously oppose it, and endeavour to get as many 
of my colleagues as I can to do so likewise. : 

I have, &e, | 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

Lord J. Russell to Sir H. Bulwer. 
(No, 100.) 
Sir, Foreign Office, March 8, 1860. 

L HAVE received your Excellency’s despatch No. 104 of the 21st ultimo, 
on the subject of the Porte’s measures of finuncialjreform, and I have to acquaint 
you that Her Majesty’s Government concur in your views with regard to the 
taxation of foreign residents in PY: 

I have further to state to you the opinion of Her Majesty's Government 
that taxes on exports ought to be lowered by the Porte for the advantage of 
Turkey herself. 

Tam, &e. 


(Signed) | J, RUSSELL.

No: 18. 


Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell.— (Received March 9.) 
(No. 122.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, February 28, 1860. 

IN my despatch No. 104 of the 21st instant I alluded to a measure which 
the ‘Turkish Government was likely to adopt with respect to taxation and the 
withdrawal of the caimé (paper money), 

‘A detailed account has lately been given by the Ottoman Government of 
the last loan of 5,000,000/, raised for the purpose of withdrawing the paper 
money in question. 

This loan only produced actually 3,888,2721, part of it having been sold at 
85, and the remainder at 60; the sum realized in piastres being 548,637,7024. 

The amount of caimé in circulation being 618,979,160 piastres, a further 
sum of 70,341,458 piastres is still wanted to effect and complete the measure in 
contemplation. ‘This sum the Porte purposes to make up partly by voluntary 
contributions of bankers, merchants, &c., partly by a tax on property to be paid 
by landlords, and estimated to produce 34,000,000 piastres, and partly by a tax 
on industrial Puree to be levied on shopkeepers, tradesmen, Ke. 

‘The Turkish Government in suddenly promulgating the decree on this 
subject has acted, as it often does act, without sufficient consideration or 
preparation. 

First, it was hardly necessary, for the small amount which a tax on shops 
in Constantinople will supply, to awaken a question which, unless discussed and 
determintd beiorchand, was certain to create ditficulties. Secondly, it seems 
unjust to tax first the proprictor for his property, anc then his tenant in virtue 
of that property. ‘The two taxes, in fact, will ultimately fall upon the tenant, 
and would do so if both were levied on the landlord; and yet, had they both 
been levied on the landlord as proprietor, inasmuch as the tax would then have 


25 


been a tax on proprietors, and consequently on persons held by the law to be 
Ottoman subjects (these alone being able to hold real property), no dispute would 
have arisen, 

But, on the other hand, the object for which the present tax on shops ts 
required is of a nature the most urgent; the benefit it will confer—a benefit in 
which foreign trade will largely participate—is great; the impost 1s but for 
once; and if it were now abandoned, the effect would be ruinous. 

The point, however, here under discussion 1s, can a fureign subject be 
justly called men to pay this tax on shopsor not? As to the tax on proprietors 
or property, there can, as I have said, be no difficulty, since no Englishman can 
publicly claim to be a proprietor; and if he is so indirectly or clandestinely, he 
must take the consequences of the position he has assumed. 

Looking at our Capitulations alone, I find two passages, viz. :— 

Article XU. “That all Englishmen and subjects of England who shall 
dwell or reside in our dominions, whether they be married or single, artisans or 
merchants, shall be exempt from all tribute.” 

Article XXXII. “ ‘That no kassabie (excise or duty) on animal food shall 
be demanded of the English, or any subjects of that nation.” 

The Turkish translation of the first Article ought to be, Tam told, “haratch 
or poll tax,” instead of ‘tribute.’ At all events | doubt whether either of 
these Articles so worded—established under circumstances so different—could 
justify our claiming the exemption from the tax (ie, the tax on shops or trades) 
now under consideration,—a tax levied equally on all classes whether Ottoman 


or foreign, and having for its object a great public benefit, 

By the Treaty of 1858, Article TH, indeed, it is laid down that ‘if any 
article of Turkish produce, growth, or manufacture, be purchased by the British 
merchant, or his agent, for the purpose of selling the same for internal consump- 
tion in Turkey, the British merchant, or his agent, shall pay at the purchase 
and sale of such articles, and in any manner of trade therein, the same duties 
that are paid in similar circumstances by the most favoured class of Turkish 
subjects engaged in the internal trade of ‘Turkey, whether Mussulmans or 
Rayahs.’ And though it might be questionable whether the word duties applied 
to goods could strictly be construed into taxes or persons, yet L think the general 
sense of the Article is that English subjects may vie with Turkish subjects in any 
trade or profession in Turkey on equal terms. 

But the Russians and Swedes have in their old Treaties words more general 
thaw ours, as may be seen from the following extracts :-- 

Article IL] of Treaty between Russia and Turkey of 1783 :—" Pareillement 
on ne doit pas exiger deux (les Russes) le droit nommeé haratz ou quelque 
autre impdt, et aprés*qu'ils auront payé les douanes c¢tablies en vertu de ce 
‘Traité pour les marchandises qu’ils auront avec eux, les Pachas, Kadis, et autres 
officiers seront tenus de les laisser passer sans empéchement.” 

Treaty with Sweden:—“ On ne demandera point de haratch des sujets 
Suédois qui sont établis en Turquie, soit muri¢s ou non mariés, qui ont 
le commerce, et on ne demandera pas également le haratch des autres sujets 
Suddois résidant en ‘Turquie, et is seront exempts de tout autre impét ou 
contribution,” 

Under these passages the Russians claim exemption from every species of 
tax, and the Russian Legation means to stand out upon this Article; and if 
‘ts resistance is successful an immense obstacle will be placed in the way of any 
possible amelioration of the Turkish finances, and consequently of Turkish 
improvement altogether,;—sinee the privileges asserted must either prevent this 
Government from increasing its resources by any species of taxation, or by 
exempting foreigners from that taxation, and only imposing it on Mussulmans 
or Rayahs ; it will cramp the industry and check the wealth of its own subjects, 
and throw into the hands of persons whose means will be derived from their 
residence and pursaitsin this country, but [rom whose means the Government. of 
the country can derive no support, every lucrative occupation or profession. 

The wording of the Russian Treaty of 1783 is, however, so broad and 
sweeping, that without some change had tuken place in that ‘Treaty, by subse- 
quent arrangements, 1 might be easy to dispute the justice of the exemption 
now claimed ; but it would not be easy to dispute its legality. 

A aveat change, however, as I have just stated, in the whole system of trade 


‘ 


r heeehe in = age 


26 


in this country did take place by the ‘Treaty of 1838, above alluded to, into 
which change Russia subsequently entered, 

Previous to that time nearly all trades were in the hands of Corporations, 
as had been the case not many years previously in Burope, Foreign residents 
could not and did not exercise any of these trades. ‘They imported goods, and 
might. travel through the country with their goods; but they were notin the 
position, as to general residence and traffic, of Ottoman subjects. 

Now the Treaty of 1838, in the Article quoted, gives them all the 
pees es ag to residence and traflie af Ottoman subjects. The Russians have 
enjoyed all the same privileges, conferred by the Treaty of 1838, that we have ; 
and, therefore, though their ‘Treaty of 178 is rather different from our old 
'Mreaties, the one is as much abrogated as the other by the succeeding Treaty. 
We all stand on precisely the same ground, and none can be favoured or imposed 
more than the other. 

The question, then, is whether we can claim the right to pay no taxes, 
which was accompanied by the privation of many privileges under the old 
Treaties, and the right of possessing these privileges, previously withlield from 
us, but granted under the new Treaty ; or whether, when we contend that we 
have the right, by the Treaty of 1783, to engage in all tratles and professions 1 
the same manner as the most favoured class of Ottoman subjects, we do not 
thereby abandon any wrivilege with respect to persons engaged in the said trades 
and professions which the most-favoured class of Ottoman subjects does not 
enjoy. 

“As for myself, | should not, whatever my opinions are, have, under any 
circumstances, compelled the payment of the present contribution by British 
subjects without reference to your Lordship ; and of course | shall not enforce 
their paying what other foreign subjects do nob pay. I think, moreover, that 
even in assenting to the right of the Turkish Government to tax foreign subjects 
+ would be well to place limits upon the exercise of that right, even for the 
sake of the ‘Turkish Government itself, as well us for that of its subjects, But 
I cannot help thinking it will be most unjust to give a foreigner all the advan- 
tages, with respect to trade and residence, of a native, und yet subject him to 
none of the burdens. 

This question should be taken up and settled or resettled. [fit was thought 
worth while to deal with the Capitulations generally, they might be remodelled, 
and made generally more applicable to existing circumstances; or if it were 
deemed easier merely to deal with difficulties as they practically arose out of 
or coupled with the Capitulations, then the Governments might simply agree 
through their He sresentatives here to explanations, to ie given in common, to 
those Articles which admitted of doubt, or might beeante impracticable or inap- 
plicable in their present form. 

This measure, however, which the Turks ought to have provoked betore- 
hand, will hardly be in time for the existing difficulty ; and whilst the tax 18 
generally levied, the point at issue with the Embassies and the Legations will 
probably have to be held in abeyance. 

[ have, however, deemed it better that the whole matter should be discussed 
here at the present time by the Representatives with Fuad Pasha, who may then 
state his own case; und L have consequently requested the Diplomatic Body 
to meet the Minister of Foreign Affairs at the English Palace on Saturday 
next. 

After that meeting the general feelings of the Diplomatic Body will be more 
clearly seen, and the future course to pursue, if the matter is to be taken into 
consideration by the different Cabinets, more easily determined, 

I shall, therefore, again trouble your Lordship on thia matter after the 
meeting I reter to has taken place; and I shall then endeavour to supply any 
deficiencies which may appear in my present communication, 

1 have, &e. 
(Signed) HENRY L, BULWER.

No. 19. 


Private Memorandum by Sir H. Bulwer.—(Heceived at the Foreign Office, 
March 18.) ; 
| Constantinople, March 7, 1860. 
; WITH respect to the special tax for completing the withdrawal of the 
caimés, one meeting has taken place amongst the Representatives themselves 
and one with the Representatives and Aali Pasha ; Fuad Pasi being ave. 

I deemed it advisable that in this manner all should near equally the 
language of the Porte, and that all should exchange their general ideas as to the 
obligations which their several Treaties reciprocally imposed. 

The Turkish Government makes this statement :-— 

We have long been aware that nothing is so destructive to commerce as 
the uncertainty which now prevails in all money speculations, owing to the 
constant and immense fluctuations in the exchange ; fluctuations kept up mainly 
by the depreciated state of the caimés or paper money. 

This is ruinous to us ; it is also, however, prejudicial to all persons. settled 
here and doing business here, foreigners as Ottoman subjects. 

We have done our utmost to remedy the evil. By a loan we have procared 
the funds which were necessary to extinguish the ereater portion of the notes in 
question, Asmall number remain, however, which ought to be extinguished 
without delay. 4 . 

To obtain the funds for this we have instituted a contribution, 10 per cent. 
on the rent of property in Constantinople, all of which is levied on Ottoman 
subjects ; and 6 per cent. levied on the rent of houses inhabited by persons 
engaged in trade ; amongst these are foreign subjects, and as these are precisely 
in the same position as nalive subjects, and moreover with respect to their trade 
or eceupation will derive the same advantages that the native subjects will do 
from the measure we have in view, we think we may fairly claim that they 
should equally pay this contributton. d : ‘ 

We deem that the vight of this claim is founded on our Treaties, and 
espectally the ‘Treaty of 1838; but at the same time, as we do not wish to 
decide this question, or treat it in any abrupt or hasty manner, or without due 
consultation with the respective Governments with which we haye entered into 
stipulations, we solemnly declare that we shall not hold this special tax, 
occasioned in a special locality by a special and urgent want—the satis(ying of 
which will be a common benefit—as any example as to the future, or as any 
reason for prejudicing, one way or the other, the general question as to taxation 
which has to be discussed. 

Upon this statement all the Representatives have concurred in holding 
pretty uicarly the following language :— : . 

We ape the position that is deseribed to us. We recognize the 
benefit which the tax in question is to secure. We acknowledge that that 
benefit will be shared by all persons, foreigners as natives, and we shall not 
object to our various subjects paying the tax in question; we will even advise 
them to do so on the condition, clearly stipulated, that the special case forms no 
general rule, but we cannot undertake to force them, nor can we allow them to 
be foreed, unless our Governments subsequently sanction the proceeding: and. 
on a suggestion of mine, Aali Pasha agreed to waive all question of force, and to 
write the different Representatives a note, saying that the Ottoman Government 


would simply communicate to the different Chancelleries the names of those who 
refused to pay. .

by Aali Pasha, we also all of us expressed our several opinions, and stated whit 
were our several ‘Treaties.

The care of the Government seems to have been directed to preventing the 
foreign merchant from paying taxes himself, though our Treaty simply stipulates 
as to his exemption from two taxes which my last despatch, No. 122 ot 
February 28th, set forth; but no provision seems tnade as to taxing merchiandize 
except at its entry. . 

As to the trades and professions, they were in the hands of Turkish corpo- 
rations, and the foreigner could not enter ‘nto them, nor did he compete in 
detail trade with the native, 

‘The consequence of this state of things was, that goods might be taxed at 
any price after thei entry, and the internal traffic of the country was nat 
practically open to foreigners. : 

The Treaty of 1888 made a general change in these particulars. 

No further charge was to be placed on goods than the general import and 
export dues; and British subjects were to be placed on the same footing as 
native ones with respect to trade and trafhie ‘athe interior, ‘This was not stated 
quite so clearly, perhaps, as it might have been, partly because the Treaty was 
of necessity made in a great hurry, its possibility depending on chances which a 
day might alter, and some fears being entertained that if we insisted absolutely 
up opening shops, competing with the corporations, and, 1 fact, taking precisely 
the position of a native, it would have led to long discussions ; but, nevertheless, 
what we desired was this, and we said quite enough in the Treaty to allow us to 
contend that we had obtained it. 

The natural consequences have followed ; corporations have disappeared or 
nearly so; foreigners everywhere exercise retail trade; and the goods which 
have paid the import duty are never charg sd with any other. 

What I hold is that, both for the sake of the ‘Turks and for our sake, we 
should still follow out, without chicane on our side or allowing chicane on the 
other, the system we had in 1898 in view. : 

‘\t a time of Turkish exclusive privilege it was fair enough of us to claim 
and hold counter privileges; but if we can now clearly and fully obtain all 
privileges in a country which the matives of that country possess, in regard to 
residing or exercising trades or occupations in that country, T do not think we 
can fairly claim more. Tam prepared, indeed, to contend that this 1s all we 
hoped for in 1658, or wished in 1838 to obtain; and that the Act was framed 
with a view of obtaining it, and not of obtaining more. 

| hold, consequently, that aecording to ‘Treaty, as according to policy and 
justice, we should take our stund on these grounds :— 

No further duties, without express stipulations, Which affect our imports 
and exports, or importers and exporters. 

With respect to retail trade, exercise of professions, residence and the 
like, the same privileges as the ‘Turkish subjects—no more, no less; and i 
order to make this condition more clear and more beneficial, [ would bargain for 
the abolition of all corporations which are ‘nacertain extent monopolies, and 
which ought, in my opimon. to have long since been done away with, as [ 
believe they would have been had it heen clearly understood that we accept the 
fair and equal condition on which we should then be placed with all Turkish 
subjects. 

This arrangement would be beneficial to the ‘Turks, because it would open 
to them a fair and equitable source of revenue, and relieve industry from the 
remnant of old monopolies. It would be advantageous to foreigners, since 
directly the ‘Turks saw that if they received all the privileges of ‘Turks, and 
made fortunes in ‘Turkey, their calling, and the income and gain they denved 
from it, would he a gain and an income to the Turkish Government itself; the 

jealousy whieh now not unnaturally exists would disappear, and the Porte 
would be as anxious to promote the settlement of foreigners in ‘Turkey as it is 
now the reverse, 

In fact, what can be more preposterous than to pretend that a Government 
ig to keep up all the expenses of a Government, maintaining order, protecting 
property ind the like, —and that persons not natives of the country should then 
come aud reside in il, carry on business in it, derive large fortunes from it, 
and vet pay nothing to the State, which would have to derive its income from its 
own subjects who are to be placed in an unequal position as to all the means by 
which wealth is obtained? 

The views | thus entertain are shared in by the Representatives of Austria,

; Sir H, Bulwer to Lord J. Russell. —(Kecewwed 3 : 
(No. 218.) (Receimed May 4.) 

My Lord, Constantinople, April 24, 1860, 

i HAVE refrained for some time from troubling you with a general 
biaternent as to affairs in this Empire, deeming that « too frequent exposition of 
those circumstances which affect it might weary the patience without having a 
claim to the attention of your Lordship. ‘ r 

a Jtappenrs to me, however, that at this season, one of those periodical reports 
¥ 0 i have heretofore at times addressed to you might not be unweleome, and 
ay ee papaya revieee the rincipal events which have occurred since my 

rival in this country, and then describe-as rapidly the characteristic fe i 
ARE es ith pidly the characteristic features of 
a ay had T arrived, when the melancholy account of the scenes of carnage 
: neh | i aondered the name of Jeddah notorious in our annals, reached here. 
Meh if of commercial rivalry, inflamed by religious fanaticism—a fanaticism 
. ie 1 the struggle in India had, in no small degree, contributed to arouse — were
