the Christian residents in the Ottoman Empire and as natur ene 
indignation of the Christian Powers. ue agsnabarally aneneney (De 

| at he to the Ministers of the Sultan, it is only fair to say that they 
eels with no less horror than dismay the intelligence of a massacre which 
Balt cred amongst its victims the Consuls of England and France, nor did they

shall only hold as complete when the indemnity which is under the consideration

of the Porte, and which I expect in two or three days from this time to announce

It was not, however, only the massacre at Jeddah that produced, at the 
time to which | am alluding, a general uneasiness and alarm. Throughout the 
whole of Turkey there reigned a feeling of mingled apprehension and Irritation, 
which the brutally-mangled victims of Montenegrin barbarity (soldiers deprived 
of their noses and ears, and thus only esca ing from their savage enemy) did not 
fail to increase. Dervishes from the further East on the one side, and refugees 
from different European States on the other, each, though from different motives, 
stirring up the opponent classes of the population ; the one vainly thinking to 
aid their cause in Hindostan by a Mahometan movement in these countries, and 
the other hoping that any outbreak here might unsettle the condition of the 
other States of Europe, traversed the Provinces and agitated the capital itself by 
fallacious prophecies and false reports; and some credit is undoubtedly due to 
the Government for the moderation and energy it displayed amidst a state of 
things so critical. The forces of Prince Danilo were defeated at Mostar. In 
Crete, a condition of affairs bordering on civil war and insurrection was, if not 
thoroughly amended, considerably softened down and improved by a double 
display of force and conciliation. In Thessaly, a rising of the Mussulmans, 
which at one moment seemed almost certain, was prevented ; and in the 
Northern Provinces of the Empire generally —provinces in which, for a moment, 
contempt for the local authority, rival feuds, and lawless habits, had produced 
‘neessant conflict, brigandage, and confusion—a vigorous and detrimental effort 
to restore abedicnce and order was so far suecessful as to reduce things to the 
ordinary level of partial submission and imperfect administration which are 
normal in those districts. In this capital, a police little seen, but by no means 
‘neffective, discovered a variety of half-hatched conspiracies, which, by generally 
warning or removing their leaders, sank into nothing ; and on the whole, whilst 
I regretted there was not the wisdom, activity, and concentration of purpose 
sufficient to establish the powers of government ona firmer basis, promising 
permanent tranquillity, 1 was certainly struck by the dexterity and judgment 
with which the most glaring evils were mitigated, and clouds that looked so 

black and menacing dispersed without a storm, 

The agitation, however, which subsided in those spots where the Turks had 
free scope for their action, remained in those frontier Provinces only nominally 
subjected to the Sultan. The Moldo-Wallachian Principalities took “ Union 
under a foreign Prince!” as their war-cry against Turkish authority ; whilst 
Servin, over the faction and discontent of which old Milosch (then inhabiting 
Bucharest) had an habitual sway, suffered its dissatisfaction to be concentrated 
into a rising against Prince Cara Georgeviteh, and the election of the wily and 
cruel oetagenarian who is now at the head of the Principality. 

An both these occasions, the only policy which the Porte could pursue was 
that of compromising with events. It aceeded to the election of Couza as Prince 
of the Moldo-Wallachian Principalities under certain conditions, and accepted as 
much of the demands of the Servian Skouptchina, or Popular Assembly, as 
related to the bringing of the Obrenovitch family back again into power, 

In these respects it is diffieult to criticise its conduct, inasmuch as that no 
ather course, not manifestly more dangerous, was open to it; for, although we 
must confess that the Moldo-Wallachian and Servian questions were thus merely 
adjourned, and will have (like most of the questions concerning this Empire, 
now rising into serious discussion, now again sinking back into silence and 
obscurity) to be finally disposed of in a more clear and decided manner; still, 
what was done at the moment was the best for the moment. 

Seareely, however, had the difficulties which these matters occasioned 
disappeared, When more serious ones arose in the prospect of European war, 
which, though in its origin confined to Italy, was not unlikely to extend much 
further; and this Government was called upon, with an empty exchequer, to 
make immense pecuniary sacrifices in order to meet a danger, of a vital nature, 
that could nat be overlooked. Great sacrifices were made; and it must be 
allowed that they produced all that could have been expected from them, and 
that the regular and irregular forees put on foot were found more than suflicient, 
notwithstanding all the ramours of plots and revolutions that were abroad, to 
maintain order, and to prevent even 80 much as a local disturbance or dangerous 
symptom of disatfection. 


al 


The work, nevertheless, which this Government had upon its hands was by 
no means easy of execution, for insurrection amongst the Rayah population was 
not more to be feared than the rising amongst the dissatisfied Mussulmans ; and 
indeed, a plot which was detected in the summer of last year, and at the head of 
which was a General of some distinction, bore evidence to the fact that if the 
dominant party at the Porte has not been able to do all that might justly satisty 
the Rayahs’ demands, it has done enough to provoke a large portion of the 
Rayahs’ ancient masters: a consideration which should not, most decidedly, 
check all reasonable efforts towards progress in the same direction, but which 
should put some limit upon rash counsels and extravagant expectations, — 

At last, and when a new Grand Vizier was appointed for the special purpose 
of endeavouring to profit by an intervening moment of repose for strengthening 
credit and reforming the finances, the rench Government for the first time 
announced openly its support of the celebrated Canal scheme of M. de Lesseps, 
and that gentleman arrived here under the avowed patronage of the French 
Ambassador. Hence a new cause for consternation, and for distracting the 
minds of the Turkish Ministers from the pressing exigencies of their internal 
affairs. Fortunately, by the skill and tact of Fuad Pasha, and a suitable 
forbearance on the part of the Foreign Representatives most concerned, this 
matter was at least deferred, and placed in @ position which, when it has again 
to be considered, will have in a certain degree settled its character, and 
diminished a portion of the dangers by which it was previously surrounded, 

In this rapid sketch of the principal events of the last year and a-balf, I 
have as yet said nothing of occurrences in Asia, because they have been purely 
of a local character, disturbing, but not threatening, to overturn the established 
order of things. Desultory warfare with the Arab: tribes, dissensions in the 
Lebanon, and the ordinary effects of a weak and bad administration in Syria, 
have been doubtless sources of constant regret, but not of any serious alarm. 
Nor has there been much deserving especial notice, except the total failure of 
Omer Pasha’s administration ; for certain violent and illegal acts during which 
he is shortly about to be placed on his trial. 

Montenegro,.—The principal subjects which now demand attention are, 

first, the Montenegrin frontier. The Commissioners have marked out the 
Montenegrin frontier line, but have left the quarrels and conflicts as to private 
claims of property unsettled all along the line. Sir Francis Gilbert justly points 
out the fully of this course, if there is any intention to make the delimitation 
itself of any practical use, and the Pashas who govern the provinces adjoining to 
Montenegro expressed the same opinions as Sir F. Gilbert very clearly. For 
my own part, I should have wished the Commissioners to have made a 
vigorous effort for determining at all events the principal questions subject to 
doubt or disagreement, but some of these gentlemen were evidently averse to 
prolonging their stay in a disagreeable country, where they were employed in 
a disagreeable duty; whilst others, following perhaps the policy of their Govern- 
ments, which is, I fear, to leave the germs of discord behind them, and thus 
invalidate the advantage which the Commission, if its labours had been fully 
carried out, Was calculated to produce, showed so much disinclination to under- 
take the work, which the carrying out of my views would have thrown upon 
them, and the opinions of my colleagues ecemed so little clear or certain, that 
I found it useless to persist in my plans, und have got the other Representatives 
to ageee with me in recommending what appears to me the best course after 
that which is abandoned, viz., some regular means of direct communication and 
understanding between the Prince and the Pashas in the frontier provinces, and 
the reference of cases not in this way decided, to the Consuls of the Five 
Powers. 

Suez Canal.—There is still pending the question of the Canal of Suez, It 
ig singular, but as yet 1 have been unable to obtain any clear notice as to what 
M. de Lesseps is doing on this subject, how far the works are being.proceeded 
with, or how far French immigration is gomg on. T am, however, persuaded 
that if Egypt is to be prevented from becoming a French Colony, a very clear 
and decided conduct is to be maintained on this subject. The canal itself may 
be a myth, and lead to no practical result; but the ascendancy and long-growing 
determination of the French to obtain a hold in Egypt is no myth, and has a 

tactical object, whatever may be the result that will attend the endeavours to 

obtain it; nor can We implicitly rely on any firm or bold policy here. ‘Mis 


Government will only be held firm if it finds we are 80, and if, therefore, we 
mean to deal practically with this question, we must look at it practically as 4 
question which we should either resolutely oppose, of be prepared to eee 
carried against us, In the meantime the whole colonization seheme which 1s 
connected with it, whatever may be done eventually as to the canal itself, should 
surely be stopped, and the intervention of the French Government appealed to, 
if necessary, on the subject. 

Syria.—Thirdly comes the question of Syria. To expect the same state of 
things in Syria that existsin a Sells or even ill-, governed province in Europe, is 
wut of the question, The warlike and more than half-barbarous mountaineers 
are in one quarter habituated to a state of military independence. In another, 
the wild Arabs of the Desert have through all times defied civilization, and 
resorted to plunder wherever there was not a superior force to overawe their 
temerity, or punish their misdeeds, In the plains there exists a peasantry 
thrifty and industrious, but for ages oppressed and subdued. How can all these, 
by the wand of an enchanter, be at once called into an homogencous class of 
cultivators, artisans, shopkeepers, and merchants obedient to the law, and 
acknowledging that equality before it which distinguishes the citizens of our 
modern communities? It appears that, for some time at least, there is only a 
choice between the two extremes of disorder generated by licence, and submission, 
the consequence of power, which will rarely be unaccompanied by oppression. 
At the present time, however, these two extremes appear unhappily associated, 
Wherever the ‘Turk is sufficiently predominant to be implicitly obeyed, laziness, 
corruption, extravagance, and penury mark his rule; and wherever he is too 
feeble to exert more than a doubtful and nominal authority, the system of 
government which prevails is that of the Arab robber and the lawless Highland 
chieftain. 

The state of things now existing in the Lebanon appears, indeed, to be 

unfortunately caleulated to prevent the progress of civilization, and defy the 
principles of order. ‘There is first the ‘Turkish authority, which has a nominal 
supremacy, but it is on every side hemmed im by the privileges accorded to the 
cveat Mountain tribes, to whom every right accorded seems the constitution of an 
abuse. Then there is the Kaimakam, or leader of the Druses, who governs this 
peculiar people on the one side, and the Christian Kaimakam (himself a Catholic), 
who governs the Catholic and Greek Christians on the other. Society amongst 
these tribes is what it was two or three centuries ago in the north of Scotlind., 
The head of a great family exercises an uncertain sway over his own elan and the 
smaller clans, whose obedience and support he is forced to purchase by conces- 
sions to their appetite for plunder and revenge. A sentence has to be executed 
against a Druse or a Christian in the Lebanon. He owes money out of his own 
district, and its payment is demanded ; or he has assaulted or murdered a man 
out of his district, and has to be punished. We will suppose the party injured is 
an Englishman: what takes place? The English Consul addresses himself to 
the Pasha, who says ‘! Yes!” and addresses himself to the Christian or Druse 
Kaimakam, as the ease may require. “The Kaimakam says “ Yes!” but he is in 
no wise concerned in the debt that is claimed, or the crime that has been perpe- 
trated: it was out of his territory, where the disorder which may prevail is of 
no import to him, and with which he has no community of sympathy or interest, 
On the other hand, the debtor or the criminal is one of his own people, from 
whom he bus received or expects a service; and without any inducement 
to compel him to be honest, or to punish him for not being so, he has every 
‘mducement to shield his delinquency, Is it marvellous, then, that nine times 
out of ten, the English Const is never refused what he demands, but never 
obtains what he secks? The ‘Turk himself has in reality no strong desire that 
his own order should produce effect, being, it 1s said, glad to show that those 
Governments which have been established independent of his own are an injury 
to the Powers who constituted them; and when the pains he apparently takes 
to satisfy the foreign and complaining functionary fail, he shrugs his shoulders, 
and says, What can Ido? you know well enough I have no power?" 

Nor do these evils stand alone, Although the majority of Christians under 
the Christian Kaimukam are Catholics, there is a constderable number of Greeks 
who hate their brother Christians worse than they do the Mussulmans, and who 
are, in fact, more oppressed by them than they would be by the Mussulmans, 

As the patron of the Catholics, moreover, founding the title on a traditional 


od 


pretension which ought assuredly to have been annihilated in 1840, steps forward 
the powerful Government of France. The Maronite is his pet and protegé: 
anything done against him offends French dignity, and must be resented by the 
French Consul-General, who is, in fact, the Christian Kaimakam. 

Thus, as far as the Lebanon is concerned, I confess that 1 look upon all 
hope of radical improvement as utterly hopeless, unless some new arrangement, 
some agreement, simple and concentrated in its character as to the government 
of that country, is entered into by the Porte, and consented to by the Great 
Powers. Some changes for the better, however, might be made; and it would, 
doubtless, be a great change for the better if tribunals were established at 
Beyrout, before which the Maronite or Druse could be brought, especially if the 
claims of the Catholic monasteries to an independent jurisdiction were done 
away with, 

‘The abuses of the Lebanon are, however, only a small part of the general 
condition of Syria, which is so bad in all respects as to necessitate absolutely the 
supervision of some one of experience and intelligence, invested with adequate 
authority to effect prompt and radical reforms; and the appointment of such a 
person is one of the points which I intend to insist upon as soon as the conclu- 
sion of the Bairam permits the renewal of business. 

Moldo-Wallachia and Servia.—4th, With regard to Moldo-Wallachia and 
Servia. The visit of Prince Couza is expected during this summer, and only 
delayed from the hostile disposition of the Chambers, and the dislike of His 
Highness to wear a fez on his presentation to the Sultan for investiture, which 
has of late years been the custom. 

These difficulties are transient or easy to be overcome, but the task of 
prevailing on the Government of the Principalities to flatter the pride of the 
Porte by the ostensible signs of respect and submission, and of getting the Porte 
to establish solid friendly relations with the Principalities by seeking to consult 
and promote their interests, is no easy one, and can only be accomplished by 
the united and constant efforts of the Representatives here, and of the Consuls 
General at Bucharest and Jassy. 

‘As to Servia, the rumours of approaching insurreetion so long rife have, in 
a certain degree, subsided, and the presence of a Servian deputation here is 
likely to be productive of advantage. 

The whole of its demands amount, indeed, to little short of independence ; 
and are certainly in no wise required for the prosperity of the Servian nation at 
this moment, But in favour of these, none of my colleagues have as yet 
expressed themselves well-disposed, and this fact alone is of no small significance, 
and can hardly, one should think, but have its weight at Belzrade, ‘There are, 
however, other subjects whereon the Servians have just reason for complaint, 
and with respect to these we shall use our united influence with the Ottoman 
Government on the side of Servia. 

| cannot help, on this occasion, observing, that in the various réunions of 
the Representatives on various subjects which have taken place at my invitation, 
the result has always been to produce a joint decision, an as matters have been 
discussed solely in reference to those matters, and without any personal feeling 
of vanity or attempt at individual influence amongst ourselves, it has generally 
been found that our differences have been in no wise important. ; 

Finances. —5th. The subject which 1 confess to have had most at heart 
since my appointment to this post, understanding that it is the object of Her 
Majesty's Government to sustain and advance the condition of this Empire, is 
the establishment of something like order in its finances; and although it would 
be presumption to say that | had made the progress which I hoped and desired, 
[ must still give to myself the credit of having laid the foundations for a solid 
improvement, By the determination with which I checked the breach of faith 
of Safeti Pasha, who was applying to other purposes the English loan, especially 
eranted for the withdrawal of the caimeés, and also by my continual remon- 
strances as to the necessity of getting rid of the depreciated paper-moncy, which 
not only disordered all commercial transactions, but created an immense and 
perpetual loss to the ‘Turkish Administration, whether as to the foreign debt 
on whieh it had to pay the interest, or on articles of foreign manufacture which 
it had to purchase, the pound sterling on these occasions having to be bought at 
from 160 to 170 piastres, have been so far effectual that the caimeé is mow 
almost withdrawn, and by June will have ceased to exist; whilst the exchange

a saving of so much to the Turkish Government in its monetary transactions 
abroad. , 

But this is not all, By the institution of the mixed European and ‘Turkish 
Financial Commission, established on my representations, sounder and clearer 
ideas as to finanee. and administration are finding their way into the mind o 
Turkish officials; the first and almost immediate consequence of which will be 
a regular budget, for the formation of which each department has to send in ant 
to justify the account of its expenses. This has engendered, i its turn, a 
certain attempt at economy and retrenchment in these various departments, and 
Lam assured that when the present statement of regular expense and receipt is 
published, it will show @ balance in favour of the latter. 

As the facts will have to be, ina certain degree, tested and sifted by the 
Mixed Commission, there will be some guarantee for their authenticity; and 
when the Turkish Government fairly carries out its intention of publishing 
annual budgets this will not only be a satisfaction to the people, but & check 
upon the Sovereign; nor should I fail to add that by such publication the 
Ottoman Government will be in advance of both the Russian and Austrian 
Governments. 

The great and main impediment in the way of such improvement as would 
otherwise be possible, consists in the loans contracted at Galata for short periods 
for temporary emergencies, for which the incoming revenue is being constantly 
miven as O security, and for which, at each renewal, immense sacrifices are 
exacted, ‘This burden on the annual revenue, with other engagements of asimilar 
character, amounts toa very large sum, and how to get this sum is the present 
question. 

My first advice to the Turkish Government is to endeavour to make evident 
its own efforts, and the desire to take advantage of its own resources ; first, by 
making some regulation as to the property called “yacout,” which is under 
different titles the property of the State, and which has only at present a very 
uncertain and limited value, since, on failure of direct descent, it reverts to the 
Church or Government, but which, by being converted into real property, could 
bring a large resource to the Exehequer, and be of immense service to the 
encouragement of industry in the country ; secondly, by making out a table or 
specification of the different mines and forest which are of immense value, but 
which are wholly unproductive, accompanied with a permission to foreign 
companies who will undertake to render them available to have the grants made 
in their own name, and not in the name of Rayahs, who tou frequently defraud 
the Government on the one hand, and the foreigners whom they represent on 
the other; and thirdly, by the employment of prisoners and the large number 
of Circassians who are now not only utterly useless, but very expensive, in 
organised companies on certain roads, which would open the mines aforesaid 
and a large tract of fertile country to the Black Sea, from which they are but a 
few miles distant. ‘This at once creates ‘a new source of wealth, and testilies an 
activity and determination towards practical and material improvement of which 
the Ottoman authorities have hitherto given no efficient proofs; but after all 
these measures, a loan will still be wanting. If this Government seeks it from 
doubtful sourees and second-rate capitalists, little will be done; and if money 
+s advanced without certain stipulations as lo its employment, either the advance 
required will not be found, or will be ineffectually thrown away. But if any 
great houses will undertake to supply the Ottoman Government with what is 
‘mmediately necessary on such conditions as I could point out, | think they will 
be doing that which will prove highly beneficial to themselves, and at the same 
time be the saving of this Empire, Which is at this moment in one of those 
erises in which ulter rain or creat, progress depends upon the conduct it 
pursues and the aid it may obtain. 

{ bring all these considerations before your Lordship, because if we are 
really interested in the maintenance of the Turkish Enipire, it is desirable to 
vive it at least such moral support at this time as ig in our power; whilst I 
should also add that all those antG respecting which we have professed our- 
selves concerned, depend on that financial condition which can alone furnish the 

means of effecting them, 

Without financial order there is no possibility of administrative improve- 
ment; with financial order it 1s impossible for a State vo be wholly misgoverned. 


Tid 


If your Lordship favoured the project of encouraging the immigration of 
Europeans into this country, by placing them ina situation of equality with the 
native population ; and if your Lordship also favoured a change in the Capitula- 
tions, which by improving the means of production in Turkey, would, and must 
eventually, be most beneficial to foreign commerce, which cannot but depend on 
the internal prosperity of the State with which such commerce is to be carried 
on, it would, I venture to say, be possible to give a new life to these people and 
their Government which, on the one hand, should not be oppressed and 
annihilated, but which should, on the other, be submitted to a well-regulated 
control, which without crushing the national energies which constitute its force 
should gradually eradicate the corrupt influences that cause its weakness. 

_ In the meantime | must impress this fact in the strongest manner on Her 
Majesty s Government, viz., that there is at the present moment at the head of 
ae ee four men equally remarkable for their integrity and ability, who 
anys risen to their exalted position solely on account of their integrity and 
ability ; and I feel confident that your Lordship will agree with me that when 
men without any other advantage than that of integrity and ability rise to the 
alan in a Borenaeat whatever in other respects that Government may 
beyond pea such Government represents cannot be considered lost 

_ Such a sympton in Turkey, though perhaps the onl startlin

No, 22. 
Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J, Russell—(Received June 29.) 


(No, a4.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, June 16, 1860. 


| CANNOT disguise from myself that the state of things here 1s 
most serious. The Empire, notwithstanding the difficulties which oppress 
and the abuses which undermine it, Bebe nevertheless have stood its 
ground, and the Turkish Government Sra ually worked on its way (under 
the encouragement and protection of friendly Powers) towards a more 
satisfactory condition, if it had been given a fair chance, and allowed that 
time which ig the usual condition of safe and sure progress, But it 
appears evident that a plan of agitation for exciting the animosity of the 
rival ereeds and races, the one against the other, is now being actively 
carried out in European ‘Turkey, and every part of the Sultan's dominions 
where foreign influence can make itself felt, 

As to the designs of Russia, they seem to me clear. She formerly 
prepared means of external foree and external conquest. These were 
found inefficient in the last war, and destroyed. She now prepares means 
of internal disaffection, internal dissolution, in respect to which ib is easy 
for her to do much, and difficult for other foreign Powers to do anythin 
+1 the wav of combating her efforts, especially as she affects to concea 
her political motives ander the religious mask of Christian charity 
and fraternity. All that is now taking: place is thus explained: the 
Bulearian discontent ; the Servian discontent; the discontent now break- 
ing out for the first time amongst the Armenians, and which an Armenian 
General in the Russian service has been actively employed in fomenting ; 
lastly, the recent demonstration at St. Petersburgh itself. 

I may, doubtless, be mistaken, and be led bial by delusive appear- 
ances or exaggerated statements ; but whilst it does not seem to me 
certain that Russia is at this moment pre yared to make a bold and 
ostensible move to overthrow this Empire, it does seem to me certain that 
che has decided on its proximate fall; and that the manner in which this 
fall is to be brought about is decided upon also: the first necessity in 
order to carry out the projects in contemplation (in the manner antici- 
pated) being, to keep u sa constant state of agitation and insolvency, 
which, by preventing al improvement in the condition of the Empire, 
must end in its destruction; nor can the Porte at this moment counteract 
projects so insidious without extraneous assistance, ‘This ts, indeed, the 
point on which | now wish to concentrate your Lordship's attention. 

The first thing necessary for Turkey is financial order, and the first 
step towards it was the framing of a regular Budget. This, as | have 
stated in a former despateh, has heen done.* 

With respect to the amount of the present receipts, there can be no 
doubt, since the greatest part of the revenue is farmed; with respect to 
the present amount of expenditure, the account given is also pretty near 
the truth. But what is still required ts, @ close examination into, and a 
strict control over, the State expenses on the one side, and a better 
oreanization as to the collection and distribution of the revenue on the 
other, ‘The Sultan has promised me, in regard to the first, that each 
department shall be forced to furnish every detail of its proceedings to the 
Mixed Financial Commission, of which I have often spoken, and which 
will shortly assume a more general and positive character; whilst, in 
regard to the second, the Government has, in a similar spirit, resolved 
that proper rules should be laid down by the aforesaid Commission, and 
efficiently carried out by a Finance Department, constituted on a new 
basis, and containing Kuropeans with the special knowledge required for 
the task assigned to them. 

Now, according to the present Budget, | hear that the receipts and 
expenditure pretty nearly ianes each other, and it is no exaggeration to 
say that the control and revision proposed are likely to. « iminish the 
expenditure by at cast one-third, and inerease the receipts in the same 


* | ain promised a copy from the Commissionor, laut have not received it. 


a7 


proportion, in which case there would be an excess of more than half the 
present amount of revenue, that is, of more than 5,000,000). sterling. I 
could go into details to prove the probable accuracy of this calculation 
put ay are not here Peanizeds oe is, however, evident that if such 
‘alculation is a just one, it would afford am le security for a loa 
5,000, 0008. or 6,000,0001. or even 7,000,0000, or 8,000,001, Seine the 
revenue at this instant was not forestalled, I should say, therefore, that 
a loan to that amount could safely be made, providing the control 
promised as to expenditure was given, and the new organization as. to 
revenue was carried out; and providing also, that the Aan in question 
was applied strictly, in the first instance, to set the current revenue free, 
The next measure necessary is to get rid of the amount of pressing 
debts not yet in any way settled, and which naturally bear down public 
eredit, and prevent anything like regularity in accounts, The large 
amount of available State property at Constantinople alone, to say nothing 
of mines, forests, and public lands elsewhere, would, if properly mana a 
more especially if Europeans were allowed to purchase, suffice to deal 
with this incumbrance. Indeed, nothing would be casier than to place 
such property in the hands of respectable merchants here charged to 
estimate its value, and to issue notes, payable at certain epochs, so as to 
allow time for its sale; these notes, representing half the property or two- 
thirds of the property to be sold, could be at once Helvered to those 
creditors whose claims at this instant can neither be denied nor satisfied. 
A large sum, calculated at 5,000,0001. sterling, might alse be got by 
converting what is called © Vakoof” property, which only descends, as | 
have said in former despatches, in a direct line, into “ Multk” pro nerty 
which the proprietor ean will away and dispose of at pleasure. soe 
Anew and considerable resource to the State is here opened, and the 
inereased duty upon imports, now under consideration, would also offer 
another, But such operations require some little time, and cannot be 
effectually carried out except by competent persons and with a certain 
capital in hand, since with anticipated receipts and increasing demands 
the Government is living at present, Irom aay to-day, on usurious loans 
of small amount. ‘This is why [ say a loan is imperatively required. 1 
will explain the present state of the capital in order to show, indeed, how 
much such a loan is a matter of immediate necessity. su 
The enormous interest granted of late by the Turkish Government 
has induced all capitalists in Constantinople to lend money to that 
Government, and in most cases the large capitalists have borrowed from 
the small ones in order to complete the sums required; added Lo this, the 
contracts with the Government, or the debts with the palace have 
equally concerned the large and small contractors and tradesmen, so that 
it is no exaggeration to say that almost all the capitalists and tradesmen 
in this city are at this time creditors, directly or indirectly, of the State, 
On the other hand, the poorer class depends almost entirely on the 
capitalists and tradesmen, and thus from the highest to the lowest there 
is a general ery on the Government for money, and a general discontent 
at not getting it. ‘The army and navy are likewise dissatisfied and badly 
off, and the funds coming in which should go to their support are already 
in the hands of parties to whom they are given for old accounts, It ‘i 
impossible that things should go on long in this manner, and the question 
is whether the English Government, in the present emergency could or 
would, alone or with other Governments, euarantee 1 loan which should 
be made on specific resources, and with the most clear and binding 
conditions as to its application. | 
My opinion, I confess, is that it would be both safe and politic under 
present circumstances to give ‘Turkey such aid on such conditions, and 
at all events, | really see no other possible means of preventing, ere long, 
a erash of which it is difficult to foresee the consequences. ny 
{ know the practical difficulties which lie in the way of such a 
guarantee, on our part, as that to which I have been alluding. ‘Those 
difficulties may, perhaps, be insuperable, but at all events I have deemed 
it advisable to place the case which suggests it strongly before your Lord- 
ship. l understand, moreover, confidentially that some negotiation as to

with it, if it approved the terms and stipulations on which the said loan 
was mace. | 

Nothing, 1 repeat, should be given without the clearest understanding 
as to the measures it would promote, and the security on which it should 
rest; but, aecompanied by proper measures and resting on proper security, 
an advance at this moment would not only benelit Turkey who received, 
and the foreign capitalist who made it: such adyance would also be an 
especial benehit to reer commerce, and British commerce in particular 
in Turkey—w commerce which will be crippled and ruined if the present 
state of things long continue. ; 

My conviction is that a suitable sum of money properly applied might 
save everything here at this time, and that when it was once known that 
the Government was relieved in some degree with respect to the immediate 
pressure on its finances, the other perils that environ it would melt away. 
But, on the other nand, unless some such supply on tolerably reasonable 
terms is found speedily (although, as I have always said and believed, 
these people do rise above expectation in a great emergency), | am bound 
to say that | deem a very serious catastrophe may any day occur, and 
take ie surprise those who have not set a just value on the eritical circum- 
stances of the present juncture. 

I have, &e.

No. 23. 


Lord J, Russell to Sir H. Bulwer. 

(No. 201.) 
Sir, Foreign Office, July 3, 1860. 

WITH reference to your Excellency’s despatch No. 334 of the 16th 
ultimo, respecting the present unsatisfactory state of the Torkish Empire, 
and the urgent necessity which exists for the Porte to obtain a loan in 
order to meet her immediate wants, I have to state to you, with reference 
to my telegram of the 22nd ultimo, that although Her Majesty's Govern- 
ment regret to learn the difficulty under which the Turkish Government 
labours, yet they must decline to give any guarantee to assist the Porte in 
raising the money she requires, for no security can be given that the same 
waste and profusion which has hitherto characterized the Sultan's expen- 
diture will not occur again, 

Lam, &c. 
(Signed) J. RUSSELL. 


No, 24. 
Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell, —( Received July 14.) 

(No, 879.) 
My Lord, Therapia, July 4, 1860, 

THE. situation of things here remains much the same. The French 
Ambassador and Russian Minister entertain great apprehensions as to an 
outbreak. Such an event, no doubt, 18 possible, but I do not think it 
probable; at all events such a crisis is not yet, Tam disposed to say, 
arrived. | 

The fact is, that my colleagues judge matters a little too much, 
perhaps, in this country as they met judge them, under similar circum- 
stances, ina country with different vabits and feelings. 

The army and navy and functionaries are three months in arrear, and 


® Goldsmidt. 


Py a ee ee BILE 
ait pel Seat BST, 


gh et aS 


38) 


Constantinople is a city of persons employed in the service of the State, 
and of these who live by them. This is serious; but it is not, perhaps, 
quite so serious as it appears; for delays and irrezularities in payments, 
here described, are of immemorial usage, and as, somehow or other, the 
Porte always ends by paying up arrears, people usually murmur, but have 
patience, 

It is, however, a condition of things producing natural clisquietude, 
and which, in any moment of excitement, would be favourable to, if it did 
not of itself actually produce, insurrection. 

What [am endeavouring to do is to get the Turkish Government, 
without relying on foreign loans, to make a great internal effort, and thus, 
and by a fair explanation of its difficulties, to deal with, at all events, a 
large portion of its present embarrassments, which would render arrange- 
ments as to the rest, and especially as to finding a present su pply of 
money, more easy. But, at the same time, if acertain sum of money could 
at once be found for present and urgent necessities, it wou ld be of great 
use, and might possibly prevent great calamities. 

The present crisis has arisen in this manner :— 

The Government was certainly preparing the means for a better 
financial system, and for dealing with the difficulties which stood in tts 
way, but, in the meanwhile, the old system continued, ‘This consisted in 
borrowing by anticipation on the incoming revenue ; and as, ‘when. the 
revenue came in, the parties who had lent it once lent it again, things 
went on, though at a great and absurd loss. 

When, however, the demand of Russia and the constant announece- 
ment of projected insurrections broke down all confidence here, the 
machinery | have been describing broke dewn also; nobody would make 
fresh advances to take up those already made, and the Government thus 
finds itself with an empty exchequer, and its revenue for the next five or 
six months pledged. I have stated this already in my despatch No. 
of , but T repeat it here, because the position in question 
cannot be too clearly explained, or too forcibly insisted on. 

If the Porte keeps its engagements, which chiefly concern English 
-houses, as to the bills given on the revenue, it will be in the difficult situa- 
tion of employing soldiers and functionaries to collect funds which are at 
onee handed over to others, whilst they, already in arrears, will receive 
nothing from those funds. 

[t is not impossible that in such a case astate of disorder would ensue 
rendering the collection of all revenue impossible, 

On the other hand, supposing the Porte, seeing these dilliculties, seizes 
the revenue (for her army and her functionaries) which she has pledged, 
and on whieh bills have been drawn on London, and are now in circulation ; 
this may create, not only here, but at home, very serious embarrassments 
and losses, which, after all, would be losses of English capital, 

What would be required to escape from this dilemma is, that some 

arty should advance for four or five years the sum which is now payable 
in four or five months. This sum, say 2,500,000/., could be guaranteed by 
the very same securities on certain branches of the revenue which are 
now allotted to the present creditors; and upon the whole i think there 
would be much more certainty in providing for the service of the State, 
and spreading its obligations over a reasonable time, than in exacting the 
fulfilment of those obligations by arresting the service of the State and 
erowding the payment which has to be made into a period which renders 
such payment almost impossible. 

‘What T think, then, most desirable is a loan which would pay off these 
delegations on the next few months’ revenue; that need not be large; 
what would render it easier, in fact. in my opinion easy, would be measures 
relieving the finances from other pressure, and such measures 1 am doing 
my utmost to enforce; but the one main point, the debt on the immediate 
incoming revenue, is the point that cannot be lost sight of, and must in 
some way or other be met. 

[think I could satisfy any capitalist, if he were here, that a loan to the 
extent deseribed might be safely made; at all events no time should be 
lost in seeing if anything can be done respecting, it.

those great efforts which have in other times distinguished their nation

The only way to deal with this state of things is, I think, boldly and

A Commission of respectable Europeans and Ottoman subjects might 
have assigned to them the publie property for sale, with the power of 
creating a loan on it, if found convenient; Europeans being allowed to 
purchase such property.* 

A bureau should be established composed of Europeans and Ottomans 
who should examine all claims, and give to those recognized as valid an 
order on the said Commission, which order should be received in payment 
of Prepersy if the holder wished to purchase, or should be paid to him as 
the public property ts sold; or should be settled, if a loan is effected and 
there is enna in hand, at once, There might, indeed, be various ways 
of managing this transaction ; but the great thing is to have the guarantee, 
und to let people see their interests are to be taken care of, and that the 
Government is endeavouring to do all it can to accomplish that object, 

Another bureau should be charged with collecting debts due to the 
Porte, which should he paid to the Commission charged with paying the 
debts of the Porte. 

The 5,000,000 serghis might be consolidated into a stock bearing 
interest of 7 or 8 per cent, As this is a fund in part created to pay the 
expenses and arrears of army and navy, the Sultan might resign (which 
would be an act both popular and usefal) from his Civil List a sum consti. 
tuting the interest on this stock, and providing by a sinking fund for ‘its

My Lord, Therapia, July 4, 1860. 

WITH reference to proceedings best auapies for the future adminis- 
tration and progress of Turkey, I should say that if I rightly understand 
M. de Thouvenel’s views as given in Lord Cowley’s No. 731 of June Id to 
your Lordship, I fear that my own would differ a good deal from them: 
Ist, as to the expediency of sending Commissioners of Inspection into the 
Provinces ; 2ndly, as to that of employing Cliistians on those Commis- 
sions; and rdly, as to centralizing the inspection of which I speak, in 
some Wee or other, at Constantinople, 

I will even go so far as to say that not only is this the best, it is the 


only, mode in which anything can be done for the amelioration of the 
Provinces, 


The evils of a Pasha’s government al 


vend much upon this funetionary 
being at one fixed spot, and subject only to the influences of that spot. 


It is, moreover, far more easy to find five or six men of character than 
thirty or forty, These five or six men travelling through certain divisions 
of the Empire with extraordinary powers, and doing prompt justice, 
having a few simple rules to guide them as to principles, would invigorate 
the Turkish authority; and by not staying in the Provinces, or inhabiting 
any particular spot in them, would not fall under proyvineial influence. 
That Christians should be attached to them, | look upon us essential. 
These men may not be high-minded, but a Greek will speak more clearly 
to a Greek as to his grievances than to a Turk; and the Greek who 
receives his countrymans complaint, however submissive outwardly to 
the Turk, feels for the national grievance, and will, in one way or other, 
bring it before the Turkish superior. Besides, it is little by little that 
you may in this way reform your Greek race itself, 

The notion of a Reform Committee composed of Ambassadors 1s 
hardly practical, They would rarely agree as to remedies, though they 
might as to the grievances. My difference with M.de Thouvenel, if there is 
a difference here, is a proof of this. hey would, moreover, be apt to batter 
down the Turkish authority, and confuse the Turkish mind. [ do not 
mean by this to exelude their influence, but I think it must rather bea 
discretional than a positive one. 

Let the inspection | mention be created with the Central authority 
here, and with that Central authority all the Representatives would from 
time to time confer as to reports that reached them. 

It is not, as | said lately, nicely framed and complicated laws that 
are required; it is justice in the simplest form, and in its universally 
recognised principles, that has to be established: the only novelty for 
which a special instruction should be given being that it should be equal 
justice between Mussulman and Rayah that is now required, 

‘The Commissioner encouraged when he punishes, as well as when he 
praises, would effect this, or if he could not, no one can, 


here might affect English commerce and English interests, Binge bere 
“4 can be little doubt that these would suffer immediately relate ae ‘ee 
“| extent of that sum which, advanced at this moment on a sufficient security

or Great Council might well form part of the system here sketched out: 
members of this tribunal might, indeed, be emploved in the way deseribed, 


| as Commissioners. But the whole plan wants consideration, In order to

This opinion as to the troops of the eapital is probably well founded ; 
but lam sorry to say that the reports lately received as to the spirit 
amongst the troops generally throughout the Empire are not satisfactory, 
and unless something can be done to satisfy the arrears of pay, their 
mutiny or their disbandment is a possible event. Moreover, were a 
mutiny of this kind to take place in any particular spot, it is difficult to 
say to what extent the example might be contagious; but, at the same 
time, it is not unlikely that Europeans estimate too lightly the patience 
under grievance and the established feeling of respect to the Sultan's 
authority, which will, under any but the most extraordinary circumstances, 
characterize the Turkish soldiery. 

There are limits, however, to all endurance, ‘The Sovereign and the 
Ministry are personally unpopular, and to keep the conduct of affairs 
tolerably ean amidst so much to agitate and trouble them, some means 
of at least satisfying the pecuniary causes for dissatisfaction becomes each 
day more essential. One simple means of dealing with a certain portion 
of the present difficulties would be a eeneral bankruptey ; and a declara- 
tion on the part of the Porte that it cannot maintain its obligations to 
alienate the current revenue, the pledging of which has been lately the 
foundation of a variety of usurious loans. 

ft would be difficult, perhaps, to put too strongly the disastrous 
effects which this would have on ‘Turkish commerce and credit: but at 
the same time, if money is not got in some way or other, and the class of 
creditors L am alluding to do not themselves offer to enter into some 
composition, it is difficult to suppose that the Government will be long 
able to continue to employ functionaries to collect taxes, and soldiers to 
protect the collection of such taxes, neither of these receiving the 
payments overdue—to which, under an ordinary state of things, such taxes 
would as a matter of course be applied. 

In the meantime [ inclose a communication T have received from 
Major Cox, and which ts much in conformity with what | have generally 
stated as to the condition of European Turkey; and also a despatch from 
Mr. Mayers at Rustchuk, inclosing the orders that were given by Ismail 
Pasha, and which are a satisfactory proof of the energy of that officer 
now commanding at Widdin; and (1 would add) of the conduct and 
sentiments of the Grand Vizier, whose instructions he is carrying out. 

His Highness Kaprisli Pasha’s absence from the capital is, however, 
a great inconvenience, and this inconvenience is increased by Fuad Pasha’s 
departure; for amongst the many amiable and noble qualities of Aali 
Pasha, energy and decision cannot he reckoned, He sees the perils of the 
situation clearly; but he sees at the same time so clearly the danger of 
every remedy that can be applied to them, that his action is paralyzed (if 
| may so speak) by his intellect, and every evil which has finally to be 
encountered goes on day by day increasing in extent and in force. 

My course would be to bring Kaprisli Pasha at once to Constan- 
tinople, and to name Omer Pasha at once at the head of the Turkish army 
in the European provinces, and to continue the work of the Grand Vizier 
by the means of Commissioners, who might be chosen from men of the 
most energy and ability the service of the State can command, 

But the Seraskier and others now in office oppose the appointment 
af Omer Pasha; the French Ambassador and, I understand, M. 'Thouvenel 
contest his military capacity; and on the other hand, lam told that the 
return of the Grand Vizier will be the signal of troubles and insurrections, 
which his presence is calenlated to prevent; and it is therefore impossible, 
under auch eircumstanees, to take upon myself the responsibility of 
enforcing measures of which [shall not be able to direct the execution, 
and as to the result of which L cannot, moreover, be positive, 

| have not, however, disguised my belief that the one thing now 
necessary here is the capacity of executing, as well as of forming, some 
ceneral plan both as to the political and financial condition of the country ; 
end that | also deem it unwise to leave a man like Omer Pasha, who, 
whatever his defects, is liked and trusted by the soldiery, and who has at 
least the talent of putting and keeping an army together, out of employ- 
ment, when there is no other General in the Empire whose qualities have 


a5 yt" he Ta = ir en cr fie F i : rts tk we ee — sts <4 


47 


been tested, or whose reputation is general, and when at any moment 
may arise an emergency in which everything may depend upon the 
conduct of the army. 
I have spoken seriously on the subject both to the Sultan and Aali 
Pasha. 
[ have, &c. 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

[nelosure 1 in No. 28. 
Sir H. Bulwer to Mr. Dalyell. 


My dear Mr. Dalyell, Therapia, July 26, 1860. 
[ DO not give you formal instructions, because if you can do any 


good, it will be by talking in the sense in which | have talked to you, 
rather than by following any precise rules T might lay down for you. — 

[ cannot indeed, at this place, say precisely what is doing at Salonica, 
nor give you, consequently, precise instructions, But my sentiment is, 
that danger is to be averted by mutual confidence, and will be brought on 
by mutual fear. ‘The secret meetings and precautions of one party are 
rendered more perilous by secret meetings and arming of another. And 
it is always to be borne in mind that mischievous persons are abroad 
spreading alarm. 

It is not natural to suppose that, all of a sudden, the Turks wish to 
murder the Christians ; if they do so, it must be from sudden appre- 
hensions: increase their apprehensions, you increase the peril. 

Such are my general axioms, which may possibly be modified by 
exceptional and local circumstances; these the local Agents must judge 
of, Speak to Mr. Wilkinson; see M. Tissot, if desirable; form a general 
opinion, and return. 

Tell Mr. Wilkinson that I have telegraphed to Athens to send a ship, 
if one is there; and Husni Pasha is ordered to return to Salonica, If 
Mehemet Pasha should be relieved summarily, telegraph. 

[ have written on what | think the probable supposition, viz,, that 
the authorities will do their best to preserve order, and that the Mussul- 
mans are not suddenly seized with a desire to kill all the Christians 
without cause, But of course should it seem desirable, it will be well to 
let the authorities be told confidentially that if any Mussulman outrage 
does happen, every party implicated in it shall be made a terrible example 
of: that the Pashas who fail in their duty will be inevitably shot; and 
that the city itself will be probably destroyed. On the other hand, the 
Christians should be quietly let to know that if they provoke a confliet, 
they will have to pay the penalty of it, and that we shall as little protect 
‘asurrection on one side as lawless violence on the other. | 

[ have, &e. 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER, 


Inclosure 2 in No, 28, 
Mr, Dalyell to Sir H. Bulwer, 


Sir, Therapia, August 4, 1860, 

[ HAVE to report to your Excellency that on arriving at Salonica on 
the 29th ultimo, 1 found that, although a panic had certainly prevailed 
public confidence was already restored. : 

It seemed to me that my remaining for any time might re-excite 
alarm, as had I proceeded to sift stories which appeared to have had little 
if any, foundation, it might (and in this country probably would have 
been supposed) that some important discovery had been made, when, in 
fact, there would appear to have been little to discover. [I however said 
to Mr. Wilkinson, who (if f may express an opinion on the subject) seems 


48 


to me to have acted throughout with much judgment, that I thought a 
report from him, a little later, as to the real causes of public apprehension, 
especially in respect to how far foreign intrigue may, or may not, have 
had anything to do with the matter, would be acceptable to your Excel- 
leney. 

| proceed to state shortly what I did, and what | was myself uble to 
ascertain. 

I met on board the steamer in which J went to Salonica Ahmet 
Rassim Effendi, a gentleman employed in the Department of Foreign 
Affairs here, who had, at a few hours’ notice, been sent by his Highness 
Aali Pasha to inquire into the correctness of the reports as to the ree 
state of Salonica, communicated by the French Embassy. Ahmet Effendi 
seemed to me intelligent, and sensible of the necessity of vigilance and 
firmness: he has himself property at Salonica. 

On my arrival, I placed myself first. in communication with 
Mr, Wilkinson, and then with M. ‘Tissot, the French Consul. The latter 
rentleman (for whom the French Ambassador had, as your Excellency is 
aware, furnished me with letters, and by whom | was most courteously 
received) told me that he had recently made three or four journeys into 
the interior (I gathered from his conversation), with instructions to ascer- 
tain the feeling at present subsisting between the Mussulman and Christian 
populations. 

Ifthe French Consul’s reports had been approves by his Government, 
[ can understand his mind being rather disposed to accept, in this 
instance, what on his own showing are rumours, well-founded possibly, 
but still ramours, as established facts, 

He informed me that ‘meetings of Turks had taken place in the 
fortress,” at least that “he was so informed by a Mussulman in whom he 
placed confidence.” Merchants whom Laupecdusntly saw, and who, from 
their every-day business relations with the Turks, are likely to have been 
accurately informed, doubted entirely the fact of any such meetings as 
deseribed by the French Consul having taken place. 

M. Tissot also stated to me that Mehemed Pasha, the Commandant 
of the troops, had expressed his intention to remain neutral in the event of 
the ‘Turks attacking the Christians.” He added that the ‘Turks then 
applied to Yussuf Pasha, a proprietor of some influence in the neighbour- 
hood, for advice, “ by whom they are dissuaded from any violent course ;” 
and that Mehemed Pasha then withdrew his adhesion. 

[ heard by no means a favourable account of Mehemed Pasha; and 
would even suggest to your Excellency that his removal, a few months’ 
henee (not at present, for removing him at this moment would only 
confirm in the eves of the population the existence of rumours which they 
now disbelieve), might be attended with good results, But the French 
Consul has no proof, positive or circumstantial, that Mchemed Pasha used 
menacing language ; and both the Military and Civil authorities, though, 
perhaps, not quite so quickly as they might have done, employed measures 
calculated to reasure the population. 

I may as well mention, confidentially, to your Excellency that 
Mr, Wilkinson told me there is a private grudge between the French 
Consul and Mehemed Pasha, which, Dera e, did not dispose the former 
to form an unduly charitable opinion of Mehemed Pasha's presumed 
proceedings, pce 

The merchants whom I saw said that they had been much alarmed 
by the French Consul’s assertion that “there was positively a plot;” but 
that as soon as patrols were established by the authorities they regained 
confidence: also that ‘certainly the panic was most perceptible out of 
business hours”? and that, in fact, commercial affairs between the Turks 
and the Christians had gone on as much as usual, 1 endeavoured to 
impress on this gentleman, and, indeed, generally, in the words of your 
Excellency’s instruction to me, that “danger is to be averted by mutual 
confidence, and will be brought on by mutual fear.” 

In our interview with the Turkish authorities, and indeed throughout, 
Myr. Wilkinson and myself acted | may say jointly, and although | took 
eare to assure the French Consul that my instructions emanated simply 


49 


from my own Embassy I had the pleasure of obtaining (in Mr. Wilkinson's 
presence) M. Tissot’s entire concurrence in the tenor of the language we 
proposed to hold. 

Mr. Wilkinson and myself called on the Commandant of the town 
(Mehemed Pasha whom I have already mentioned,) and on the Kaimakam 
(or Lieutenant-Governor, acting in the stead of Husni Pasha, who was 
absent, but will by this time have returned to Salonica). . 

To Mehemed Pasha, after expressing our satisfaction that things had 
assumed a more reassuring aspect, | said that the languaye which the 
Porte had voluntarily held to your Excellency as to the determination of 
the Sultan to punish his officers in Syria who had failed in their duty, 
would be, it was hoped, a warning to all ‘Turkish officers invested with power 
and confidence. 1 considered that it would be your Excellency’s wish that 
1 should make such observations unmistakeably, but with great courtesy ; 
and they were perfectly well received. 

To the Kaimakam, the principal civil authority, I held generally the 
same tone, requesting him to impress on Husni Pusha that not only in 
Salonica, but everywhere within his jurisdiction, we consider the Pasha, 
and the Pasha alone, absolutely and directly responsible for anything that 
may oceur; and T especially mentioned Serres, an important town in the 
Pashalic, where the Greek and Turkish elements in the population are 
nearly equally balanced, and where the Mudir® (Subordinate Loeal 
Governor) is stated to adopt aline independent of his superior and irritating 
to the Christians, . 

I may say to your Excellency that Husni Pasha himself wonld seem 
to be, though a man of a certain ability, vain and superticial, and I suspect 
that some of the dissatisfaction on the part of the Turks at Salonica may 
have arisen from the feeling that the Pasha, in order to court the Consuls, 
is disposed to prefer Greeks to the Local Turkish Beys, in his disposal of 
petty places and sources of emolument. : 

As to the origin and causes of the public appreliensions, which 
certainly very generally prevailed, 1 would add that although fam disposed 
to think that the panie was exaggerated, and although good and not bud 
relations habitually exist at Salonica between all classes of the population, 
there were incidents which may have served to excite, even if they did not 
partially justify, the alarm. 

In the first place the news from Syria had produced a feeling of dread, 
a shrinking from the Turks, and ameine together on the part of the 
Christians, and very probably in some degree a counter-sentiment on the 
part of the Turks, 

In the middle of this, some church bells taken from a number stamped 
with the Russian Eagle, and which arrived at Salonica some months since 
(it is stated as a present from the Russian Government to Greck Churches 
in the town and neighbourhood—a fact in regard to which | have requested 
Mr. Wilkinson to inquire and report to your Excellency), were cleared 
from the custom-house, and put up ina Greek Chureh within, or on the 
very limit of, the Turkish quarter, and close to a mosque. These bells 
were rung at the hour that the muezzin calls from the minaret the Mussul- 
mans to prayer; a proceeding which naturally excited considerable 
dissatisfaction among the Turks, 

Tt is said that the Mussulmans sent a deputation to the Turkish 
authorities, and expressed their determination, if this were continued, to 
enter the church and destroy the ornaments. 

The Greek Bishop, on the plea of alarm, ordered the ringing of the 
bells in all the churches of Salonica to be discontinued. 

The Turkish Kaimakam (Lieutenant-Governor) requested the Bishop 
to resume the ringing of the bells, stating that he (the Kaimakam) would 
be answerable that nothing should occur, 

The Bishop declined, 

In the meantime, it is stated (this, also, Mr. Wilkinson will, if possible,

Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell—(Received August 17.) 
(No. 490.) 
My Lord, Therapia, August 8, 1860. 


DURING the last two months I have, as your Lordship is aware, 
been constantly engaged with discussions relative to the Turk: hh 
finances. nor 

The ignorance which | must presume prevails in the Turkish Govern- 
ment itself, and the differences which have occurred in the information | 
have from time to time received from it, have formed one of the greatest 
difficulties | have had to contend with; sincea plan which was good under 
one state of circumstances, and under one amount of debt, might not be 
so, the circumstances and the amount of the debt to be dealt with bein 
different; and 1 say this, because during the last conversation ! had with 
Rushdi and Aali Pashas, many facts presented themselves in a different 
point of view from that in which they had appeared previously, and many 
statements were quite different from those which had been formerly 
made. are 

The amount of the general debt seems, however (as | imagined 
would prove the ease), pretty much what [ have stated it to be, but the 
amount of each particular debt is by no means the same. What. stands 
out from it all, however, are these two facts: the greatest portion of debts 
that now press upon the 'reasury may be consolidated and a fund 
provided to pay the interest, and a sinking fund; but there remains 
afterwards about 7,500,000/, sterling which the Government says 
could be paid with something less than 6,000,000L, and for dealing 
with which a sum of money is urgently required. Of this, 5,000,000L 
would be to relieve the current revenue for the next seven or eight 


months, which is now handed over to capitalists as 2 fuarantee for sums 
advanced and spent; the Government remaining in this way actually 
with its expenses undiminished, ancd indeed by the present state of 
circumstances increased, and altogether without the ordinary and natural 
means to meet those expenses. 

The remaining 2,500,000/. is for debts of various kinds, and 
especially for arrears due to the army and navy, and the different 
functionaries throughout the Empire. | 

It is clear that this condition of things cannot be left as it is; at the 
same time, never having been able to fix the Government, notwithstanding 
all its wants, all its exigencies, its constant demands and our unceasing 
conversations, to any specific project or engagement, | addressed Aali 
Pasha the other day the inclosed letter, embodying therein what | 
conceived was the substance and intention of one or two of your 
Lordship’s telegraphic despatches, and if the answer prove perfectly 
satisfactory, 1 think we might safely advise any foreign capitalists to 
enter upon the loan that would be required; but Aali Pasha is so timid 
that 1 doubt about his taking any effectual measures with respect to the 
State property, orthe property called “ vacoul,” or venturing, in short, 
upon any of those bold and decided steps which in extreme circumstances 
often constitute prudence. At all events, | have deemed it better to put 
on record this last effort, and also my general opinions. 

There can be no doubt that the best way of escaping from external 
Europenn influence would be to eniploy able Huropeans in the Turkish 
service; that the best way of practically executing new ideas would be to 
emp those who have been brought up since such ideas came into vogue ; 
and that this is not a moment when a statesman should falter as to the 
adoption of a course of policy so clearly required. But | own that after 
the experience | have lately had, | am somewhat disheartened; and that, 
at all events, as long as the present Administration lasts—and | confess 
[ should he puzzled to name a better—I shall, if pretty well convinced of 
its good intentions, never be certain of its resolute action, 

At the same time, bad or weak as it is, we have to deal with it, and 
with the situation which exists in connection with it; and whilst, on the 
one hand, [see no means of safety for either without a loan from Europe, 
on the other hand I know no stronger means for establishing a salutary 
authority over affairs than that which such a loan properly employed and 
guaranteed, might effect; whilst | think it not unlikely that many of those 
ens which men will not promise without being certain of a result, they 
would yet get courage to perform, if that result in the shape of five or six 
millions were before them clearly. 

I have, &e, 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

Str H. Bulwer to aloli Pasha. 


Therapia, ce Aoilt, 1860. 

MON Gouvernement ne peut pas garantir un emprunt a moins qu'll 
n’existe quelque combinaison générale parmi toutes les Grandes Puissances 
i cet effet, ot & Vheure qu'il est je sais rien d'une telle combigaison ow 
entente. Mais il s’énonce prét a favoriser un tel emprunt atiprés des 
grandes capitalistes pourvu qu'il voit ici une disposition d'adopter les 
mesures qui pourraient conduire, moyennant un emprunt, & un avenir 
financier solide, 

Or, les conditions sur lesquelles je pourrais conseiller le Gouverne- 
ment de Sa Majesté a s’y intéresser, sont— 

1. Que larrangement financier auquel cet emprunt sera appliqué 
soit complet, et détruise entidrement le systéme de prét A cours termes. 

2, Que toutes les dettes flottantes qu'on a l’intention de consolider 
aient leurs intéréts assurés par une garantie spéciale, 

P 


nature of the feelings of horror, resentment, and indignation which have 
been excited in this country by the atrocities lately committed in Syria. 
From the time of the Treaty of Kainardji of 1774 to the Treaty of 
Paris of 1856, Turkey was fettered in her internal government by her 
engagements to Russia. By repeated Treaties and Conventions, by armed 


interference at one time, and specious protection at another, the Christians 
of the Turkish Empire were made the subjects of the Czar quite as much 
asof the Ottoman Porte. But a few ellorts were wanting to break the last 
links which bound the people of the Principalities, Bulgaria, and Bosnia, 
to the Sultan, and make him cither a vassal of Russia or an Asiatic 
Power. 

At this time Great Britain and France interfered. The siege of 
Sebastopol and the provisions of the Treaty of Paris converted the exelu- 
sive protection of Christians by Russia into an engagement, general in its 
nature, respectful to the Sultan in its form, by whieh it was hoped the 
lives and properties of the Christian subjects of the Porte would be 
ruaranteed, and their condition gradually improved, — 

It was therefore with a shock of amazement, as well as of horror, that 
the intelligence of the massacres of Hasbeya, Zahlé, Deir-el-Kamar, and 
Damascus, was received in Great Britain, [It was not that a barbarous 
Mahometan or Pagan tribe had murdered by wholesale other tribes with 
which they were at enmity—it was not the utter inhumanity which marked 
and deepened the shade of these murders—it was that ‘Turkish Pashas 
and Bevs, chosen by the Sultan to govern and protect his subjects, were 
evidently abettors and favourers of these massacres. Among these 
Kourshid Pasha was one of the most prominent, The troops communded 
by him either looked on in apathy, or actually participated in the worst 
ofthese massacres. Wuad Pasha, sent on aspecial mission to inquire and to 
punish, proposed actually to leave this man in the administration of the 
Government whose powers he perverted, and it was not till a British 
Admiral interfered that he consented to deprive the Pasha of his authority.! 
Achmet Pasha at Damascus gave full sway to the dissolute mob which 
murdered and plundered Christians. Othman Bey, at Deir-el-Kamar, did 
still worse; he disarmed the Christians, and then admitted their murderers 
to slay the unarmed multitude, 

When the news of these events, even in imperlect reports, reached 
this country, men naturally said, “The blood anid treasure of Great 
Britain and France were poured out freely to maintain the independence 
of the Sultan, ‘They asked no territory, no exclusive privileges in return ; 
they asked only that the Christian subjects of the Porte might be treated 
with humanity, to the great advantage of the Sultan himself, An 
outbreak of fanatical Mahometans we could well understand, But this 
treachery, brutality, and cruelty, on the part of persons selected by the 
Sultan himself to govern his best Provinces, shows cither some deep 
design to exterminate the Christians, or an unheard-of degree of weakness 
and apathy at Constantinople, or an amount of venality and corruption 
which it is difficult to eredit.” 

You must not be surprised that such feelings should be excited, and 
such reflections made; nor would it be of any use to conceal from the 
Porte that either the whole system of Ottoman Government must be 
replaced hy one founded on integrity ancl justice, or the Sultan must 
prepare himself for the abandonment of his cause by his best and most 
persevering allies, | 

lam, &c. 
(Signed) J. RUSSELL, 


No. Sl, 


Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell —( Received Seplember 2.) 


(No; 517.) 
My Lord, Therapia, August 20, 1860, 


AS a good deal of confusion seems to prevail in public opinion with 
respect to the state of Eastern affairs of late years, the origin and nature 


. — a ro * - , 

= = . a * Af wt = : = -_ ta - " 

re w ” wa bil fie weil Art Se bie ere oe \; cs + 
* Sat ; 


ae Terme, 


a7 


of the Hatti-Humayoun, the position of the different European Powers 
and the conduct which has been and is to be pursued by Her Majesty’s 
Embassy at Constantinople, | think it not amiss to put before your Lord- 
ship clearly the views which I entertain upon these matters, and the facts 
with which those views are connected, 

Previous to 1840, the two great influences at Constantinople were 
those of Russia on the one side, which had established her power over the 
Turkish Councils by the Treaty of Unkiar Skelessi, and of Great Britain 
on the other; which was known not only as essentially interested in main- 
taining the Sultan's authority but also as having, through her great naval 
position in the Mediterranean, the most adequate means for repelling any 
Bassai aggression, which it was then thought would have the Black Sea 
lor its starting-point, 

The influence of England was, at the period to which | am now 
referring, immeasurably increased by the Syrian campaign, for through 
her means at that time not only the projects of Mehemet Ali for the 
independence of Egypt were controlled and checked, but the Province of 
Syria was also taken from him and restored to the Porte. 

‘This event gave, [ may say, almost unlimited authority to the advice 
of Her Majesty’s Ambassador, and as a proof of that authority, and in 
return for the assistance which had been rendered by a Christian Power, 
the decree called the Edict of Gulhané was issued at Lord Ponsonby’s 
instigation: that decree granting equal rights to all classes of the Sultan’s 
subjects, and containing, in theory at least, the foundation for a new 
system of government, My immediate predecessor, Lord Stratford, 
inherited the position which this edict, and the events which had produced 
it, aecasioned, Tle found at Constantinople a very remarkable man who, 
with great defects, had great energy and tact, a certain knowledge of 
Europe, and an immense ambition. This man, Reschid Pasha, attached 
himself naturally to our Embassy, then all-powerful, as a means of 
attaining his own views; and Her Majesty’s Ambassador could not but 
find it a great convenience to have a man like Reschid Pasha acting on 
cordial and unreserved terms of intimacy with him. Up to the period of 
the late war with Russia Lord Stratford and Reschid Pasha together 
were thus able, except during trifling intervals, to carry things pretty 
well their own way; nor was the immense wealth, however corrupt its 
sources, which Reschid Pasha accumulated during his continuance in 
office, without its effect in maintaining his influence in a capital where 
much, T need hardly say, is effected by largesses, and what might else- 
where be not improperly termed bribes. 

But a new state of things, which has not always been sufficiently 
considered, commenced with the war to which Lam alluding, Previously 
few people had imagined that any large European army, without it was 
the army of Russia, would interfere in the affairs of the Kast, and this 
added much to the consideration which was attached to our maritime 
force, 

At the war a new feature in affairs presented itself, The French not 
only sent a much larger army than our own to the Crimea, but this army 
created for itself a great reputation; and, as our navy had not any oppor- 
tunity of showing its superiority over the navy of our ally, the impression 
left was certainly one which added greatly to the respect and fear felt 
for the French name, and diminished in thus much the almost exclusive 
respect which had been previously paid to our own, One of the first 
symptoms of this change was the decrease of Reschid Pasha’s power. 
The understanding which had existed between this statesman and the 
British Embassy was accompanied, as I have said, by many advantages, 


but it had also the disadvantage of uniting together all the younger men, 


of ability in Turkey who were aspirants to power, or who were not willing 
to submit to the complete supremacy of eschid: these became in this 
way arrayed against Reschid and against the British Ambassador, and 
they selected for their patron the French Ambassador. A struggle 
commenced with alternate success, but in which Reschid’s rivals must be 
eonsidered on the whole as victorious, since, notwithstanding all his 
abilities, influence, and reputation, he was on several oceasions driven

=f be es 


‘a: =
