ro 


ob 


from office, and only regained it by some violent effort, which made his 
tenure of power, whenever he repossessed himself of it, feeble and insecure. 
His death ended the contest, 

I arrived here at that time. Had I been disposed to pursue the 
previous policy of Lord Stratford | should have committed an anachronism, 
for the grounds on which that policy had been based and supported no 
longer existed, 

The position of France, the position of Great Britain were changed, 
and Reschid Pasha had left no successor. ‘The only course, therelore, 
that was open to me was to conciliate these whom | found administering 
affairs, to convinee them there was no personal hostility towards them on 
the part of Her Majesty’s Representative; and, instead of continuing any 
eonflict with M. de Mhouver) to endeavour to carry on, without lowering 
the just pretensions of my own Government and country, affairs here on a 
friendly understanding with him. 

The results of the Peace, moreover, had in no small degree affected the 
reneral diplomatic state of things here. ‘This Embassy and that Embassy 
had no longer altogether an isolated line of policy. A sort of Couneil of the 
Five European States had been created with respect to Kastern affairs ; 
and it was necessary to act in such a manner, if anything was to be done, 
as would ensure the concurrence of my colleagues, disputes with whom, 
unless imperatively called for, were to he avoided. 

| give this sort of sketch because in it must be sought the clue af my 
eonduet: ner have | yet said all, 

As lone as Russia had a large military establishment in the Black 
Sea, with which she flattered herself that she could at any time reach the 
Turkish capital, and as long as her position here was one hiehly gratifying 
to her vanity, if it did not perfectly satisfy her ambition, her conduct was 
not likely to be fretful or restless, nor had she any stron, motive for 
attempting to excite insurrection amongst the Rayah population, The 
destruction of her flect, and the great blow given to her moral authority 
hy the events of the last few years, has caused the Cabinet of St. Peters- 
burgh to be at once mortified and dissatistied, and it was thus led to seek 
by the only means yet left ro it to regain its former ascendancy, and to 
annihilate the consequences of its late defeats. The course | allude to is 
clear. It consisted in creating troubles in the heart of the ‘Turkish 
Empire itself, An additional motive, therefore, was given to Her Majesty's 
Giovernment and Her Majesty’s Ambassador for endeavouring, as far as 
was in their power, to strengthen the Government, and to support it 
against those insidious attacks which, under the plausible pretext of 
reform and protecting the Christian population, were likely to be, and 
were, as | think it is now sufficiently manifest, really directed against 
ii, 

The preceding narrative will explain with tolerable clearness the 
policy | have pursued up to this time, and the causes for that policy: and 
‘our Lordship will be able, after reading my observations, to determine 
hee far | have been hitherto right, and to point out any change you may 
think expedient in my line of conduct—a line of conduct which is to be 
considered as generally conciliatory towards the Representatives of other 
Powers, without being unduly subservient to them, and as tending to 
support the Ottoman Government as far as it is possible, without, how- 
ever being blind to its defects, or the necessity for its amelioration, and as 
indifferent to the patronage of any particular English party in ‘Turkey, 
and simply friendly to any individual, or set of individuals, who adminis- 
tered, with tolerable honesty and ability, public affairs. 

I should wish, however, now to SiLy i few words on the subject of the 
Hatti-Humayoun to whieh such reference is constantly made as a sort of 
eateh-word, but of whieh, notwithstanding, | venture to say nothing very 
necurate is generally known,

details; but in going more into detail, it has hardly arrived more definitely 
at any positive result,

intrigue, a new spirit may be infused into the Administration, But for 
this purpose the Sultan must be firm, the four Ministers must be stern in 
punishment, and determined to resist corruption in every shape.

No. 34. 
Lord J. Russell to Sir H. Bulwer. 


(No, 5134 

Sir, Foreign Office, September 19, 1860. 
UPON a fair and just consideration of the note of the Turkish 

Government addressed to your Excellency, and of which a copy is inclosed 

in your despatch No, 575 of the 4th instant, | have to observe :— - 
lat. ‘That the plan of leaving the Army and thie Civil Service unpaid 


=e 


63 


with a view to borrow for the purpose of supplying those necessary 
branches of administration seems a very improvident arrangement. 

2nd. That if there are debts to be paid off within short terms it seems 
desirable rather to use the credit of the Government for extending those 

jayments over terms of thirty or forty years, than to leave the Army anil 
Civil Service to be paid by loan. 

ard. If it isymeant by the term “ good offices. of England and France” 
to ask those Powers to guarantee a ‘Turkish loan, England on her part 
has determined not to accede to such a proposal. 

4th. It appears to Her Majesty’s Government that the first step is to 
ascertain what is the ordinary revenue of the Sublime Porte; the second, 
to provide that that revenue, and no more, is collected; the third, to ascer- 
tain what are the proper estimates for the Army, Navy, and Civil Service; 
the fourth, to see that the sum allotted to these branches of administration 
is honestly and economically applied. When these four things are done 
the credit of the Porte will no doubt stand hieher than it now does, and a 
loan, if required, may be raised in London and Paris on good terms. 

Sth. Her Majesty’s Government have no wish to send to Constanti- 
nople an English fancHonney to give unpalatable advice which will not be 
taken; but if the Turkish Government wish to examine their finances 
with a real purpose of reform, it may he possible, though difficult, to find 
an English gentleman who, with a French colleague, will be ready to 
become a Member of the Turkish Council of Finance. 

You may read this note to Safvet Effendi, and if desired give him a 
copy of it. 

lam, &e. 


(Signed) J. RUSSELL.

Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell,—( Received September 21.) 
(No, 608,) 
My Lard, Therapia, September 12, 1860, 

[ DULY received your Lordship's despatch No. 443 of August 25, 
and read a large portion of it yesterday to Aali Pasha. After begging 
to convey my grateful thanks for the obliging manner in which your 
Lordship does me the honour to speak of me personally in that despatch, 
I beg also to state that | most entirely and cordially agree in the senti- 
ments which your Lordship expresses, both as to the recent events in 
Syria, and the course which ought to be pursued by the Turkish Govern- 
ment wherever its rule extenils. 

Your Lordship will indeed easily recognise that my own views have 
been in strict conformity with those of your Lordship on these subjects. 

To say nothing of the constant and numerous other instances in which 
T have urged upon the Porte a course of more energy, the suppression of 
abuses, and the promotion of reforms, | may refer your Lordship to 
various notes and communications addressed to the Porte and to the high 
functionaries of the State within the last twelve months, and reported in 
the despatches of which a list is inclosed ;* and 1 would wish especially 
to call your Lordship’s notice to the following passage in a recent letter 
from me to the Acting Grand Vizier :— 

“Il mest démontré que sans des changements radicaux le Gouverne- 
ment Ottoman ne peut pas durer,

With regard to Syria especially, in addition to ny various written 
communications on this subject, in the early part of the spring, and before 
any serious troubles broke out in the Lebanon, I had a meeting with 
Rushdi Pasha, fali Pasha, and Fuad Pasha, to which I was resolyed to 
have a witness, and took, consequently, with me the first dragoman. 

At this meeting I told these three men, the leading men in the Empire, 
that, unless they sent a High Commissioner and troops to Syria, and took 
up the whole question of that province (which I had been urging them for 
the last.twelve months to do) in the most serious manner, I should throw 
the responsibility of what might oceur upon them. ; 

Recently again, as to the examination of the accounts of the Military 
Department, and also as to the nomination of incapable functionaries, I 
have taken, as your Lordship is aware, a very decided part; and the 
consequence has been a most determined i ntrigue against me. 

_ The poor Sultan, indeed, is so surrounded at this time that it is next 
to impossible for him to get at the truth, and those who desire to make 
fortunes by his favours represent any one whose conduct is likely to check 
the abuses by which they profit as the enemy of the Monareh and the 
Empire. 5 

For my own part, the course I have taken hitherto has been to defend, 
as far as | could, the integrity of the Turkish dominions: but to do m 
utmost to correct the abuses in the government of them, and if the time 
should arrive when I believe the attempt | have been engaged in is a vain 
ane, I shall then not fail to announce my conviction frankly to your Lord.

No, 37, 
Lord J. Russell to Sir H, Bulwer. 


(N ua S61 a os 
Sir, : Foreign Office, October 18, 1860, 
= i ived whe » advice he Great Powers 
THE. time seems to be arrived when the advice of the Pow 
may be of advantage to the Porte, and, if sincerely adopted and acted 


7 


upon, may save the Sultan from those dangers which too evidently menace 
the security of his Throne. 

The reports you have received from Ter Majesty’s Consuls i the 
Turkish Empire, and especially the answers to the “Qneries” which you 
sent to them, furnish the materials for a judgment upon the present adimi- 
nistration of the Turkish Empire. 

Tt seems to be the general result that the administration of Turkey 
has been considerably improved in the last thirty vears, but that it still 
leaves much to be desired. This is especially the conclusion to be drawn 
from the interesting despatch of Mr. Longworth, containing an account of 
his conversation with the Grand Vizier. 

It may be observed, generally, that the iapeate most dangerous for a 
Government are not those in which a people is kept in abject subjection 
and weakness, but those in which the subject, having acquired wealth, 
knowledge, and strength, turns his eyes to the examination of the defects 
of the Government under which he lives, and is ready and able to form 
combinations to wrest from his rulers the redress of the grievances under 
which he suffers. 

The Turkish Government is precisely in this condition, The Christian 
subjects of the Porte, instead of being purposely kept in a state of poverty 
and ignorance, which was at once the climax and the safe@uard of oppres- 
sion, have been allowed to carry on trade, to acquire property, and, indi- 
rectly, to exercise influence in the State. But the Turkish mode of admi- 
nistration has not improved in the same proportion. Many vexatious 
practices are still retajned, and there is still allowed to Turkish Governors 
that arbitrary license which in good hands may possibly be a benefit, but 
in bad hands is sure to be a scourge, This, therefore, is a period in 
Turkish administration when the Christian subjects of the Porte are sure 
to feel their grievances keenly, and are most likely to combine for their 
removal. They have knowledge, wealth, and numbers; they will be apt 
to prepare physienl strength for a struggle with their Mahometan 
masters. 

The reform of the Turkish finances, which ought to be an easy opera- 
tion, will be attended with the greatest difficulties. Turkey is not encum- 
bered by a very large debt; it has an ample revenue. ‘Two simple rules 
would seem to be sufficient for the guidance of this branch of the State. 
The one is, to collect by the least-oppressive means the sums necessary for 
the purposes of the State, and no more; the other is to apply with honesty 
and economy the sums thus collected to pay the Army, Navy, Administra- 
tion of Justice, Civil List, and other expenses of the State, 

Tam afraid the Turkish Government sins against both these rules. 
More is collected, and in a vexatious mode, than the State requires, and 
the sums thus collected are not honestly applied to the necessary expendi- 
ture of the State. Some part, perhaps a large one, is intercepted by the 
collectors, who make the peasant pay more than his due, and, if he appeals 
to the Pasha, bribe the Pasha for his connivance, Another part is diverted 
from its proper purpose by the Ministers of the Porte, who leave the Army 
and Navy unpaid, while they grow rich by the means which should be 
applied to defend the State. 

Financial confusion, discontent in the Army and Navy, complaints of 
corruption, misery in the provinces, and disorder in the capital, are the 
inevitable effects of these gross abuses. 

It will be the first duty of the Representatives of the Great Powers to 
point out this fatal defect, and suggest the obvious remedies. 

The evidence afforded by the Reports of the Consuls shows sufficiently 
the abuses which prevail in the collection of the revenue. A separate 
Department of the State should be made answerable for that collection, 
and no Provincial Governor should be allowed to sereen those collectors 
who wring from the peasant more than is due from them, 

In like manner, an account should be required of the sums paid to 
each Department for Army, Navy, Justice, Foreign and Home Depart- 
ment, and Civil List. The Commissioners will, with the assistance of the 


Grand Visier, be able to ascertain whether the sums received by the 


is 


Department are actually spent on that part of the public service which is 
under their administration, 

It will be no part of the business of the Representatives to inquire 
whether these sums are wisely applied; into such details they cannot 
usefully enter: but their business will be, to inquire whether they are 
actually applied to the public service, or go to feed the foul stream of 
political corruption. 

In urging the reform of financial abuses, you will endeavour to obtain 
the assent of all your colleagues in denouncing corruption, whatever may 
be the hands which receive the bribe, and whoever may be the Minister 
into whose coffers the gold thus ill-acquired may flow. It would be a 
public, nay, 1 European misfortune, if the Representatives of the Great 
Powers should assume the patronage of some favoured Minister of the 
Porte, and endeavour to maintain him in wealth and dignity at the 
expense of the essential welfare of the State and of the unfortunate 
peasantry of the provinces. ‘Too often have those who preside over the 
miszovernment of Turkey secured impunity by playing one Christian 
Power against another, and escaped from the performance of solemn 
promises by the rivalry of jealous Ambassadors, Let it be your care 
loudly to disavow, and practically to abjure, all partiality, I! such 
passions are allowed to prevail, it will not be the fault-of the Sultan if the 
evils of his administration remain uncorrected; it will be the fault of that 
Christian Power which sereens and covers the corruption of his ollicers, 

[t will be essential, therefore, to ascertain in what manner the 
11,000,000. sterling, or whatever sum the revenues of the Porte may 
amount to, is distributed, Upon this distribution, with the reserves | 
have mentioned, the Commission may have many valuable remarks to 
offer, which I will not here anticipate. 

The next great question is the administration of justice. 

It appears, from several of the Reports of the Consuls, that, while the 
inferior Courts admit Christian evidence, the superior Courts do not. Thus 
the spirit of the Hatti-Humayoun is evaded, 

In some provinces the Hatti-Humayoun is not even published; in 
many, perhaps in most, its provisions are disregarded, 

The Reports of the Consuls furnish the materials with which you can 
construct a plan of reform for the administration of criminal and civil 
justice in Turkey, 

It is desirable that, with a view to improve the condition of the 
Christians, and to strengthen the position of Turkey by ensuring justice 
and equality to all the subjects of the Porte, the Representatives of the 
Great Powers should meet in Conference at Constantinople. You are 
therefore instructe! to propose to the Ambassadors and Ministers of 
Austria, France, Prussia, and Russia, to meet and to compare the Reports 
of their respective Consuls, with a view to give, collectively, advice to the 
Porte. 

Tam, &c. 
(Signed) J. RUSSELL.

Lord J. Russell to Sir H. Bulwer,

Sir, Foreign Ojfee, October 25, 1860, 

THE Turkish Ambassador, M. Musurus, communicated lately to 
Lord Palmerston during my absence on the Continent, and attenwarda to 
me, & message addressed to him by the Grand Vizier, requesting very 
earnestly the assistance of the British Government, either by guarantee or 
otherwise, with a view of procuring a loan of 2,500,000. sterling, for the 
purpose of supplying the urgent wants of the Turkish Treasury. 

| applied immediately to persons upon whose intelligent acquaintance 
with ‘Turkish finanee [ had reason to rely, My object was not to seek at 


69 


once the power of obtaining a loan for the Sultan, but rather the means of 
correcting the judgment of the Goyernment as to the present financial 
position of Tu ‘hey 

I send you the result in the shape of two inclosures to this despatch. 

The first contains what I believe to be a tolerably accurate balance- 
sheet of Turkish finance; the expenditure being divided into Departments, 
and the receipts given in a gross sum. 

The second is a short paper of remarks drawn up by a gentleman in 
whose judgment and information I place great confidence. 

From these documents it appears that the year 1859-60 gave the— 


£ 
Expenditure 3 ne 13,281,655 
Receipts ., 50 ms 11,268,994 
Deficiency .. Bs 2,012,661 


[t is obvious that unless the taxes are made more productive, or the 
expenses vigorously retrenched, the Turkish Government is proceeding 
rapidly to bankruptey. But ifa million could be added to the revenue by 
a tax on houses, or duties on stamps or customs, and if at the same time 
a million could be reduced from the expenses, the credit of the ‘Turkish 
Government would be at once restored, and there would be little ditfieulty 
in renewing the floating debt which at present hangs upon the Treasury. 

But unless steps of this decided character can be taken, Her Majesty's 
Government are of opinion, with the writer of the paper, that a temporary 
loan would merely postpone the difficulty, to return again in a more 
aggravated form, and would thus do more harm than good 

Still more pernicious would be a re-issue of inconvertible paper. 
Such an act would destroy all faith in Turkish promises and probity, 

You are directed to express these views to the Grand Vizicr, and 
communicate them to the Representatives of the Four Powers,

Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell.—( Received October 27.) 
(No. 656.) | 
My Lord, Therapia, October 15, 1860, 

WITH respect to your Lordship’s despatches Nos, 485 and 512 of the 
Lith and 15th of September, my shortest way of answering them would 
be to give your Lordship in as few words as possible my ideas as to the 
general state of the Turkish finances, as to what can be done at the 
present time about them, as to what has been done, 

l. In regard to ascertaining the revenue and forming an estimate of 
the expenses according to the existing system; this, after much trouble 
and difficulty from the want of accurate accounts, has been at last 
effected, 

2. The control which your Lordship suggests has been in a certain 
degree provided for by means of a Mixed European Commission, which, 
however, is formed on a wrong basis, as | pointed out at the time, and 
must be improved, } 

3. ‘The establishment and carrying out of a better system of percep- 
tion can only be done by a proper Ministry of Finance: this I consider as 
a sine quill non, and no other but one which has the greater number of its 
principal employés Europeans can suffice; of this lam sure. The Turkish 
Government neither refuses nor assents, 

4, The revenue may be considered at the present time to be at about 
10,000,0006 or 11,000,000/, ‘The expenditure may be taken at something 
over; but Tam really afraid of stating a figure. [hav sometimes been 
told they balanced, and figures shown are to that effect; at others, an 
excess of S00,0001, ; at others more. Perhaps 1,000,000/. would be near the 
mark, 

T 


 —— |. 


m= P+ 


70 


The expenses by the control I have pointed out, if efficient, would 
be diminished by, at least, 2,000,000/, or 2,500,0001. 

The receipts by a proper Ministry of Finance would be increased in 
the same proportion, ve 

ft is on this fund that the State would have principally to count in 
iis subsequent arrangements, and this 1s why a proper Ministry of Finance 
is an absolute necessity. 

| now come to the debts, which throw the Government at this moment 
into such confusion. 

I will take them at about 18,000,000L sterling. This would include 
claims not yet arranged, set down at 9 QO0.000L: the Government now says 
only 16,000,000!, exclusive of foreign debts, ate 

“1 must then separate these 18,000,000/, into two divisions, one of 
12,000,000/., the other of 6,000,0008. First, these 12,000,000/. have to be paid 3 
‘tis clear they cannot be, but an interest of 7 per cent. on them might. 
‘The capital, therefore, must be conyerted into a debt, with interest, ancl if 
this interest is duly secured it assumes the value of capital. | 

The 6,000,000/. remaining, however, which | have named as a debt are 
not in facta debt, but a debt that has been paid and left a vacuum in the 
treasury which has to be supplied. This vacuum has been ereated in the 
following manner: ‘The Government: has placed the revenue of the next 
seven months out of its power. Tt has made it over for sums received and 
spent, ‘Thus the whole of the expenses of the State have to be paid for 
the next seven months, and there is nothing to pay them. Indeed, for the 
last two or three months there has been nothing but w hat has been found 
by short loans at 35 or 40 per cent, 

"It is this 6,000,0001, which 1t was wished to horrow. Ifa loan cannot 
be made, what is tv be done? You may refuse to pay other creditors and 
ro on, but you cannot refuse to pay the army, navy, and civil fanetionaries, 
or the attributes and action of a Government cease, A simple way would 
he to make some arrangement with those to whom the revenue has been 
thus alienated, and to spread the sum they have now to receive over a 
series olf years. ; fs 

'This, of course, has been thought of, but found impossible, ‘I he 
creditors will not consent ta relinquish the obligations they hold, They 
got the money, in fact, advanced on bills at short date; 1 these bills are 
not paid,—and they cannot be paid if they relinquish the receipts which 
were toacquit them,—it entails immense losses, perhaps bankruptcy, on all 
the various houses which have dealt im these transactions, ant 4,000,0007. 
or 5,000,000L would, | hear, full on Knglish houses. 4} 

This then not being possible —for it was the first thing to think of— 
where are the 6,000,000/, to come from? There is no money in the 

treasury. In the present condition of the Empire any extraordinary 
amount of taxation for the purpose would probably excite universal 
insurrection, for it would unite all classes and sects in a common cause , 
besides, the returns from taxation would be slow—the want 18 immediate. 
After thinking over every other method, the only one I can see is the follow- 
ing one—a forced loan for about 1,000,000/., or 1,500,000/, which is all 
the country would bear. [t might be taken in plate, and exacted from 
men of all ranks who are in employment, But there would still remain 
4,000,000). My notion in respect to these would be this: to place the 
State property easily disposable, which the Government only values at 
about 1,Q00,000/. sterling,*® in the hands of a Mixed Commission, which 
should issue treasury bonds, The existing caimés should be taken up by 
these bonds.¢ which would be received in payment of the State property 
isold always by competition), and 5,000,000/, of new paper money would 
then be issued, 

lt is to be remembered that this sum, cither in metallic or mn paper, 
is in a certain degree required for the ordinary trafhe to replace the 
5,500,000 of caimés which have been recalled, and for which no substitute of 
any kind has betn created. Butin issuing this new paper it must be kept 


© ‘The Government much anderstates this, because it dows mot like the oxpedienty 
+ ‘The number of “caimis'’ out is, on ihe contrary, understated; the Government must be 
1 = i 2. 48 i f t 
mate to find property that would balance the value of the old “caimés,” and it could do this, 


71 


wholly distinct from the former, and its current value maintained. My 
advice for this would be as follows :— 

The new paper (which would only circulate in and about the capital) 
should be numbered, and one sixth drawn and paid at parevery year; for 
which purpose a fund should be allotted and placed in the hands of a 
Mixed Commission. The value of the paper would thus be sustained, and 
the Government, in fact, succeed in spreading over six years this sum of 
6,000,000/. it has now to pay in six months. 

There is much, doubtless, to say against such a project, but I see no 
other better; for no other can meet the next six months’ expenditure; and 
unless this can be met it is impossible to think of the other reforms; for 
the Turks are so far right when they say “unless we can pay our army, 
and navy, and functionaries to-day, how can we sit down and make plans 
for to-morrow?” ; 

All, however, who are in the habit of lending money at high interest 
and for short terms will oppose a general scheme which is to meet the 
general embarrassments. 

They would prefer renewing from three months to three months their 
present loans, and thus sink the country for their own profit daily and 
daily deeper in the gulf in which it would finally and irretrievably be lost. 
But in my opinion one great and decisive measure, com pretending the 
whole condition of things, is the only one to adopt. 

I have dealt purposely in generalities, without entering into figures or 
details, in order to give as broad and clear a view as possi ble of the actual 
state of things, and explain why the 6,000,000L., so often talked of, have to 
be found somehow, and that immediately; and why I think a new paper 
issue, as under the regulations I have jaid down, the old paper money 
being recalled, is not a bad expedient. A loan at moderate interest was 
the simplest mode; and when that ts got this paper money may be, if 
aaijsable. withdrawn; but that loan being now impossible, the measure | 
have Eo tstee seems to me somewhat ingenious, and not impracticable. 
Having, however, put it before the Porte for consideration, | do not urge 
its adoption, for the success of any measure will depend on its mode of 
execution, and as over this | can have no control, | cannot be sanguine 
as to any result. 

As your Lordship will perceive, the whole value of the plan of consoli- 
dating the other outstanding engagements must depend on changing the 
actual deficit intoa surplus. Nothing would be easier in skilful and hones 
hands. But credit can only be re-established when these are found, 

The ‘Turks are wholly ignorant in financial matters, and such an 
education as that required is not given ina moment, The Greeks and 
Armenians, though clever in commerce and speculation, are as ignorant as 
the Turks as to the general principles of finance and financial administra- 
lion; consequently the revival of Turkish credit and the employment 
of foreigners in the financial departments are in my opinion, as | have 
frequently and now repeat, inseparable. 

= I have, &e, 
Signed) HENRY L. BULWER,

No. 41, 


Eurl Cowley to Lord J. Russell.—( Received November 10.) 
(No. 1,424.) 
My Lord, Paris, November 9, 1860. 
THE reports from Her Majesty’s Consular Agents in Turkey, a 
printed summary of which | was instructed by your Lordship’s despatch 
No. 1,040 of the 26th ultimo to transmit to M. Thouvenel, appear to have 
made a considerable impression upon his Excellency. He said to me 
yesterday that they confirmed the opinion which he had long held, that 
the reforms most necessary in Turkey were the reconstruction of the 
Medjlis, and a better administration of justice. Many persons objected 
that the material, that is, the persons who could ameliorate either the one 
or the other, was wanting. This was true to a great extent, but. still 
everything must have a beginning, and if the members of the Medjlis 
were to be chosen by election, the people would find out within four or 
live years the fittest persons to nominate, 
I have, &e. 
(Signed) COWLEY,

de la Turquie d'Europe, j’ai consacré tous mes soins & remplir fidélement 
les ordres que notre auguste Maitre et Souverain a bien voulu me donner 
en me confiant ln mission de m'enquérir de Ja situation de ses sujets et de 
faire droit A toutes leurs justes réclamations sans distinction de race ni de 
religion. Les punitions exemplaires dont j'ai frappé et les fonetionnaires 
qui se sont livrés des actes contrevenant a leur mission, et les fermiers 
des dimes pour avoir pergu de l'argent en sus de ce qui leur était dA, 
ainsi que quelques autorités du clergé Gree tant pour les extorsions qu’elles 
avaient commises que pour leur conduite scandaleuse; le réglement 
définitif de la propriété dans la province de Nissa qui y donnait lien a 
des dissentiments continuels entre les Chrétiens et les Musulmans: la 
mise sur un pied assez convenable de la gendarmerie dans les provinces ; 
Vamélioration des Conseils Provinciaux, en y introduisant des membres 
Chrétiens choisis (’aprés un mode d’élection basé sur les principes de la 
plus parfaite égalité; lextirpation du brigandage dans les provinces que 
Jai parcourues ; et, en définitive, les ordres péremptoires que j'at donnés 
i tous les Gouverneurs de surveiller activement A Pexécution des instrue- 
tions que je leur ai laissées, prescrivant nettement et clairement leurs 
devoirs, ne manqueront pas de produire un effet salutaire, malgré toutes 
les intrigues du dehors, car j'ai eu Voceasion de constater que toute la 
Roumélie est sillonnée des émissaires politiques A qui sont dues les infor- 
mations qui ont donné lieu aux derni¢res récriminations contre le 
Gouvernement Impérial. 

Aussi, par lenquéte que jai fait exéenter par les fonctionnaires 
Musulmans et Chrétiens de ma mission avec le concours de I'Kvéque et 
des membres Chrétiens du Conseil de Nissa, il a été démontré que toutes 
ces récriminations, qui ont fait tant de bruit en Europe, sont dénudes de 
fondement. 

Pendant ma tournée, ayant eu l'occasion de recueillir tous les renseigne- 
ments nécessaires sur l'état des populations, leurs youx Iéeitimes, les 
défauts de l’administration, je mappliquerai, sous les auspices de Sa 
Majesté Impeériale, et avee le concours de mes colléwues, 4 ¢laborer et a 
soumettre a la sanction Impériale les projets tendant & obvier aux abus 
et A snppléer aux défectuosités de administration, par la complete 
organisation des Conseils, la mise en vigucur du Code Civil avec sa 
procédure, abolition du systéme d’aflermer les dimes, et la formation de 
corps réguliers de gendarmes dans toutes les provinces de l'Empire, 

Dans la prévision que le contre-coup des derniéres affaires de I’Italie 
pourrait se faire sentir dans quelque province de I'Empire, par les 
intrigues étrangéres, le Gouvernement Impérial a déji pris les mesures 
convenables de précaution et de surveillance, conyaincu qu'il est que le 
principe de non-intervention entre les Souverains et leurs peuples, faisant 
partie du droit public de l'Europe, sera également respecté A légard de 
Empire Ottoman contre les tentatives révolutionnaires qui ne font que 
détruire les meilleurs efforts des Gouvernements, et qui, notamment dans 
un pays, comme la Turquie, peuplé par des habitants de diverses races, de 
diverses croyances religicuses, ne peuvent qu’engendrer la haine et la 
discorde, qui tendent & rendre toute bonne administration impossible,

No. 44. 
Lord J, Russell to Sir H. Bulwer, 
(No, 649.) 
Sir, Foreign Office, November 22, 1560. 


THE Turkish Ambassador called upon me yesterday, and read to 
me the despateh from the Grand Vizier, of which | inclose a copy.* 

He then asked me whether I was not satisfied, and whether I would 
not instruct you to postpone the meetings of the Representatives which | 
had announced to him, . 

I told him the promises of the Grand Vizier were very fair, but I 
could by no means postpone the meetings of which I had spoken. ‘That 
in my view the abuses and defects of the ‘Turkish Government were 
capable of remedy, but it must be a practical remedy, ‘That the exercise 
of two qualities, namely, energy and integrity, would set all right; but 
there was no time to lose. ‘That in our inquiries respecting Syria we 
were informed that the old system was to appoint as Governor the Pasha 
who could pay the highest bribe at Constantinople. That his object, on 
arriving at Syria, was to repay himself and become rich. Hence the 
oppressions and robberies practised upon the people: and when by these 
means a Pasha had become rich, the process was in two or three years 
repeated—fresh bribes at Constantinople, fresh oppression in Syria. 
That the time was come when such a Hee must be abandoned. 

[ send you other papers given me by M, Musurus, 

j Tam, &c 
(Signed) J. RUSSELL,

No. 45. 


Sir H, Bulwer to Lord J. Russell —(Reeetved Novenber 26 ) 
(No. 744.) 
My Lord, Coustantinople, November 12, 1860, 

I HAVE received your Lordship’s despatch No, 561 of October 18. 
The view which your Lordship takes with respect to the fact that it is 
not when a nation is in its most abject condition, but when it begins to 
rise from that condition, that the ery of discontent is heard, and the 
demand for reform is made, seems to me statesmanlike, just, and peculiarly 
applicable to the condition of Turkey at the present time. Lt may likewise 
be said that when a Government undertakes to satisfy a large class of its 
population which has been previously in an inferior condition to the rest, 
if is rarely wise todo so by halves: and it is evident that the policy of 
the Porte is to do all that it ean be called upon to do fairly and safely, to 
give the Christian population in the Empire a portion of that political 
power which has been hitherto pretty well a monopoly of the Turks; 
indeed, it is quite clear that men who have all the means of giving their 
children the best education cannot but be discontented when they look 
forward and see that there are few or no roads in their own country to 
honour and distinction. Nevertheless, your Lordship is well acquainted 
with the fact that when a question of race has to be considered as part 
of the question of government, the points in dispute are not easy to be 
arranged, and that every effort on the part of the governing race will, for 
many years to come, and perhaps always, be received with distrust, and 
repaid by ingratitude. The position in which we ourselves stand in the 
lonian Islands—possessions which owe so much to our guardianship of 
them—is sufficient to show that when even the Turks have done all that 
they have yet to do in the way of justice and equality, they will still leave 
the seeds of future dissatisfaction behind: and that it is not merely by 
the complaints of the one race that the conduct of the other can be judged. 
Your Lordship, however, is perfectly right in considering that the main 
and principal evil of the Turkish Administration—one in which | am 
sorry to say the Christians bear an equal share with the Turks—is the 
corruption which shetelly pervades it, and which renders the taxation at 
once grinding to the people and insufficient for the exigencies of the State. 
This cancer lies so Geely in the core and heart of this society that it is 
exceedingly difficult to provide a remedy for it; and the only difference 1 
can trace between past and present times is, that dishonesty and peen- 
lation were formerly universally practised without being considered 
dishonesty and peculation; whereas now, though they are equally universal, 
they are beginning to be known under their right names: this distinetion 
brings at present no practical good, but it promises with respeet to the 
future that some good will come of it. | also entirely agree with your 
Lordship that it will be for us Representatives to pay great attention to 
this class of abuses, and that it will be our duty also to suggest some 
remedies for them. ‘These remedies may be easily laid down on paper ; 
there will, however, | fear, be much difficulty in carrying them into 
practice, because it will be difficult to find any men who can be employed 
in putting them down who are not already tainted by them, A new seale 
of salaries must, moreover, be at the same time instituted, for it is next 
to impossible at the present time for any subordinate functionary to live 
an the means whieh the State allots to him. Salary, in fact, is little 
considered except in the very highest places, and | have constantly scen 
men take places which have no salary attached to them, as far more 
lucrative, in point of fact, than others in which salaries are paid, 

The legitimate emolument of a small Turkish functionary, and even 
of a large class of the superior ones, is regarded as nothing in comparison 
with the illegitimate means for making money which office in this country 
affords. Alter this state of things—and it ought to be altered imme- 
diately—and you alter naturally the basis of the present Budget, and the 
State will have to charge itself with a far greater expenditure us the only 
means by which it can possibly, though not certainly, procure a larger 
revenue, In short, the question is not one so easy to deal with as it may

must be formed to visit them, and the most capable and honest men

fla ; F \] J 


this—in order to deal with that deficit I have alluded to, and which must 
be dealt with—some plan must be devised. A loan was one means, but it 
was judged impossible, or, if possible, only on ruinous conditions, 

Vhat, in fact, are the terms of the new loan now in contemplation ? 
A debtor 16,000,000/, is to be created, at 6 per cent., at a rate which could 
bring in little more than 8,000,000/. to the Turkish Government, thus 
doubling the 8,000,000L of present debt, and borrowing the §,000,000/, 
which are to be received, apparently at 12 per cent., but in reality at . 
much more. For, first, the interest of the whole of the debt is to begin 
when the first instalment on the loan is made; and these different instal- 
ments, [ understand, are to go over cighteen months, Consequently, the 
Government will be paying, for the first half-vear, interest upon 8,000,000/. 
at 12 per cent., or on 16,000,000/. at 6 per cent, (whieh is the same thing) 
that is to say, during the first year it will furnish 1,000,000/. out of the 
6,000,000/. it receives, Neither is this all. ‘The whole of the commission 
on the first half-year's interest is to be taken out of the first instalment ; 
so that the Turkish Government will receive in reality little more than 
500,000/. at the present time, But I have said that it wants immediately 
7,000,000/, for the next nine months to cover those urgent calls upon it 
which have to be settled before any revenue comes into it for acquitting

them, and the sum now borrowed only comes in by degrees during the 
next eighteen months. Consequently, the Turks will have to make a 
further loan, and in this way it may be fairly calculated that the Porte 
will really have to pay 1,000,000/. revenue in perpetuity for 6,000,000/. 
which is all it will get clear. Yet, the terms of this loan, though ruinous 
tu the Porte, are not more so than might have been expected if the Porte 
had recourse to foreign capitalists at this time. Seeing this, and in order 
at once to meet the existing exigency, which I knew would be met, and 
must be met, somehow, | should rather have advised, first, obtaining’ 
1,000,0002, or 1,400,000, at least, from the national property: secondly, 
another 1,500,0002 (which might have been got easily on loan) by an appeal 
from the Sultan to his Mussulman subjects. I would then have set by 
the 1,200,000/ a-year, which is now to be paid permanently for ten years, 
and upon that sum thus assigned I would have Noeevad ant small sum 
(say 2,000,000/.) immediately required, and issued new paper, recalling the 
caimés which are inconvertible, that should have been convertible at par, 
in certain proportions every six months, In this manner the surreptitious 
issue of further caimés would have been stopped; the new notes, a portion 
of which would have been convertible at par every six months, could not 
have lost much of their nominal value, and would, when it was seen they 
were taken up, have increased the national credit, and by the very revenue 
now assigned in perpetuity | would have in such manner annihilated, in 
less than ten years, the whole 8,000,0001, which is now doubled. to 
16,000, 0008, 

The plan has objections, but | am inclined to think it was the soundest 
for the Otteman Government to have adopted, and I rather believe that 
experience will prove the truth of what I say. 

The information which your Lordship has received ts from some one 
well acquainted with the financial affairs of this country, but I shall be 
panne surprised if he is not in some way also practically interested in 
them, 

| should think, in fret, that the document referred to proceeds, directly 
or indirectly, from some party who has made, and is in the habitof making 
small loans of 200,0002. and so forth to the Turkish Government, which at 
hieh interest have been reneweil. 

[t is therefore for his interest to say, “ Patch up the present state of 
things; let the Turkish Government renew where it can the bills, now out 
by new bills for three or four months to come, and wait for better times, 
when it may possibly be able to make a loan on better terms.” , 

But what will he the state of Turkey, even if not threatened by foreign 
complications, if it goes on another year on this system of making and 
renewing small loans? How in such a state of things can it establish any 
permanent basis of expenditure or receipts? We should thus, I venture 
to think, be sapping our plan of reform at its very basis? Is it not better 
on the contrary, for Turkey to take up and treat the whole of its financial 
difficulties manfully, and at once; filling up the deficit created by arrears 
and the anticipation of increasing revenue by some extraordinary expedient 
consolidating its other engagements ; and thus standing square in face of 
the improved system of receipt and expenditure tt has to mtroduce 7 

A foreign loan was one way of filling up the said deficit, and the most 
usual, but certainly the most expensive one, ‘The other I have just named 
if fairly carried out, was the least costly, and would have furnished the 
best return, Moreover, | repeat, by bringing the national property into 
circulation it would have added to the wealth of the country. By giving a 
proof of the loyalty of the Mussulman subjects of the Porte, it would have 
added to the Sultan’s authority, and T will venture even to add, that by 
creating a paper money regularly convertible and converted into cash at 
par would, after the first few months, have steadily increased the Ottoman 
credit, Such at least are, | confess, my convictions; and I think if your 
Lordship will do me the honour to read through my somewhat wearisome 
statement, they will he shared in by your Lordship, and also by any 
financier acquainted with financial matters here, and who has no rerscinl 
interest in them. But I have avoided pressing any notions of my own

which had not your formal approval on the Porte, and on the 7th, | commu- 
nicated to the Grand Vizier and Aali Pasha your Lordship’s recent despatch 
on this important and interesting’ subject. 
I have, &e. 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER,

No, 48. 


Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J, Russell.—(Recewed November 27.) 


(No, 771.) 
My Lord, Therapia, November 16, 1860. 

| INCLOSE your Lordship a communication relative to the Grand 
Vizier’s return, and the reforms he is preparing; and [I beg at the same 
time to take notice of the various despatches which your Lordship has 
lately sent me relative to the state of this Empire, and the dangers 
menacing tt. | 

These may indeed naturally Gill your Lordship’s mind with anxiety. 

With reference to what | have myself already written on the subject, 
I may now say that I am more and more inclined to think that Russia, 
Prussia, and Mrance have made up their minds as to the Ottoman Govern- 
ment’s approaching downfall, and that Austria alone wayers as to the 
course she should pursue respecting it. 

What I confess | am most afraid of is, this Empire slipping away, as 
it were, by degrees; the consequence of successive incidents, over the 
euidance of which we should have no control, 

Already France appears pretty evidently to have her attention bent. 
ina particular direction, ‘Throughout Egypt and Syria she has extended 
an influence which no other European State can claim, 

As to Russia, she menaces Turkey on every side, and has merely to 
select where her attack can be made with most convenience to her. 

The views of Prussia are less to be understood, but looking at the 
conduct of the Prussian Agents I cannot doubt what they are. 

It becomes, therefore, a grave matter of consideration to us what line 
of conduct we should ourselyes pursue, and perhaps the time is come to 
look the question fairly in the face, and consider all its bearings, 

The exercise of a joint protectorate by the Great Powers is an excellent 
expedient for preventing the impending catastrophe, if it has been estab- 
lished on a truth; that is, if all the Powers who guaranteed the existence 
of the Ottoman Empire really and honestly wish it to exist: but at the 
present moment, can it be said that this is the ease? Or rather, is not the 
pretence a mere temporary mystification, to which every one affects to 

ive eredence, but which no one really believes; matters im the meantime 
being rendered more critical by the want of that resolution and that 
energy in the Turks themselves which afford the only valid guardianship 
to men in desperate situations. Nothing, indeed, can be more evident 
than the great and striking evils which corrode their society and their 
Government, and appear to conspire with fate against the continuance of 
their power; still nothing, if we look reasonably at the general history of 
human affairs, ean be more unjust than the present violent and sudden 
clamour against them, ; 

However desirable such transformations may be, there is no example 
of a country being within a few years thoroughly metamorphosed, and 
brought from a very bad into a good eonclition. | 

‘The two greatest reformers in modern times are Peter the Great, 
personified in an individual; and the National Assembly, representing the 
spirit of an age, Both these attempted suddenly, and with immense 
power and immense ability, the work of a complete new order of things. 
Peter the Great has been dead 135 years; and if a Mixed European 
Commission were sent into his dominions, [ fear their report would be by 
no means satisfactory, even to the Emperor Alexander himself, ‘The 
National Assembly passed away amidst scenes of blood and confusion ; 
and although some of the seeds it sowed took root, others seem at this 
moment scattered to the winds, 5 

ull

BG 


Much has been done, no doubt, in Russia since 17 
since 1790: but both Empires have passed 
and the result they aimed at been, th 
tudes, but very partially achieved, 

When, therefore, we expect in Turkey great and radical changes 
amidst the most difficult circumstances, and in the shortest period of time, 
we simply expect inipossibilities, 

The Hatti-Humayoun itself was merely valuable as a declaration of 
principles which might serve as a landmark for future lerislation. It did 
nothing itself in the way of legislation, and | doubt much whether, by 
pointing too suddenly and extensivel y at an entire alteration in manners 
and feelings, it did not give rise to many of the evils which usually follow 
such experiments, aud actually followed those of the same kind made in 
France in the eighteenth century, All attempts, in fact, to sweep away 
at onee long-established customs and laws, tends invariably to doubt and 
confusion, now ereating delays, now generating impracticable expedients ; 
authority in the meanwhile falling into abeyance, 

A more gradual and steady progress, each step of which had been 
marked by some clear and positive though moderate change—a change 
which could have mingled the new with what was old, and that yet existed 
around it—would, if 1 mistake not, have created a better situation at the 
present lime, and given greater hopes of building up new improvements, 

vy having laid a more solid foundation for them. SUill it is impossible to 
deny that great progress in many directions has been made, and that the 
road to further progress is opened wide: were the Turks to return from 
the extreme of absolute impunity into which they were hurried when they 
abolished the opposite extreme of absolute tyranny. I do not think, 
therefore, that the critics of the Porte are Just to the Porte: and yet the 
period has arrived when even the friends of the Turkish Government 
cannot merely be just; for the present abuses, which it may be fair to 
admit, are the consequences of preceding evils, must nevertheless be now 
either immediately removed, or greatly modified, as the only condition on 
which the sovereignty of the Sultan ean be much longer exercised, 

The position then in which we stand is this: the defence of Turkey 
is confided to the hands of a bad y, the greater portion of which is, perhaps, 
predisposed to destroy it, and its prolonged existence depends upon so 
great and so prompt a change in its internal condition as it requires no 
common degree of energy to produce, and no common stock of hopefulness 
to expect. 

| tis no wonder that, amid such circumstances, a great number of the 
most intellectual Turks abandon themselves almost carelessly to destiny, 
and, in the absence of any plan for exertion, fall into more than the usual 
apathy of their nation. 

_ Over a bystander, this feeling at last creeps. His words, itis true, 
are respectfully listened to, and partially acquiesced in, ‘He cuts” (as a 
colleague said to me the other day, rather expressively than ele rantly) 
“into butter,’ but he produces no firm conviction, though he meets with 
no firm resistanee ; a result whieh ought hardly to surprise us, when we 
consider that we talk to people whose reason may possibly be with us, but 
whose long-established habits and early prejudices are against us, 

Aware that it is impossible to return to the past, not exactly knowing 
where the future is leading them, they pause in distrustfulness at every 
step they are called upon to take in a strange path; and alter a disin- 
terested counsellor has repeated the same advice for months, until the 
time is past by for adopting it, the enly satisfaction he reaps is a final 
expression of regret at not having acted at the proper season on it. 

I will not deny that 1am myself in no small degree discouraged by 
past experience, Nevertheless, [ have endeavoured, as far as I could, to 
form some opinions as to what is yet to be essayed, and will here endea- 
vour to express them, 

l think, then, on seeing what ean be done to 
of Turkey, we must begin by 
what Turkey ought to b

a foreign hand intervenes to prevent the national authority from main- 
taining order and acministering justice according to its own usages and 
laws. 

The letter of the old Capitulations, moreover, is invoked amidst a 
state of things to which it no longer applies, and the Ottoman Govern- 
ment is told that it is to take its place amongst the great Governments of 
furope, to adopt their civilization, and to answer to the exigencies which 
that civilization demands; whilst it is frequently denied the ordinary 
attributes which the smallest Republic in South America, and even, 
Hf lam not mistaken, the King of the Sandwich Islands is allowed to 
POSSCSS. 

In short, there is a general jumble of conflicting ideas and customs, 
of conflicting expressions and pretensions, out of which, without some 
more definite and precise understanding, it is impossible to create rezu- 
larity, power, or security. It is idle, then, to ask or expect that a Govern- 
ment can exist under ‘conditions by which any Government must 
necessarily be destroyed; and if we really wish sincerely for the continu- 
ance of the Ottoman Empire, we must rest its continuance on something 
like rational and solid foundations, 

I will now endeavour to point out some of the principal causes which 
check or interfere with internal improvement, and | will then say a few 
words as to external protection. 

The first and principal evil against which the Ottoman Empire has at 
this instant to contend is that which exists in the head of the State, The 
netual Sovereign has many qualities, but he has also defects, some of 
which spring from nature, others from education, 

He wants both a continued energy and and a fixed) will; but even 

these are of little advantage when the individual who possesses them does 
not clearly understand the purposes to which energy and will should be 
directed; and how can a man shut up in a palace, only spoken to by 
thase who stand trembling before him, without any practical acquaint- 
ance with lis own country or Europe, carry forward consistently a plan 
Which is to combine what is peculiar in his own country with what is 
applicable to it in Kurepe? "This, however, is an evil which cannot now 
be remedied, but they who wish well to Turkey should do their best to 
prevent its being perpetuated, 
— ‘The heirs to the throne, though saved by the Sultan’s humanity from 
the bowstring and the poisoned bowl, are kept immured from all society 
but that of women and eunuchs, and they are some day to be suddenly 
called from this ignominious solitude to the cares of administering a 
mighty Empire. If the two Princes next in immediate succession, the 
Sultan's brother and son, were allowed to travel, or at least to mix here 
with such persons as would be capable of giving them some instruction as 
to their future duties, one of the greatest impediments to Turkish progress 
would he, in some degree, removed, 

The second evil, which has grown in some degree out of the former 
one, is the less of all authority, and a complete chaos in the publie 
administration, 

l say that the cause of this is in some degree to be sought in the 
character and condition of the Sultan, beeause itis he alone who can give 
a atrong and decided impulse to affairs. Were he able to see what should 
be done, and resolute enough to say itshall and must be done, all those 
minor obstacles over which his Ministers are constantly stumbling would 
be at once put out of the way. But this cause is not the only one. 

The Ottoman Government, when it undertook to place the Empire on 
a hew foundation, was neither entirely sincere in its professions, nor did it 
clearly understand what it was about, 

lt accepted the announcement of great, immense, and sudden reforms, 
less with a desire to reinvigorate Turkey than to gain Europe. It wag 
less occupied with the laws it was to make than with the newspaper 
articles it would produce. It consequently undertook too much too 
suddenly, and got confused amidst the novelties it promulgated. An 
uncertainty between the old and the new everywhere prevailed, and still 
prevails. A Pasha said to me the other day, “ What am I te do? |

which has to deal with a peculiar subject, in which all classes in ever 
nation, and all nations, are alike interested, and which can only be SH 
cally dealt with by men accustomed to order and regularity in eceauate 
honest by education and character, and acquainted with certain rules os 
to taxation and expenditure, which are pretty well general in their appli 
cation, and which cannot be acquired at once, nor carried out eaicinnly 
by men never used to order or regularity, and by habit, practice ‘and 
confirmed education, corrupt, I allude to the financial administration 
wees simply the question as to how a Government is to acquire the 
most we ue wants, and a people have to subseribe the least out of its 
. You will not—you cannot, I feel convinced—get a decent financial 
system established here, except by the employment of Europeans. Nor 
is it any use canvassing the avite or inconveniences and contingencies 
which may attend such a change; the change ts one of absolute fiemesit F 
and nothing in this world can be done that is not subject to wile ai 
none vee and contingencies.

; Sy See NEON, betnel ¥; Je of foreign protection. 
st present general protectorate. And 
here the first question which presents itself to my mind is, whether the 
present plan of a joint protectorate is a sound one, If it is “Ou must 
render it real and positive, and net imaginary and vague. ihe ereat 
Powers of Europe have not up to this time rested their protection of 
Turkey upon any common principle except the negative one to which I 
have already alluded, that the Empire had better not be destroyed 
But, passing by the fact that this, though commonly said is not 
commonly felt, [confess that it seems to me that if a certain number of 
States are to unite heartilyin protecting another, they must see some commo 
and positive advantage in so doing: the advantage that they avoid in 
A is hardly sufficient for men to be long connected in any line of contluee 
io ea ee see as their object, not merely something to avoid, but something 
i, then, there is to be a European confederacy for maintaining: the 
nas sal that coniederaey must be plainly and openly founded 
same general system, from whie . Ww Confederacy has 
Bee ae ystem, from which the whole Confederacy has adyan- 
I see no other than that of the protecting States looking on the 
Ottoman territory as in some respects a common colony to them all, i 
which, with a common object, they would have many of the great a 
common adyantages they would hold in a Colonial poneescion thou, i 
such COMY should be governed by a Turkish Sovereign, and ina Rania 
oa a oe i in ir eta 
ae : i with the habits, wants, and peculiar composition of its national 
_ Something of this Kind exists, indeed, at the present time, but it 
exists vaguely and confusedly, On the one hand, Kuropeans exerci 
powers in Turkey which are incensistent with practical government a 
the other hand, they are debarred from rights which it would be ady : 
tageous to the Turkish Government that they possessed. | 
; Having a clear object in view, | am fos stating clearly what that 
abject demands. ‘There should be a fair and common Treaty of Com a 
applicable to all nations. All foreigners should have a right ofresidence 
and of acquiring and holding property on the same terms as the nativ e 
they should, moreover, be subjected to the same taxation as the ies 
and, if they maintained aseparate legislation from the native one whi fh 
I think they should not do, or only do for a time, they should h Bee 
common law and common tribunals for themselves. How can we inde i 
call upon the Turks to form laws for Turks, Greeks Jews Arn antanie 
and Kuropeans, when the Muropeans resident in this very Sees 
to which the ‘Turks have to apply their legislation, cannot form an aH ner 
laws or appoint any common tribunals, ) Pein 
I repeat what [have said in many previous despatches—here is the 
first step towards that result to which, if we aim at Turkey existing, acd

existing with anything like vigour or independence, we must direct our 
attention. 

If the Christian Powers had a common legislation in Turkey for 
themselves, who cannot see that this, ere long, would become the legisla- 
tion of the Turks? And when the Turks and Huropeans had common laws, 
the natural result would ere long be a facility for framing: one legislation 
out of the two, and establishing & judicial system to which all whe resided 
in the Ottoman Empire might be subject. 

‘These, however, are grave and serious enterprises, and the question 
is, whether we are serious In our objects ; if we are, they deserve the 
efforts necessary to carry them out; if weare not, all the time we talk about 
them is wasted. 

The question, however, here presents itself as to what power other 
States which afford their guarantee to Turkey on conditions which, if 
accepted and carried out, they would be likely to consider a common 
benefit, should exercise over Turkish affairs. 

| must say, for my own part, that I think there are only two ways of 
looking at this matter of foreign interference. Hither there is no necessity 
for it and no adyantage in it, and then it should not exist at all, in theory 
or practice; or there is a necessity for it, there is an advantage in it, and 
1 would then have it clear and positive, and bounded precisely by the 
necessity and advantage alluded to. 

A nation is not so humiliated by an interference, checked in degree, 
and defined in extent, and likely to be limited in duration, if it is proposed 
for a certain purpose—as it is by an unlimited, undelined interference, 
constantly exercised, and not tending to produce any specific result, It is 
only essential that the result aimed at by foreign interference should be 
profitable to the nation whieh submits to it, 

The present Provincial Administration is perpetually in conflict with 
our Provincial Agents, or those of foreign Powers, as to the vices of that 
Administration. 

We receive complaints, we make complaints; explanations are required 
from the parties complained of, a definite judgment can rarely be arrived 
at: and the Pasha who differs from a Consul either escapes with impunity, 
or is hunted down by the continued pursuit of the Dragomans of the 
Embassy to which the Consul is attached, rather than condemned by any 
deliberate conviction on the part of the Turkish Government that he 
deserved punishment or dismissal. 

A far more simple and practical course would be to have the inquiry 
into the conduct of the accused Pasha before the Grand Council open to 
the diferent Embassies or Delegates. they may send there, and even to 
eive them, if united in considering him guilty, the right to protest against 
vis acquittal. 

But L confess | go further than this: let it be clearly set forth that 
the interference of Europe in“Turkish affairs is to produce certain bene 
ficial results, which results might be produced within a moderate period, 
after which it would be reasonable to expect that ‘Turkey could exist on 
sound foundations, and [should hardly care how far that interference 
extended. 

Any plan, for instance, of establishing a judicial system here, which 
would dispense with the constant and daily shock between the Turkish 
and foreign authorities, which brings, as | have said, this Government 
more into contempt than any well-delined authority for establishing: proper 
tribunals would do; any interference which had for its object the organi- 
zation of a proper financial Administration, and the total demolition of 
that overgrown weed of corruption which now overlops und destroys all 
the natural fruits of industry, would be mm my eyes an evil which the 
Turks might for a time undergo, as less certain to be ultimately ruinous 
than the national system of abuses at present in vigour, 

Your Lordship will thus perceive that Ido not view foreign inter- 
ference with such horror as some people, if the extent of foreign inter- 

ference is marked out, and if its objects are for the benefit of Turkey; but 
what I think fatal is foreign interference the extent of which is not 


; mab 


92 


marked out, and the result, if not the object, of which must be, to lay 
prostrate the Government which we affect to suppor | 

I would rather see a clear authority assumed within prescribed limits, 
than a doubtful authority exercised without any limit at all. A perpetual 
system of duplicity is a bad system of reformation; and the time seems to 
ine come, if we wish to maintain this Empire, to cease to attempt main- 
taining it amidst a series of delusions. 

The necessity for foreign interference, however, might in a great 
derree be averted by the Turks themselves if they clearly saw their own 
interest. ; : 

They may wish to avoid it, but, in the position in which they stand, 
antl even according to the plan which they have sketched out for them- 
selves, the public spirit and the general notions of Europe must find their 
way into Turkey, and exercise an influence over it. Either that influence 
will come from without, and through other Governments, or it might be 
exercised within the Ottoman Government itself, and be blended with it. 

Let the Sultan do as Peter the Great did, as even Mehemet Ali did: let 
hin invite Kuropeans on honourable terms into his service; let him make 
use of them in honourable ani responsible situations, and in a little time 
Turkey would be sufficiently Europeanized to look Europe fairly in the face, 
and treat asa really European Power with her. This, perhaps, after all, is 
one of the best and most practical reforms that Turkey could undergo; 
but, unfortunately, it depends solely upon the Sovereign, who could alone 
impose upon native functionaries the employment of foreign ones. 

At present any attempt made in this direction is a sham. The 
foreigner (with one exception, Admiral Slade) is given his salary to oblige 
the Ambassador who recommends him, but is carefully kept from situa- 
tions of trust, unless, indeed, he adapts Islamism, and then, though he 
may he actively employed, he is universally despised. 

It may be urged, | know, that much that | have said is theoretical,— 
all difficult. This is true; but it is by resorting in a certain extent to 
theory, and dealing with difficulties, that Statesmen grapple with great 
events which, if lett to run their course, get after a certain time beyond 
all control. What I see at the present time is that the current of alfairs 
is drifting things insensibly, though not, perhaps, without some external 
agency, ina course which is opposite to the notions which we have long 
entertained ; and we must either be prepared to arrest that course or be 
ready to accept the consequences to which it will ingeabealy lead, 

As to the manner in which this Government must aid itself and aid 
its friends, by its own intrinsic energy and resolution, | am convineed that 
no desultory pressure from without will be sufficient to arouse such 
qualities. "The work must either proceed from sume startling cause, such 
as a last battle for existence, or be left to the gradual effects of time and 
its continual exigencies, and | now fear that the results of time will be too 
slow to contend against the swilt pace of approaching dangers, or it must 
be put more actively into motion by clear and definite suggestions or 
demands, made either by the Five Powers, if they act together as a 
Protectorate, or by Great Britain alone, if she were willing to incur the 
responsibility of acting as an ally. But the real value and gist of such 
suggestions or demands must depend upon the policy with which they are 
embodied, 

What I should wish, | confess, is to make the Ottoman Empire a 


reality of some sort, rather than a nondeseript shadow of which it might 
be said with truth :— 


"Dat sine mente sonum gress ue effingit euntis.” 


[t is with this view that I would say a few words as to those provinces 
which, with an independent existence in themselves, still form nominally 
part of the integral Empire of Turkey. All that I think the Ottoman 
Government should now aim at concerning these provinces is, to have in 
them a frontier people, satistied with the Ottoman Government, disposed 
rather to resist than aidany attacks upon it, and consenting to avow that 
allegiance which costs them nothing, as long ag it can be proffered in a 
manner that does not wound the national sentiment.

DANS sa sollicitude constante pour le bien-étre et la prospérité de 
toutes les classes de ses sujets, votre Majesté Im viriale a daigné me 
contier Vimportante mission de faire une enqucte sur ee plaintes formulées. 
au nom des populations Chrétiennes de Roumélie, et de passer en inspec- 
tion l'administration de certaines provinces de ses possessions MKuro- 
péennes. 

Ces plaintes ont naturellement attiré Pattention de votre Majesté 
Impériale. Kin conséquence, je donne dans ce respectueux rapport la 
priorité a l'exposé de mes investigations 4 cet égard. 

Pénétré des ordres que votre Majesté m'a donnés & mon depart, et 
appréciant Vimmense importance pour |’Empire de connaitre la vérité et 
de redresser, s'il y a lieu, les justes griefs de vos sujets, je nai rien négligé 
pour atteindre ce but. Je me suis fait une régle, partout on j'ai été, de 
convoquer en ma présence les habitants indistinctement, de les engager a 
me déclarer tout ce quils pouvaient avoir a dire, avee les assurances les 
plus solennelles données au nom auguste de votre Majesté de les garantir 
contre toute sorte d’injustice qu’ils auraient pu redouter s‘ils exposaient 
la vérite. sees 

_ Arrivé au Pachalie de Niche, centre d’oh la plupart des plaintes 
étaient parties, je me suis empressé de former un ‘Tribunal Extraordinaire 
composé des fonctionnaires Musulmans et Chrétiens attachés 4 ma 
mission, du Conseil Provincial, ainsi que du Métropolitain Grec, afin 
(examiner tous les faits dont la liste avait été communiquée A votre 
Gouvernement avant mon départ de Constantinople. J’ai honneur de 
mettre Gi joint ous les yeux de votre Majesté Impériale un résumé des 
travaux du dit Tribunal, Les nombreuses piéces justificatives qui s’y 
rapportent ont été déposées aux archives de la Sublime Porte, ; 

Votre Majesté Impériale verra par le document soumis & sa haute 
appréciation, et je lui affirme de la maniére la plus positive, que je n'ai 
trouvé aucun indice de tout ce prétendu systéme de persécution organisé 
par le fanatisme Musulman contre les Chrétiens, Les assassinats, les 
viols, les vols, les spoliations dont on a fait parvenir la liste jusqu’a des 
Cours amies et alliées, ont presque tous disparu devant l’examen con- 
sciencieux du tribunal, Chaque fait a été l'objet d'une enquéte spéciale ; 
aucun moyen légal na été omis. ies 

Est-ce A dire que je fai pas eu a réprimer ou a punir aucun crime 

aucun méfait? Non, Sire, ce serait méconnaitre la nature humaine et 
soutenir une prétention ridicule que de nier l'existence des féaux dont les 
Gouvernements les plus policés wont pu encore se débarasser. Ce que 
jientends établir, ct ce qui resulte de mes recherches, c'est qu'il Pat 
parfaitement inexact que vos sujets Chrétiens alent A se plaindre de la 
moindre persécntion systématique de la part de leurs compatriotes Musul- 
mans ; que comme société humaine chaque communauté fournit son 
contingent de criminels contre lesquels la justice a a sévir, et que le 
contenu de la liste en question n'a existé que dans l'esprit de ia ui 
cherchent 4 nous dénigrer auprés des Puissances, J'ai di prolon er fan 
séjour 4 Niche pour avoir le temps de bien constater la vérité de an que je 
prends la liberté davancer, et de pouvoir me faire une idee juste AG Y 
dont les habitants ont besoin pour le progrés de leur prospérité. 

Aprés avoir accompli le récit fidéle de cette partie de ma mission, je 
demande la permission 4 votre Majesté Impériale de lui exposer Sealetient 
les renseignements que j'ai recueijlis sur l'ensemble de Administration

des Provinces que j'ai parcourues, des mesures provisoires que j'ai prises, 
et les branches de cette Administration qui exigent d’étre améliorées 
promptement et sérieusement. Le mode de fermage dex dimes, Vorganisa- 
tion des vabtiés, l'état des voies de communication, donnent A tous les 
sujets, sans distinction de religion, de justes raisons de se plaindre. La 
création de quelques Cours Criminelles offrant aux justiciables toutes les 
garantics nécessaires est une des réformes qui répondraient aux vaeux au 
pays et au désir paternel de votre Majesté Impériale. Les réglements qui 
établisgent actuellement Vassiette de Vimpot foncier et la perception 
des impéts indirects (russoumat), exigent cealement d'etre réformés, 

Ye prends la liberté de déposer au pied du tréne de votre Majesté 
Impériale les dispositions provisoires que j'ai établies dans les Provinces 
que je viens de visiter, ef que Vai envoyées aux Gouverneurs des contrécs

Parmi les abus que j‘ai constatés, je crols de mon devoir den

veux parler de la conduite peu édifiante de quelques uns du haut clergé 
Gree en Roumélie. Touten rendant justice a la respectabilité de la plupart

quill y en a qui, méconnaissant la nature de leur mission religieuse, se 
livrent & des abus indignes & tous points ducaractere dont ils sont révetus. 
Je me réserve de soumettre sous peu 4 la sanction de votre Majeste 
Impériale le travail de la Commission Provisoire que votre Majeste a

est Aespérer que V'exécution religieuse et fidéle de ce reéglement mettra fin

quil opprime les Chretiens. 

Le Gouvernement de votre Majesté Impeériale, conformément & vos 
ordres, Sire, s‘occupe activement et sérieusement de Vélahoration des 
projets de lois sur chacun des points en question, projets qui seront souniis 
gous peu a la haute décision de votre Majesté Impériale. 

La Providence a confié, Sire, A votre Majesté Impériale la rlorieuse 
mais difficile tiche de régénérer son grand Empire. Le pas qu'elle a fart 
faire dans cette voie est immense. ‘Tout le monde reconnait que ce qui, 
sous yos auspices, a été fait chez nous en dix ans, a da mettre ailleurs un 
temps infiniment plus long pour staccomplir, et occasiouner de graves et 
crandes perturbations. La Turquie doit & votre Majesté Impériale le 
Pienfait (avoir traversé sans aucune secousse violente la majeure partie

Certes, nous ne pouvons pas prétendre a la perfection, Notre Admi- 
niatration, comme j'ai eu Vhonneur de le dire plus haut, laisse encore 4 
désirer; mais, pour bien juger du présent, cest-A-dire, pour MIG

fois qu'elle pensera que ce progrés est laeuvre absolue de son régne et de

Jajoute ces quelques observations non pas dans le but de conseiller a 
votre Gouvernement de se contenter de ce qui a été fait et de rester 
stationnaire: loin de moi cette pensée; je erois, au contraire, que les
