No. 55, 
Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell. —( Received December 17.) 


(No, 819.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, December 5, 1860. 


{| SHOULD mention to your Lordship that notwithstanding my 
‘nvitation to the Representatives to meet and privately discuss amongst 
ourselves the measures of reform which it might be most advisable to 
suggest to the Porte, no meeting has taken place: the French and 
Russian Representatives requiring instructions ; the Russian rather 
objecting to the ‘ntroduction of the Sardinian Minister into the project; 
and the Internuncio considering that as the Porte promises reforms, it 
would be better to leave it the initiative. 

Under these circumstances there has been nothing for me to do but 
to urge the Porte to adopt such measures at once is will satisfy any 
reasonable expectations, and thereby prevent the necessity for that 
advice which it appears unwilling to recely = 

The measure, | confess, to which I should attach the most practical 
importance, is that of sending Commissioners with suflicient powers into 
the provinces, and charging them with the task of introducing the few 
simple and necessary meastires required, and whieh are mentioned in my 
note to my colleagues, The appointment of such Commissioners, with 
adequate instructions, and the communication of such instructions to the 
Representatives, 1s, however, 1 think, the least that can sullice. 

[ shall write more on this subject by the next messenger at thesame 
time it is satisfactory to think that Her Majesty’s Government has so 
clearly shown that whilst it is desirous to. maintain the Ottoman Empire, 
it is equally anxious to see its administration improved and strengthened, 

[ have, &c. 


(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER. 
No. 56, 
Lord J. Russell to Sir H. Bulwer. 
(No, 726.) 
Sir, Foreiqn Office, December 20, 1860, 


| HAVE received your Excellency’s despatches Nos. 810, 814, anid 
819, of the Sth instant. 

In the first of these despatches your Excellency combats the opinion 
expressed by M. Thouvenel, that it would be expedient to extend the 
constitution and sphere of action of Local Councils, and you maintain, on 
the contrary, that the attention of such Councils should be restricted to 
local matters, and that no measure should at present be adopted which 
would relieve the Porte from direct responsibility for the acts of those 
agents, or those agents from their responsibility to the Porte. In the last 
of these despatches, you state that circumstances having prevented the 
Representatives from meeting together for the discussion of measures of 
reform to be submitted to the Porte, you had urged the Porte to adopt 
spontaneously such measures of reform as would sutisly any reasonable 
expectations, anc render it unnecessary for the Representatives to oller to 
the Porte any advice on the subject. 

I have to state to you, in reply, that I agree generally in the opinion 
whieh you express in the first despatch. 

It is, nevertheless, to be observed, that if Christians are to be admitted 
to take part in the administration of affairs in ‘Turkey, they ean only be 
so admitted as far as Local Councils are concerned. It is scarcely ta be 
supposed that any Christian would be allowed to hold one of the great 
offices at Constantinople. 

With regard to the question of reforms, you will state to Aali Pasha 
that the friends of Turkey wish to see reforms effectively introduced and 
vigorously prosecuted . while the enemics of Turkey desire them to be 


[ee ee oe 0 oe 


ANd a ey 


120 


postponed or refused, in order that a ground may thereby be laid for the 
(lissolution of the Empire. 

The worst vice of the old system is that the Governor of a Province 
bribed the Ministers for his appointment; the officials of the Province 
bribed the Pasha for liberty to oppress; and the tax collectors bribed the 
officials for license to rob, That the Empire has been governed by what 
a great Irish orator called, in reference to his own country in former 
times, a subordination of vultures.” 

Unless this vicious system is totally abandoned, no instructions or 
ediets will do any good. 

You will state this to the Grand Vizier in the clearest terms which 
are compatible with courtesy towards the Minister of a friendly Power. 

Lam, &c. 
(Signed) J. RUSSELL, 


No. 57. 
Lord J. Russell to Sir A, Bulwer. 
(No. 727.) 
Sir, Foreign Office, December 20, 1560. 


WITH reference to the statement in your despatch No, 8)7 of the 
5th instant, that if the Governments of France and England could have 
got 10,000,000/ for Turkey on reasonable conditions it would have been 
the best and simplest mode of proceeding, I have to inform you that it 
was unreasonable to expect that England and Franee should obtain 
10,000,0008 upon their own credit for Turkey without having the entire 
management of Turkish finances. France refused to undertake this 
management, and Aali Pasha refused to grant it, and so the project fell 
to the ground. 

| should wish to be informed whether, in your opinion, it would be of 
any use now to send an English Commissioner to Constantinople, not to 
serve as a financial screen, but to ascertain the truth, | 

Tam, &c, 
(Signed) J. RUSSELL.

Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell.—( Received December 24.) 
‘No. 794.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, November 28, 1860. 

I INCLOSE the report of the French and English Commissioners 
with respect to the state of Turkish finances and the manner in which 
they have themselves been employed, and deem they ought to have been 
employ) in reference thereto, ; 

M. de Plasuc, the French Commissioner, is here especially for the sole 
purpose of attending to the Commission, of which he is a member, and 
consequently he gives up all his time to the object, and keeps his Ambas- 
sador constantly informed on it. 

Mr, Faleonnet, although he is perfectly willing to give such informa- 
tion from time to time when asked for it, is Director of the Ottoman Bank, 
and generally engaged in business as well as Financial Commissioner, 
and cannot, therefore, amidst his various other eccupations, attend to this 
especial one so systematically or assiduously as his colleague. 

With respect to the inclosed report, for instance, M. Te Lavalette had 
it on the Monday or Friday—mine did not get to my hands till the 
Wednesday following; and as the post was going off there was neither 
time to read nor to copy it, and still leas to make those observations on it 
which it might require, 

I shall have some further remarks to make on this subject on a future 
occasion, 


But what I say now is not in the nature of a complaint against

f lin lat issist but rather 
_Falconnet’s unwillingness to do what he can to assist me, but : 
Hee BIERRHOR of the fact that he cannot, from the nature of things, do 
so much for me as M. de Pleeuc can do for ee rench Ambassador. 
I have, &c. :

se. respect to the Budget now given, l do not eae ee fhe 
principal facts ig quite sufficiently clear, although they are alluded to, 
nd even stated. . : 
me The Budget is, as | have said before, partly imaginary; for about a 


SANS peel ERS 
ah et: om | 


million and a-half are taken off as future reductions, without any state- 
ment or knowledge as to how these reductions are to be effected. The 
Budget, moreover, is wholly a prospective one, since several months’, if 
not a year’s, revenue is, as I have frequently observed, made over by 
anticipation as security for various loans raised and spent. 

othing, as far as [ can see, is taken into account for claims not yet 
decided, and which will amount to a considerable sum ; nor does it seem 
to me made sufficiently conspicuous that the deficit which the Commis- 
sioners say presses upon the Treasury, is the sum of debts I have so often 
spoken of as assigned to Galata merchants. 

[ am likewise by no means sure that the arrears due to the troops are 
included ; sufficient, however, appears to show— 

Ist. That the sum now borrowed is insufficient to meet the urgent 
demands of the Porte. 

2ndly. That the Porte has added nearly 1,500,000/. sterling to the 
side of its expenditure on a balance-sheet which only reduces itself to 
a tolerable equality between receipt and expenditure by 1,500,000/, of 
unspecified reduction, 

ardly. That notwithstanding this very unsatisfactory state of things, 
nothing would be so easy as for the Turkish Government to restore its 
finances by skilful and prudent management, 

The examples which M. de Plauc, who was employed on the Greek 
finances, draws from Greece, testify to this conclusively. But, on the other 
hand, | think it equally clear that without such sxilful and prudent 
management, this new loan will be a further gigantic step towards that 
bankruptey with which the State is threatened, 

What appears to me not improbable, indeed, is, that the Porte, 
finding what it has now to receive, will not meet what it has to pay; will 
again revert to credit at Galata, for a moment re-established by this 
present supply; and be at the end of another year in precisely the same 
situation as at present, with the increased interest of the new loan, 

To prevent this some means must still be resorted to for supplying 
the hiatus between what is instantaneously wanted and what will be instan- 
taneously received, 

At the conclusion of the document, is an exposition of views as to 
paper-money. Mr, Falconnet’s opinions are clear; M. de Plouc’s are 
not so, What M. de Plosuc means to state, he tells me, is that neither a 
loan nor paper-money of any sort would be of present use, without economy 
and management in the future; but he considers that a paper-money of 
some sort may yet be necessary, and that the plan of a paper-money which 
would have been made convertible at par in certain proportions periodically 
out of the revenue now allotted for the interest of the loan, might have 
been of the two plans a preferable one. 

[tis possible that the Turks may object to the only mode by which 
they can reconstitute their finances on an European model, viz., the employ- 
ment of Muropeans, 

But without this in two years at latest will certainly arrive the 
following crisis: either the Turkish Government will not be able to pay 
its ordinary expenses as a Government, and must then lose the breath, so 
to speak, which keeps a Government alive, or it will not be able to pay 
its Muropean creditors, 

These, then, will step in and demand the control over and manage- 
ment of at least those revenues which are assigned to them as a security, 
and in this way the hand of Kurope will be laid upon the Porte far more 
heavily than it would be at present by its adopting sufficiently early that 
European aid in the way of talent and intelligence which is now at its 
command, and might be used in subservience to its orders and as an aid to 
its authority. 

I have, &c. , 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

124 


attention of the Porte to these recommendations, and most earnestly to 
urge their adoption. ; 

The Undersigned has further the honour to state to Aali Pasha, with 
regard to the present Budget, that the principal facts connected with it 
do not appear to be stated with sufficient clearness, and the manner in 
which the proposed reduction of a million and a halfin the expenditure is 
to be effected, is not stated at all. No allowance has been made for claims 
not yet decided upon, which will amount to a considerable sum, nor is it 
clear whether the arrears of pay due to the army are included in the 
statement of the expenditure to be made. 

The Budget is, moreover, altogether a fallacious one, as far as the 
statement of income is concerned, inasmuch as the revenue for several 
months, if not for a whole year, is made over by anticipation as security 
for various loans raised and already spent. It appears therefore— 

Ist. That the sum now borrowed is insufficient to meet the urgent 
demands of the Porte. 

2ndly. That the Porte has added nearly 1,500,0001. sterling to the 
expenditure side of the account on a balance sheet which only brings out 
a tolerable equality between receipt and expenditure by the statement of 
1,500,000 of unspecified reductions. And, 

Srdly. ‘That, notwithstanding this very unsatisfactory state of things, 
nothing would be so'casy as far as the Turkish Government to restore its 
finances by skilful and prudent management, 

The observations which M, de Plaue makes with rerard to the 
finances of Greece, and the inferences which he draws from his experience 
in that country, conlirm this opinion, 

But without such good and improved management this new loan will 
only bea further step towards that bankruptey with which the Turkish 
State is threatened ; and it is, therefore, to be feared that at the end of 
another year the Porte may be not merely in the same pecuniary embir- 
rassment as at present, but will have the further difficulty of having to 
furnish the amount required for the interest of this new loan. 

The Government of Her Britannic Majesty. in directing the Under- 
signed to press strongly on the notice of the Sublime Porte the absolute 
necessity of introducing better management into its financial system, is 
actuated solely by the desire which it has so constantly evinced to promote 
the welfare and prosperity of the Empire of the Sultan. 

Her Majesty's Government feel it to be their duty to warn the Porte 
strongly of the disastrous consequences which must inevitably follow a 
ierseverance in the improvident system of financial administration which 
vas lecl the ‘Turkish Government into its present difficulties. 

The Undersigned is, therefore, directed to state that no amount of 
loan would be of any permanent benelit to the Porte, unless a system of 
economy and of regularity is introduced into the various Departments of 
the State. That experience of the past has proved to demonstration that 
such improvements will not, and eannot be introduced by those Turkish 
Agents who have been the cause of the present deplorable financial 
condition of the Porte; and that if the Sultan wishes to save his ‘Throne 
and his Empire from the catastrophe towards whieh they are rapidly 
approaching, he will authorize a Commission of experienced and trust- 
worthy Europeans to devise and carry into immediate execution those 
improvements and reforms which may seem to them to be necessary. 

The Undersigned is accordingly empowered by his Government. to 
state formally to Aali Pasha that, tu His Imperial Majesty the Sultan is 
willing to agree to such an arrangement, Her Majesty's Government will 


co-operate by selecting 2 competent person to be a member of such a 
Commission,

(No. 10) ir H. Bulwer to Lord J, Russell —( Received January 18.) 


My Lord, 

M ee er | . Constantinople, January 8, 1861, 

Sanath ML. Th Karl Cowley’s despatch giving an account of a conver 
M. Thouvenel on the subject of the meeting of the Represen- 


127 


tatives to discuss the measures of reform it might be advisable to suggest 
to the Porte, and J have read Mr. Lowther’s respecting a conversation 
with Baron Schleinitz on the same subject. 

1 confess I have been at a loss from the first to understand the sort of 
opposition this step has met with from the French Embassy, nor ila 1 see 
any reason for the opinion which Baron Schleinitz says has been expressed 
by the Russian Government on this subject. i. 

In regard to the Representatives meeting to exchange the opinions 
which they might have been enabled, through the information of their 
Agents, to form as to the state of the provinces, and discuss the measures 
best adapted to supply the most striking defects in Turkish administra- 
tion, the course 1s one 50 simple, so natural, 80 clearly connected with the 
duties of the Representatives at Constantinople, and the constant obser- 
vations of the French and Russian Governments as to the necessity of 
reforms, that I cannot imagine any surprise at or objection to it. 

In fact, if the five Representatives are to act together in supe of 
any measures of reform | can hardly see any other way in which they are 
to commence such 4 course of action. With respect to the reforms | 
propose being more sweeping than any which the Russian Cabinet ever 
suggested, Her Majesty's Government has never objected to necessi?y 
reforms, however sweeping, Nor have I, as Her Majesty's Ambassador, 
opposed any. But reforms are intended to consolidate the Empire. 

What 1 did object to was a measure which in reality proposed no 
reforms at all, but which would have been disorganizing, and not consoli- 
dating the Empire. I mean a promenade throughout it of foreign Agents 
who would have collected round them every element of disaffection, and 
by creating an exaggerated state of despair on one side, and expectation 
on the other, rendered all tempe rate and practical efforts at improvement 
subsequently impossible. 

It appears to me, | confess, that a certain soreness has been created 
by the lead on this occasion having been taken by Her Majesty's Govern- 
ment: but this was, In m opinion, essential ; since it be oved Her 
Majesty's Government to show that its support of Turkey was not 2 
support of its abuses, which it was as anxious as any Government tr sce 
amended; and I say this, because they who have eriticized our policy as 
to the Ottoman Empire have generally endeavoured to degrade our 
advocacy by representing us as indifferent to the causes for dissatisfaction 
which the state of this Empire no doubt presents. 

I have, & 


c, 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER. 
No. 64. 


Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell —(Received January 18:) 


(No. 16.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, January 8, 1861. 

YOUR riaatn | says in your despatch No, 726 of December 1860, 
that in agreeing with my general observations, it is still ta be considered 
that if “ Christians are Lo be admitted to take part in the administration 
of affairs in Turkey, they can only be so admitted as far as local Councils 
are concerned, since it is not to be supposed that any Christian would be 
allowed to hold one of the great offices at Constantinople.” 

It is difficult to express all one’s ‘deas on rather a difficult subject 
without running into tediousness—a fault which I fear I have not always 
avoided; but whilst Lam against giving great powers to local commu- 
nities in the present state of gociety and eivilization in ‘Turkey, because | 
am against giving great powers to ignorant, violent, and prejudiced men. 
hostile to each other, and whose discussions, while they disorganize the 
existing authority, can lead to the establishment of no authority in its 

lace,—I am for placing Rayahs as the Representatives of the central 
authority in responsible situations in the Provinces: these would in the 
end discipline their own race, and at the same time, by the very functions

they held as the representatives of that race, teach by degrees the domi- 
nant one to respect it, ‘ : 

Animated by a fecling of nationality on the one side, and linked by 
position and interest with the ruling Government on the other, they would 
finally become a constituted and moderating power. 

The Fanariotes, in fact, in a less enlightened age, were in some degree 
this; and there can be no doubt that the Greeks, since this class (which 
disappeared after the Greek revolution) passed away, have lost the political 
consideration they formerly enjoyed, and which in nosmall degree, though 
in an indirect manner, benefited their nation. 

It is, moreover, in this way that much of the power now exercised by 
the Greek clergy, who are the greatest scourge of the Greek people, would 
pass to persons more amenable to the ordinary laws and the ordinary ideas 
af civil society. 

lt is, however, no easy matter to obtain the reform on which I lay 
such stress, from the Turks. The reason is evident to me on the spot, 
though it will not so instantly strike one less practically acquainted with 
its peculiarities, We are apt in Europe to imagine that Turkey ts a 
nation composed of various races, which should each see the benefit of 
being united. But to an inhabitant of Turkey the Turkish Empire is 
as nothing. His nation is the particular portion of the Empire to which 
he belongs The Greek looks to his nation as his race; the Armenian 
the same; the Turk the same, ‘To tell the latter—I speak generally—he is 
to make a great State by giving upa portion of the power which the Tarks, 
as Turks, possess in that State, is much the same as to tell the English 
they would become agreater State if they took the French into their 
bosom, and made M.de Morny Lord Mayor of London! ‘The patriotic 
fecling is not with the land—it is with the class on the land with which 
each individual is connected. ie 

Nevertheless, the object to accomplish, though difficult, is in no wise 
impossible. Many yet remember the time when Rayahs held high office, 
and are willing to acknowledge they fulfilled the duties of their posts with 
weal and ability; and there is rising up a fecling amongst the official 
classes of the Turks that the employment of Rayahs in many political 
positions would be advantageous, 

Whiat has alarmed them has heen the theory that the Rayah and the 
Turk are equal, and the deduction drawn from it that consequently the 
Rayahs, being most numerous in many places, would, were such a theory 
acted upon, be ere long the masters, in which case the Moslem knows 
well he would not long enjoy the toleration he would have practised, 

The best course, in my opinion, is at the present time to insist !ess 
an principles, and more on facts—endeavouring to obtain, in the way and 
in the degree that is practical, a positive advantage. The new state of 
things thus brought about might end in nullifying or mitigating the 
fecling of nationalities by absorbing it in the fecling of government. Let 
Rayahs be seen in official situations fulfilling their duties to the advantage 
af the Government, invested with power, whieh in the East is prestige, 
and little by little they will feaaieilt work their way into the privilered 
or ruling body, 
Such, at all events, is the plan | would follow, and think the best to 
advise. 

Those Greek islands wherean nota Turk is to be found should be 
governed by a Greek. In the larger islands and provinces where a large 
Cireek population exists, the Greeks should hold second-rate situations, 
but situations nevertheless of authority and trust. As a part of the 
poverning power they may, and probably will, become attached to it. 
Indeed, T must say my experience proves this, for | knew few more 
zealous servants of the Porte than the few Rayahs who have gained the 


confidence of the Porte; and men are, indeed, generally satisfied without 
rising above those who were born above them, if they merely rise above 
those whe were born ona lovel with them. 

Tdo not like to pursue this subject here further; but what I have 
sid will show the direction of my mind,

No. 69. 
Sip H. Bulwer ta Lord J. Russell. —(Received February 3.) 


(No, 66. Secret and Confidential.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, January 22, 1861. 


{ HAVE alluded in my despatch No. 819 of the 5th ultimo to the 
difficulties which, after some experience, 1 am obliged to confess prevail 
with respect to a joint action of the five Representatives on urkish 
affairs. ‘The fact of it is that at the present time three Powers at least 
are neatly always found ucting together, and in hostility, more or less 
apparent, to the Turkish Government—Russia, Prossia, and France; 
whilst the influence of Austria ‘s little excited, and in a great degree 
annihilated ; so that in fact the joint action of five comes, more or less, in 
coalition of three against one, and as this coalition does not seem to 
originate here in the individuals who represent the Governments in ques- 
tion, but rather to be the consequence of ‘natructions more or less direct 
from those Governments, it i8 difficult for me to prevent it by any 
‘ndividual exertions on my part; the most I can do being to moderate 
its effects. 

With respect to reforms, for instance, the more I think we analyze the 
conduct of the different parties, the more we ehall find that whilst what 
we propose is meant sincerely for the good of Tarkey, what the other 
Governments who speak of reforms propose is with a view to the exten- 
sion of their own influence ; and that with respect to the Christian 
population they are more anxious to appear in the vanguard as their 
protectors than really to do them any essential service : this alone 
prevents a measure roposed by me from obtaining that support, from # 
great part of my colleagues, which it deserves. Whilst with the Turks 
themselves, they being aware that no counsel I might give them will have 
a practical effect unless it obtains the concurrence which they foresee is 
hardly to be expected, my ‘solated recommendations riecessarily lose a 
part of their authority. Nor 1s this all: if any measure | suggested did 
abtain the adhesion of Russia and those Powers who aet with her, it 
would immediately become suspicious in the eyes of the Porte. [ am, 
therefore, of opinion that the best position we eould stand in as to Turkey 
would, if possible, be that of taking a separate and distinet line with her, 
by saying clearly, We will support you against such and such contigencies, 
providing you do such and such things ; making it evident to the Turks 
that they must do what we tell them to do, and assuring them that if they 
do, they may then rely upon Us. 

Isa y this now, not because I think the course [suggest may at this time 
be possible or advisable, but because a time may come in which we shall 
have to consider whether an ‘golated ora joint position with other Powers 
‘s the best for ‘Turkey or ourselves, and my present opinion certainly 
inclines me to think we should have far more influence on the Porte if we 
acted alone in the manner J have pointed out than we can have by acting 
in common with others, and as our influence will always be beneficially 
‘afluenced towards Turkey, 80 the greater that ‘nfluence is the more Turkey 
will benefit by it. It is true that, on the other side, we thus avoul direct 
antagonism, and ean neutralize many hostile measures but as the condition 
of ‘Turkey is one in which to be stationary is to avait destruction, and 
that action 15 inipereuivey wanted, all that prevents action or thwarts 
good action tends necessarily to a decline, more or less eradual, and 
therefore only differs from the effects of violent hostility in the time and 
manner by whieh the same reault is brought about. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) LIENRY L. BULWER.

Inclogure 1 in No. 72. 
Sir H. Bulwer to Aah Pasha. 


Constantinople, January 29, 1861. 


THE Undersigned, Her Britannic Majesty’s Ambassador Eixtraor- 
dinary and Minister Plenipotentiary to the Sublime Porte, has the honour 
to state to his Highness Aali Pasha, Acting Minister for Foreign Affairs, 
that he has been directed by his Government to eall the serious attention 
of the Porte to the present state of the Turkish finances. ‘The means 
whieh the loan recently contracted in Europe has placed at the disposal 
of the Porte for extricating itself from the most urgent claims upon the 
Turkish Treasury, present a favourable opportunity lor taking measures 
of a decisive nature to place its finances on a secure and satislactory 
basis: while the report lately presented by the British and Brench 
members of the Mixed Commission is extremely valuable, as containing 
suggestions made, alter mature consideration, by persons well qualified to 
form opinions on the subject. | 

[t is almost unnecessary for the Undersigned to point out to his 
Highness Aali Pasha, 1 the first place, that the establishment of a Mixed 
Commission of some kind 1s essential for any reorganization of the Turkish 
finances; and the same reason which would thus call such a Commission 
‘nto action would equally point out the necessity of the European element 
of that Commission being so constituted that the voice of the Europeans 
should not be overpowered by the adverse opinions of the Turkish 
members; in other words, the number of the Turkish and European 
members of such Mixed Commission should be equal. 

In the next place it is of great importance that the duties of the 
Commission should be clearly detined, and this should be done, not only 
with regard to the duties which the Commission might be ealled upon ta 
serform, but also with regard to the powers which it should be abso- 
lately required to exercise ; and moreover, a person should be selected 
for the office of President who could devote his time exclusively to the 
subjects with which the Commission would be engaged. Distinct orders 
should also be given that the different public offices should furnish 
punetually all the decuments which the Commission may require. In no 
other manner could it exercise its functions with advantage, for unless the 
measures which the Commissioners might recommend were founded on 
aoe data, they might be prejudicial instead of beneficial to the 
state. 

The Undersigned is further directed by his Government to inclose 
to his Highness Aali Pasha an abstract of the reforms recommended in 
the Report of the British and French Commissioner, and in doing so begs 
to call the attention of the Porte to these recommendations, and most 
earnestly to urge thei adoption, 

Mhe Undersigned has further the honour to state to Aali Pasha with 
regard to the present Budget, that the principal facts connected with 1 do 
not appear to be stated with sufficient clearness, and the manner in which 
the proposed reductions of 1,500,000 piastres in the expenditure are to be 
effected is not stated at all, No allowance has been made for claims not 
yet decided upon, which will amount to a considerable sum, nor is it clear 
whether the arrears of pay due to the army are included in the statement 
of the expenditure to be made. 

The Budget is, moreover, altogether a fallacious one as far as the

It appears, therefore— 

l. That the sum now borrowed is insufficient to meet the urgent 
demands of the Porte. 

2 ‘That the Porte has added nearly 1,500,000/, ta the expenditure 
side of the account, on a balance-sheet which only brings out a tolerable 
equality between receipt nd expenditure by the statement of 1,500,000. 
of unspecified reductions ; and 
3. That notwithstanding this very unsatisfactory state of things, 
nothing would be so easy as for the Turkish Government to restore its 
finances by skilful and prudent management. 

The observations which M. de Pleue makes with regard to the 
fnances of Greece, and the inferences which he draws from his experience 
in that country, confirm this opinion, But without such good and 
improved arrangements this new loan will only be a further step towards 
that bankruptcy with which the Turkish State is threatened, and it is 
therefore to be feared that, at the end of another year, the Porte may he, 
not merely in the same pecuniary embarrassment as at present, but will 
have the farther difficulty of having to furnish the amount required for 
the interest of this new loan. 

The Government of Her Britannic Majesty, in directing the Under- 
signed to press strongly on the notice of the Sublime Porte the absolute 
necessity of introducing better mana ement into its financial system, 18 
actuated solely by the desire which it has so constantly evinced to promote 
the welfare and prosperity of the Empire of the Sultan, Her Majesty’s 
Government feel it to be their duty to warn the Porte atrongly of the 
disastrous consequences which must inevitably follow a perseverance in 
the improvident system of financial administration whieh has led the 
Turkish Government into its present difficulties. ‘The Undersigned ts 
therefore directed to state that no amount of loan would be of any perma- 
nent benefit to the Porte, unless a system of economy and of regularity is 
introduced into the various Departments of the State: that experience of 
the past has proved to demonstration that such improvements will not, 
and eannot, be introduced by those Turkish Agents who have been the 
cause of the present deplorable financial condition of the Porte; and that, 
‘f the Sultan wishes to save his Throne and his Empire from the catas- 
trophe towards which they are rapidly approaching, he will authorize a 
Commission of experienced and trustworthy Europeans to devise and 
earry into immediate execution those improvements and reforms which 
may seem to them to be necessary. 

The Undersigned ts accordingly empowered by ‘his Government to 
state formally to Aali Pasha that, if His Imperial Majesty the Sultan is 
willing to agree to such arrangement, Her Majesty’s Government will 
co-operate by selecting a competent person to be a member of such a 


Commission. 
The Undersigned, &c. (Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

Abstract of Report of the English and French Commissioners with respect to the 
State of Turkish Finances, and Measures and Reforms recommended by 
them. 


THE duties of the Commission were to examine into the financial 
condition of the Empire; to remodel the laws in the matter of taxes and 
financial udministration; and to restore order and regularity in the 
Government. With these ends in view, they proposed— 

To form Budgets ; 

To regulate the employment of them ; 

l'o insist upon accounts being given up; 

And strongly urged that publicity should be given to the acts of the 
Administration,

The ‘Tarkish Members of the Commission strongly opposed nearly all 


the measures brought forward by the other members, and it was very 
evident that the Porte intended to

The English and French Commissioners urged that the General

hee ‘ ee that regulative power im the employment of the public 
money. was absolutely indispensable. 


With regard to the Customs, the practice of keeping up running debts 


with merchants was denounced, and it was recommended that the practice 
should be forthwith abolished. 


[t appears from this Report that the Army and Navy remain unpaid, 


and every day the debt and obligations of the Porte are increasing. an 
that Nel eats of the following year are Ina great measure, if not 
wholly, swallowed up- 


It is evident, however, that the natural wealth and resources of Turkey 


are not turned to the best advantage, ‘The forests bring in but 150,000 


francs, whereas in Greece the forests produce 270,000 francs. 
The mines are badly worked, or not worked at all, and bring in but 
little to the revenue. , 

The duty on beasts, euch as sheep, goats, &c., produces 20,000,000 
francs, whereas horses, camels, donkeys, %c., are not taxed at all; and 
nothing can justify this exception. ; 

Stamped spe and patents have little or no duty on them, 

Monopolies have been abolished, and an end put to many and gross 
abuses arising from them, but exceptions should be made for salt and 
rabacco, which in able hands should produce @ very considerable income. 

it would be easy for Turkey, by thus increasing her revenue, to give 
security for a loan to carry on public business. 

The town of Constantinople, which enjoys at the present time the 
privilege of exemption from income tax, should be made to pay it, a5 other 
towns in the ‘Turkish dominions, Me 

One of the chief losses of the ‘Treasury arises from the faculty 
possessed by certain Ministers. of issuing “serghis —that is, promises 0 
payment—without limit. A Government which cannot command eredit 
must provide reserves of metal, or must reduce its €x nditure imme- 
diately after a crisis. The Finance Minister alone should have the power 
to issue “serghis,” and only then on proof that the expenses have been 
‘neurred by the other Ministers, and the amount of “serghis” to he issued 
should be fixed. hase | (wt 

The French and English Commissioner protested strongly against 
the further issue of paper money, which they said would be the ruin of the

Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell —(Recetved February 16.) 

(No. 96.) | 
My Lord, Constantinople, February 6, 1861. 

SOME little time since M. de Labanow requested Aalt Pasha to 
convene the Representatives and consult with them as to the reforms 
which the ‘Turkish Empire required, laying before them at the same time 
those the Porte had prepared. Aali Pasha declining this pro osal, Prince 
Labanow made a suggestion to me which I thought practicable, and might 
srove advantageous. a 
It was thie that the Porte should before publishing the reforms it 
has now in hand, communicate them to the Representatives ; 80 that if they 
had any remarks to make upon them, they might do so previous to their 
becoming law. | | 

This plan I had already myself spoken of, and 1 have advocated it 
with Aalit Pasha; but the real difficulty arises from the prejudic 
manner in which the Government of Russia seems determined to view and 


136 


represent every effort of the Turks towards improvement. It is not only 
that the memoir I have just been reading as to the Grand Vizier's tour 1s 
unfair, but the very tone, spirit, and ingenuity it displays, makes one feel 
that it is the production of an enemy whom nothing can coneiliate, anc 
this not unnaturally indisposes the Porte to take such an enemy into 
its councils. 

It may indeed be anticipated as possible that the Russian Envoy's 
design is to discredit before ‘and the reforms of the Porte, and not to aid 
in making them palateable, 

Tam rather, however, for not taking this into account, more especially 
as the Russian Government may be merely desirous to have it appear 
that no reforms are granted without her interference, and also beeause if 
the reforms proposed are really at all adequate to the occasion the support 
of some at least of the other Re resentatives would counterbalance the 
unjust disapprobation of that of Russia. 

What, however, I think probable is, that the Porte, instead of giving 
us acopy of the projects of law to which Trefer, will write us a memorandum 
upon thas which is a very different thing. 

Aali Pasha, however, will, { think, asfaras Tam personnally concerned, 
allow me to compare that memorandum with the documents themselves 
on whieh it is based; and in this manner I shall be able better to judge of 
the effect which such documents would produce on my colleagues, and 
advise oract accordingly. 


I have, &e. 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

My Lord, Constantinople, February 18, 1861. 


| HAVE been informed by the Grand Vizier that the projected 
reorganization of the general system of police throughout the Ottoman 
Empire was submitted to the Sultan a short time back, and, having 
received the assent of His Imperial Majesty, orders. were given to the 
Seraskier Pasha to carry the same into immediate execution. 

Phe reforms to be effected in the mode of levying the tithes have 
likewise been agreed upon in the Council of Ministers, and were laid before 
the Sultan for his sanction on the 7th ‘natant, Having applied to Aali 
Pasha to know whether it was the intention of the Porte to communicate 
any detailed account of these reforms to the Re resentatives of the Great. 
Powers before they were promulgated, his Highness stated to me that as 
the two measures concerning the tithes and police related exclusively to 
the interior government of the Em ire, he was indisposed to make any 
preliminary communication as to those measures to the Reprennienie 
of foreign Powers, though. simultancously with their promu ation they 
would undoubtedly be submitted to the Representatives ol the Great 
Powers for their information. 

He added, however, that with respect to the reform that was likewise 
to be made in the tribunals, for admitting Christian evidence, he should 
not feel the same objection to communicate the project relative to this 
subject to the said epresentatives previous to 1ts cing made public, 
since foreign Governments were directly or indirectly more immediately 
concerned in this matter,

NOUS avons sous les yeux un Mémoire volumineux ¢émané réecem-

Inclosure in No. 80. 


Sir H. Bulwer to Aali Pasha. 


Constantinople, le 11 Mars, 1861. 

Le Soussigné a remis une note sur état financier de la Turquie a son 
Altesse Aali Pacha, datée du 20 Janvier dernier. Cette note est restée 
jusquiici sans réponse. Or, état financier du Gouvernement Ottoman 
devient chaque jour plus critique, et la note en question, écrite depres les 
ordres du Gouvernement de Sa Majest¢, indique les moyens par esquels 
le erédit Ottoman pourrait renaitre, 

En outre, il faut se rappeler que les avis ainsi émis viennent du 
Représentant du pays od seul peut-ttre, dans un cas extreme, quelques 
sécours pécuniaires pourront se trouver. 

Le Soussigné demande respectueusement si 500 Altesse Aali Pacha et 
la Sublime Porte ne prennent pas une sérieuse responsabilité en négligeant 
ct méme en laissant sans réponse, dang les circonstances pareilles, des 
conseils qui peuvent étre d'une si haute importance. 

in adressant cette question a son Altesse Aali Pacha, le Soussigneé ne 
yeut en rien mettre en doute ni le patriotisme, 1 \intelligence, ni l'activite 
de son Altesse le Ministre des Affaires Etrangéres de la Sublime Porte. 
Bien au contraire, le Soussigne a la plus haute estime pour les qualités 
personnelles et ‘ntellectuelles et éminentes en toul genre de l'homme d'état, 
i qui il s'adresse dans ce moment, et il connatt aussi les nombreuses. et 
eraves occupations de son Altesse. Mais de toutes les questions qui 
sagitent dans ce moment, la plus grave est justement la question des 
finances. 

A titre done d'un ami dont le soin constant a été la prospérite de 
Empire Ottoman, le Soussigné appelle encore une fois Pattention de son 
Altesse sur cette question, sollicitant la réponse qu il attend depuis si long- 
temps, et prenant sur iui de conseiller 4 la Sublime Porte de se mettre 
d'aceord avec les opinions du Gouvernement de Sa Ly sur un point 
ol ces opinions, ainsi que celles des capitalistes et du public en Angleterre, 
pourraient avoir une si grande valeur pour le bien-¢tre de la Turquie. 

(Signé) HENRY L, BULWER,

No, 83. 


Lord J. Russell to Lord Hobart and Mr. Foster. 
(No. 1.) 
My Lord, 
SI, Foreign Office, April 22, 1861. 

1 AM glad to avail myself of your services at Constantinople for 
inquiring into the condition of the Turkish finances, with a view to the 
improvement of the system now existing in regard to the collection, 
appropriation, and account of the revenues of the Porte. You will 
understand that in the performance of the task assigned to you, you are 
to act entirely under the direction of Her Majesty’s Ambassador, but 1 
should wish you to report to me from time to time the progress that you 
may be making, and your views as to the course which may be likely to 
ensure permanent benefit to the Porte as the result of your mission. 

The resources of Turkey are abundant if properly turned to account, 
and it is probable that even under the present system the money levied on 
the population would, ‘Cit were all brought to account, place the Treasury 
‘1 a much better position than that in w tich it now stands, But between 
the tenant or tax-payer and the Treasury, numerous parties intervene 
who, though it may be in different degrees, all combine to intercept for 
their own benefit a portion of the revenue which should be paid to the 
State; while large sums are wrongfully exacted under one denomination 
or the other, no portion of which is ever brought to account. 

[t seems to me that the first point to belcuked to is, the sum that the 
several districts are legally bound to pay, and the security which can be 
devised for the entrance of that sum into the ‘Treasury, and, as regards 
she tax-payer, for his immunity from further exaction not sanctioned 
ny law. 

The next point, with a view to ‘nerease the revenue of the State, 
would be to fix what proportion of this money DON illegally exacted by 
local tax-gatherers might, without pressing injuriously on the population, 
be fairly added to the taxes which are now legally levied; and it is pro- 
hable that a very considerable addition might be made to the public revenue 
not only without any imerease of the burthens imposed on the population by 
the exactions of the tax-gatherer, who levies additional sone pulionE for his 
own personal enrichment, but with a positive diminution of these 
burthens. 

The next point would be, to provide for the issue of money from the 
Public Treasury under due control ‘and responsibility ; while the last would 
be the establishment of a proper system of audit, and the regularity and 
sublicity of the accounts of revenue and expenditure, With respect to 
the head of expenditure, if ts essential to ascertain the exact sums issued 
to each Minister having the control of any branch of the public service, ancl 
the mode in which those sums have been appropriated to Civil, Military, 
and Naval services. These accounts should be given in detail, 

1 feel persuaded that the knowledge which you possess in regard to 
matters coming within these several categories will enable you to offer 
very valuable suggestions to the Murkish Government, and I need not say 
that if the result of your labours should be to place the Turkish finances 
on a sound footing, you will have rendered a very essential service, not 
only to the Parte, but to the general interests of all Powers having rela- 
tions, whether political or commercial, with Turkey. 

The changes which are now in progress in regard to the duties on 
exports and imports will probably enable you to sug est many improve- 
ments in the ‘Turkish Custom-houses, for the benefit o trade. 

When you have agreed to a report upon any one branch of the various 
matters which will form the subject of your inquiry, you will submit the 
regult to Sir H. Bulwer, and you will, after consultation on the different 
points involved in such report, leave tt to him to convey the substance of 
such report to the Sultan in the manner which he shall deem most 
conducive to the end in view, and most in conformity with that friendly 

ae

interest in the prosperity of the Turkish Empire whieh has induced Her 
Majesty to sanction your mission, | 
Tam, &c. 
(Signed) J. RUSSELL.

Sir H. Bulwer to Lord J. Russell.— (Received April 27.) 
(No, 277.) . 
My Lord, Constantinople, April 16, 1861. 

| INCLOSE to your Lordship a copy of the financial project which 
the Porte has at last brought out. 

1 need hardly say that it is very contrary to what I should have 
advised, but it is so thoroughly impracticable that 1 can never be carried 
‘nto execution, and T look upon it merely as an excuse for sanctioning the 
monthly emission of 30,000,000 piastres in caimés, which the Porte 
absolutely requires for its urgent wants. In the meantime the scheme 15 
to undergo the revision of the European Mixed Commission, and conse- 
quently will, L should trust, die still-born in its hands. 

There are various means by which the Parte might provide a remedy 
for its difficulties, and some of these will no doubt, in the end, be adopted ; 
but the total iznorance of the Government itself in matters of finance, and 
the readiness with which it listens to speculators and bankers 10 
Galata, who represent all sound views as impracticable, lead the Porte 
astray. 

Phe errors which it is making are so Bross and palpable that they 
cannot be persevering. 

The publication of the Budget, the control of the expenditure, and a 
new organization of the Iinancial Department, must preeede any solid 
measure. 

With these steps taken the establishment of an equality between the 
revenue and expenditure is easy, and the local debts might then be without 
much difficulty provided for. tae 

[ shall see with pleasure the two gentlemen whom Her Majesty's 
Government propose to send out, | 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) HENRY L. BULWER.

de ce Ministére, ainsi qu'on le verra par la publication des bude, Mais

Lord J. Russell to Sir H. Bulwer, 
(No. 272.) ‘ 
Sir, Foreign Office, April 29, 18G1. 
| HAVE received your Excellency’s despatch No, 277 of the 16th 
instant, inclosing a copy of the new financial scheme of the Porte, which 
is to be referred to the revision of the Mixed European Commission, and | 
concur in your opinion with regard to it; but as our Agents specially 
appointed to inquire into the Lurkish finances are on their way to 
Constantinople, it is not necessary for Her Majesty’s Government to 
examine this plan. 
Tam, &c. : 
(Signed) J. RUSSELL.

No. 87, 


Lord J. Russell to Sir H. Bulwer. 
(No. 437,) 
Sir, Foreign Office, July 4, 1861. 

THE accession of a new Sultan appears to Her Majesty’s Government 
to furnish a fit oceasion for the Turkish Government to make a vigorous 
effort to relieve the Empire from that financial embarrassment to which 
all the enemies of the Porte have pointed as the sure and proximate cause 
of the fall of the Ottoman power. 

Yet there are ample means in Turkey for falsifying this prediction. 
The Empire has provinces of great fertility, and is inhabite by races 
some of which have industry and a genius for trade, while others furnish 
hardy and obedient soldiers, the fit materials for a strong and faithful 
army. 

anise: order, energy, and impartial justice, would soon redress the 
most flagrant evils: economy would in a few months furnish a surplus 
beyond the ordinary expenditure. | 

" Upon these means Her Majesty's Government are conyinced Sultan 
Aziz, ought to rely. Her Majesty’s Government would consider they were 
faithless friends to the Porte if they held out any prospect of the guarantee 
of a loan, It would be pouring water into.a cask with a hole in its 
bottom. But when order and economy with publicity of accounts shall 
have restored confidence to capitalists, the Sultan will find that the credit 
of bis Government will stand as high as that of any State on the Continent 
of Europe. 

herd Hobart and Mr. Foster will, I trust, before long be able to 
furnish you with an authentic report on the state of the Turkish finances. 
With that report in your hands your Excellency will be able to give 
proofs to the Porte of that sincere friendship which animates Her Majesty's 
Government, and which is as far apart from flattery as it is from hostility 
or indifference. It is the anxious desire of Her Majesty’s Government to 
see the Ottoman race combine with its ancient virtues of courage and 
fidelity, the improvements and the acts which modern civilization has 
ard over Europe. 


Lam, &c. 
(Signed) J. RUSSELL.
